# Writing Science

## Citation

Schimel, J. (2012). *Writing science: How to write papers that get cited and proposals that get funded*. Oxford University Press. https://global.oup.com/academic/product/writing-science-9780199760237

## Abstract

Establishes a rule-based method for structuring scientific papers and proposals around story rather than chronology of methods. Covers the whole arc of a manuscript: story selection and stickiness (the SUCCES framework), the OCAR macrostructure (Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution) and its LDR proposal variant (Lead, Development, Resolution), paragraph- and sentence-level flow (topic-stress linkage, the 2-3-1 rule of emphasis), word choice and verb energy, and condensing for page limits. Method is drawn from the author's own manuscript-editing practice and worked revisions of real (anonymized) sentences, paragraphs, and abstracts, moving from whole-paper architecture down to individual word choices. Closes with chapters on writing across language and disciplinary limitations and on communicating to public and policy audiences.

## Architecture

Twenty-one chapters and two appendices, in four movements that run from the largest scale to the smallest. Chapter 1 states the sequence: story and story structure first, then paragraphs and sentences, then word choice, then the situations that complicate all three.

**Chapters 1–3 — why story.** The premise (a scientist is a professional writer, judged by citation rather than publication count), the claim that a paper is a story rather than a report of results, and the imported framework for what makes a story memorable, the Heath brothers' SUCCES.

**Chapters 4–9 — the shape of a paper.** Chapter 4 names the four elements — Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution — and the structures built from them: OCAR, LD, LDR, ABDCE, the hourglass, the spiral, IMRaD. Chapters 5 through 9 then take one element per chapter, in order, with chapter 6 covering the funnel that carries the reader from opening to challenge. Chapter 4 is the book's hinge; every later chapter is written in the vocabulary it establishes.

**Chapters 10–17 — the same shape, smaller.** Chapter 10 makes the recursive move explicit: sections, paragraphs, and sentences each carry their own complete arc. Chapters 11, 12 and 13 apply it to paragraphs, sentences, and the links between them. Chapters 14 and 15 drop to verbs and words. Chapter 16 covers condensing to fit hard page limits, and chapter 17 runs a full multi-pass revision on a single manuscript, which is where the preceding thirteen chapters get exercised together.

**Chapters 18–21 — complications and close.** Imperfect data, writing science in a second language, writing for public and policy audiences, and a closing chapter that reopens on chapter 1's first sentence.

The load-bearing chapters are 4, 12, and 17. Chapter 4 supplies the structural vocabulary; chapter 12 supplies the topic/stress mechanics that chapters 13 through 16 all depend on; chapter 17 is the only place the whole method operates at once on one piece of text. Chapters 5 and 9 carry more weight than their neighbours because they cover the two power positions, the places a reader's attention actually lands.

Most chapters end with exercises. Appendix A holds the author's own answers to the revision exercises, which makes the book usable as a course rather than only as a reference; the answers are cross-referenced into the chapters that set them in the chapter treatments below. Appendix B is an annotated list of other writing resources.

## Chapters

### Chapter 1 — Writing in Science

**Claim.** A scientist is a professional writer, not merely a scientist who occasionally writes. Success in science is measured by influence — by citation, not by publication count — and influence is a function of communication as much as of the underlying ideas or data. Writing quality is therefore not cosmetic; it is a rate-limiting step on the value of all other scientific work.

**Mechanism.** Schimel builds the chapter's argument in stages rather than giving a single procedure:

- He distinguishes surviving from succeeding: "publish or perish" describes survival, but "You don't succeed as a scientist by getting papers published. You succeed as a scientist by getting them cited" (p. 3).
- He invokes the H-factor as the operative measure of success and works a numeric illustration: "If you have 10 publications that have each been cited 10 times, you have an H of 10; if you have 30 papers that have each been cited 30 times, you have an H of 30; but if you have published 100 papers and none have been cited, on the H-factor you would rate a flat zero" (p. 3).
- He argues that writing quality and clarity of thought are not simply cause and effect running one direction — clear writing can produce clear thinking, not only follow from it — and treats this as a corrective to a common false belief.
- He reframes writing as a professional tool coequal with the technical tools of science (molecular biology, chemical analysis, statistics), and observes that most scientists treat writing as something done post hoc, after the data are in, rather than as a skill developed deliberately.
- He states what he calls the overriding principle of the book and repeats it for emphasis: "It is the author's job to make the reader's job easy" (p. 5).
- He distinguishes his book's approach from other science-writing books: those books, in his account, treat writing as something a scientist *does* (mechanics — figures, bibliographies, submission and review process); his book treats being a writer as something a scientist *is*, addressing craft — story, structure, paragraphs, sentences, word choice — rather than manuscript mechanics.
- He states the book's own organization: it proceeds from large scale to small — story and story structure first, then paragraphs and sentences, then word choice, then a final section on challenges specific to particular types of science writing.

Section 1.1, "Writing versus Rewriting," adds a second, distinct claim: the book is fundamentally about rewriting, not writing. Schimel states that he commits, in his own first drafts, every error he warns readers against, and that first drafts do not matter because no one else sees them. He warns that attending too closely to the book's principles while drafting can be paralyzing: "if you think about these principles as you draft, you may never draft anything" (p. 6). The recommended practice is to draft fast, without self-censoring, in order to have material to revise, and to let understanding and expression improve each other in successive passes — clearer understanding produces clearer expression, which in turn produces clearer understanding, in an iterative cycle that continues "until they run out of energy, interest, or time" (quoting Joseph Williams, p. 7).

**Evidence and examples.** The chapter argues by citing other writers on writing rather than by working through a before/after revision example. Three block quotations carry the chapter's case for writing and rewriting as generative, not merely transcriptive, processes:

> No matter how rational your thought may be (or appear to be) on a particular problem, no matter how detailed your intentions and plottings, the act of writing will almost always prove rebellious, full of unforeseen difficulties, sidetracks, blind alleys, revelations. Good, clear writing — writing that teaches and informs without confusion — emerges from a process of struggle, or if you prefer, litigation. Most often, the terms of the formula given above need to be reversed: "clear thinking can emerge from clear writing." Imposing order by organizing and expressing ideas has great power to clarify. In many cases, writing is the process through which scientists come to understand the real form and implications of their work.
>
> — Scott Montgomery, *The Chicago Guide to Communicating Science* (p. 4)

> I wish I had a secret I could let you in on, some formula my father passed on to me in a whisper just before he died, some code word that has enabled me to sit at my desk and land flights of creative inspiration like an air-traffic controller. But I don't. All I know is that the process is pretty much the same for almost everyone I know. The good news is that some days it feels like you just have to keep getting out of your own way so that whatever it is that wants to be written can use you to write it. But the bad news is that if you're at all like me, you'll probably read over what you've written and spend the rest of the day obsessing, and praying that you do not die before you can completely rewrite or destroy what you have written, lest the eagerly waiting world learn how bad your first drafts are.
>
> — Anne Lamott, *Bird by Bird* (p. 5)

> They rewrite an earlier draft into something clearer, they more clearly understand their ideas. And when they understand their ideas better; they express them more clearly, and when they express them more clearly, they understand them even better . . . and so it goes, until they run out of energy, interest, or time.
>
> — Joseph Williams, *Style: Ten Lessons in Clarity and Grace* (p. 7)

A fourth quotation, from William Zinsser, is given as a short attributed line rather than a block passage: "Rewriting is the essence of writing. I pointed out the professional writers rewrite their sentences over and over and then rewrite what they have rewritten" (William Zinsser, *On Writing Well*, p. 7).

A fifth quotation, again from Anne Lamott, is offered as what Schimel calls "the last word on rewriting":

> Shitty First Drafts. All good writers write them. That is how they end up with good second drafts and terrific third drafts. I know some very great writers, writers you love who write beautifully and have made a great deal of money, and not one of them sits down routinely feeling wildly enthusiastic and confident. Not one of them writes elegant first drafts. All right, one of them does, but we do not like her very much.
>
> — Anne Lamott, *Bird by Bird* (p. 7)

Schimel follows this with his own observation that the quote "highlights just how wonderful a writer Lamott is — her third drafts are terrific" (p. 7), and describes his own practice of accumulating 10 to 20 drafts of a paper before finishing, and rereading earlier work as often painful because "imperfections glow like neon signs" (p. 7).

Each of these quotations functions as testimony rather than as an analyzed case: Schimel does not revise a sentence in front of the reader in this chapter. Instead, he assembles authorities — a science-communication scholar, two writing-craft authors, and a working novelist-turned-writing-teacher — to establish that struggle and iteration are normal even for accomplished writers, before stating his own book's premise that the text to follow will teach rewriting rather than one-pass composition.

No numbered lists, figures, or tables appear in this chapter. No exercises are given in this chapter (pages 3–7); none appear before the chapter break at printed page 8.

**Terms introduced.**

- **H-factor (H-index).** A citation-based measure Schimel uses to define scientific success in this chapter: a researcher with 10 publications each cited 10 times has an H of 10; a scientist with 100 uncited papers has an H of zero regardless of publication count (p. 3). Introduced here as the justification for prioritizing citation and influence over publication volume; not otherwise defined mechanically beyond the illustrative example.
- **Writing versus rewriting.** The distinction that gives section 1.1 its title: writing is the act of producing a first draft; rewriting is the iterative revision process Schimel identifies as "the essence of writing" (quoting Zinsser, p. 7). The chapter frames the entire book as being about the second of these, not the first.

**Connections.** This chapter is the book's opening statement of purpose and sets up the book's overall structure, which it states explicitly: story and story structure first, then paragraphs and sentences, then word choice, then a final section on specific science-writing challenges — the sequence the following chapters are organized to follow. Chapter 2, "Science Writing as Storytelling," begins immediately after this chapter (printed page 8) and opens with an epigraph from Raymond Chandler, taking up the story-based approach this chapter identifies as the book's organizing perspective.


### Chapter 2 — Science Writing as Storytelling

**Claim.** A scientific paper is a story, not a presentation of results: it is built from data, but the data are not themselves the story. Papers and proposals that get cited and funded are the ones that tell the most compelling stories, and scientists can deliberately become better storytellers without sacrificing objectivity or professionalism.

**Mechanism.** The chapter opens by addressing journalist Elizabeth Kolbert's remark that scientists don't tell stories. Schimel argues this is wrong but explains why the impression arises, offering three reasons (p. 8–9):

1. Scientists tell stories using a formalized structure that doesn't match the structure journalists use, so the stories get lost in cross-cultural communication.
2. Many scientists are poor storytellers — they either don't see the story clearly or can't tell it clearly.
3. Many scientists are uncomfortable framing their work as "telling stories," associating "story" with fiction and worrying that claiming to write a story means abandoning objectivity.

Schimel argues storytelling is compatible with objectivity: "To tell a good story in science, you must assess your data and evaluate the possible explanations — which are most consistent with existing knowledge and theory? The story grows organically from the data and is objective, dispassionate, and fully professional" (p. 9). The failure mode is authors who decide on the story before collecting data and then force the data to fit it. Schimel brings in Anne Lamott's *Bird by Bird* to make this point, applying advice she gave about fiction to science:

> Characters should not, conversely, serve as pawns for some plot you've dreamed up. Any plot you impose on your characters will be onomatopoetic: PLOT. I say don't worry about plot. Worry about the characters. Let what they say or do reveal who they are, and be involved in their lives, and keep asking yourself, "Now what happens?"
>
> — Anne Lamott, *Bird by Bird*, quoted p. 9

Schimel maps this onto science: the "characters" are not people but molecules, organisms, ecosystems, or concepts — nitrogen cycling in arctic tundra, benzene and its reactions, genes and their functions. The scientist's job is to "listen to" these characters by analyzing data with an open mind, then develop them as characters in the paper (p. 9).

The chapter frames science communication as a social imperative: as science has moved from "esoteric, ivory-tower natural philosophy" to something that directly affects public life, the inability of scientists to communicate has become a crisis affecting how science is used (or misused) in policy. Schimel's diagnosis: talking to policymakers in dense "science" is like speaking louder in English to people who don't speak it — it doesn't work and it reads as arrogance. His prescription is not that scientists must become popularizers like Stephen Jay Gould or Carl Sagan, only that they become better storytellers (p. 10).

**Section 2.1, "Finding the Story"** (p. 10), sets out the procedure for locating the story inside a data set. The core distinction: the ultimate goal of research is not the raw data (from the mass spectrometer, the DNA sequencer, the telescope) but the understanding derived from them. Schimel formalizes this as a progression — data become information, information becomes knowledge, knowledge becomes understanding (Figure 2.2) — and argues that a paper's power depends on how far along this path it takes the reader: "The further along the path from data to understanding you can take your work and your papers, the more people will be able to assimilate your contributions and use them to motivate their own work and ideas — and that should be your goal" (p. 11). He restates the chapter's central formula here: "The data are supporting actors in the story you tell. The lead actors are the questions and the larger issues you are addressing. The story grows from the data, but the data are not the story" (p. 11).

#### Figure 2.2 (p. 11)

**Caption.** Figure 2.2. The flow of science, from data to understanding.

**What it shows.** A single horizontal chain of four rectangular boxes, all the same height, arranged left to right and connected by three arrows. Each arrow is a short horizontal line with a solid arrowhead pointing to the right, running from the right edge of one box to the left edge of the next.

The box texts, in order left to right:

1. Data
2. Information
3. Knowledge
4. Understanding

There are no other labels, no numbers, no branches, and no return arrows; the flow is strictly one-directional from Data to Understanding.

**What it demonstrates.** Getting from raw data to understanding is a sequence of distinct transformations rather than a single step: data become information, information becomes knowledge, and knowledge is synthesized into understanding. The further right along the chain a piece of work is carried, the more finished the scientific product.

From this Schimel derives a two-stage process for developing a story from data, which he calls elaborate-and-slash: "develop your story from the bottom up, then tell it from the top down. Start with the data, think about them, listen for the story they are trying to tell, and find that story" (p. 12). This means listening not only to the data's "loud proclamations" but its "quiet, uncertain mutterings" — a shoulder on a spectrum, a nonsignificant treatment effect, an outlier — and deliberately overinterpreting the data at first, exploring what they might mean at their farthest fringes, before applying Occam's razor to cut back to the simple core story (p. 12). Schimel's rationale for not simply starting with the simplest story: doing so risks imposing a plot on the characters rather than letting the data reveal one, repeating the trap Lamott warns against. Only by exploring the boundaries and limits of the data can the important story be found (p. 12).

**Evidence and examples.**

*The DNA discovery example* (p. 10–11). Schimel contrasts Rosalind Franklin's Photo 51 — the X-ray diffraction image of DNA — with the sketch of molecular structure that James Watson and Francis Crick built from it (Figure 2.1). He argues it was not the raw data (Photo 51) that became famous but the interpreted model: "Photo 51 means nothing to me beyond its role as a historical artifact. The double helix model of DNA, however, I understand. It is interpretable by many and is at the core of the work of thousands of scientists spanning from medicine to soil microbiology" (p. 10). He notes Franklin's lack of credit for her role has been controversial but treats that as a separate issue from his point about the relative value of data versus interpretation (p. 10). Applied to the data-information-knowledge-understanding progression: Photo 51 was data; Franklin's analysis of it produced information (the atomic structure of crystallized DNA); Watson and Crick's model produced knowledge (the double helix structure); explaining how that structure enables cell replication and heredity is the final step, understanding (p. 11).

#### Figure 2.1 (p. 11)

**Caption.** Figure 2.1. Photo 51, Rosalind Franklin's critical X-ray diffraction image of crystallized DNA and the simple model of its structure developed by James Watson and Francis Crick.
Both images © 1953, Nature Publishing Group, reprinted with permission.

**What it shows.** Two panels side by side, each with a label set above it.

Left panel, labeled "A. Photo 51": a square black-and-white photographic plate — an X-ray diffraction exposure. The field is a dark, roughly circular halo filling most of the square against a lighter square background. At the center of the halo is a small bright rectangular blank (the beam stop), flanked left and right by short dark bars. Radiating out from the center, dark diffraction spots are arranged in two arms that cross in an X pattern — a row of blurred spots running up-left and up-right of center, and a matching row running down-left and down-right, with the innermost spots largest and the outer ones fainter. Above and below the center the pattern is empty. There is no scale bar, no axis, and no annotation on the plate.

Right panel, labeled "B. Model of DNA": a line drawing of the DNA double helix. A single straight vertical line runs the full height of the panel as the helix axis. Two continuous ribbon-like strands wind around that axis in a regular helical coil, crossing over each other at intervals; the drawing shows roughly three to four full turns from top to bottom. Between the two strands, short parallel horizontal rungs are drawn as ladder-like cross-hatching filling the space between the coils in each turn. Two small arrowheads sit near the middle of the drawing, one pointing up-right on the left side of the axis and one pointing down-right on the right side, indicating the opposite senses of the two chains. No bases, atoms, dimensions, or letter labels are marked.

**What it demonstrates.** The figure sets the raw observation beside the interpretation: an experimental X-ray image on the left, and on the right the structural model that image was used to derive. The two panels are the same subject at two different stages of scientific processing — the data, and the story built from the data.

*Bill Dietrich's graduate research on hillslope soil depth* (p. 12–13). Dietrich, later a geomorphology professor at UC Berkeley and a National Academy of Sciences member, studied how hillslope steepness controls soil depth in the Pacific Northwest for his doctorate. Most of his data fit a tight relationship between slope angle and soil thickness, which by itself made "a perfectly good story" (Figure 2.3). But there were outliers — soils far deeper than the relationship predicted. Rather than ignoring them, Dietrich investigated and found that hillslope bedrock is uneven, forming hidden "wedges" that fill with debris and soil over time; once filled they are invisible on the landscape but can flush out in heavy rainstorms and create lethal mudflows. Evaluating the processes that fill and flush these wedges became a focus of Dietrich's early career. Schimel's point: "Because he listened to his characters carefully, recognized that the most important story wasn't in the average but in the outliers, and then explored those outliers, he came up with more novel, exciting, and important science" (p. 12).

#### Figure 2.3 (p. 13)

**Caption.** Figure 2.3. The top figure illustrates the relationship between hill steepness and soil depth in the U.S. Pacific Northwest; the bottom figure illustrates a cross-section through a wedge. Redrawn from Dietrich and Dunne (1978).
Copyright © 1978, E. Schweizerbart Science Publishers. www.borntraeger-cramer.de. Reprinted with permission.

**What it shows.** Two stacked panels.

Top panel — a scatter plot in a full rectangular frame.

- Y axis, labeled "Vertical soil thickness (Meters)" on two lines, with labeled ticks at 0, 1.0, 2.0, 3.0, and 4.0. Minor ticks appear on the frame between the labeled values.
- X axis, labeled "Hillslope angle (Degrees)" on two lines beneath the panel, with labeled ticks at 0, 10, 20, 30, 40, and 50.
- Four different plotting symbols are used, with no legend printed anywhere on the figure: open circles, filled (solid) circles, "x" marks, and "+" marks.
- A dense cluster of points fills the lower part of the plot, roughly between 0.1 and 1.35 m of soil thickness, spread across the whole 0–50° range of angles. Within that cluster the open circles, filled circles, x's, and +'s are interleaved.
- Two smooth curves are drawn through the plot, forming an upper and a lower envelope around the dense cluster. The upper curve starts at about 0.75 m at 0°, rises to a broad maximum of about 1.35 m near 18–20°, then falls steadily to about 0.2 m at 50°. The lower curve starts at about 0.3 m at 0°, stays nearly flat at about 0.35–0.4 m out to roughly 20–25°, then declines to near 0.05 m at about 40° before rising slightly to meet the upper curve at about 0.2 m at 50°. The two curves converge at the right-hand end.
- Well above the upper envelope, a scattered set of about twenty open circles lies between roughly 1.6 and 4.0 m of thickness, spread across angles from about 5° to about 40°. Readable positions among these outlying points include: two circles near 2.0 m at about 5° and 6°; a circle near 2.0 m at about 9°; a circle near 3.0 m at about 10°; a circle near 3.0 m at about 15°; a circle near 2.6 m at about 17°; a circle near 2.0 m at about 20°; a circle near 2.25 m at about 25°; a circle near 2.0 m at about 25°; a circle near 1.6 m at about 30°; a circle near 3.55 m at about 30°; a circle near 2.0 m at about 31°; a circle near 4.0 m at about 35°; a circle near 3.0 m at about 35°; a circle near 2.0 m at about 35°; and a circle near 2.55 m at about 40°.
- A single small downward-pointing arrow is drawn just above the upper envelope near 23° at about 1.25 m, next to an "x" symbol. No text annotation accompanies it.

Bottom panel — a cross-section drawing in an L-shaped axis frame (left and bottom axes, with tick marks also along the top right of the plot area).

- Y axis, labeled "Meters", with labeled ticks at 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6.
- X axis, labeled "Meters" beneath the panel, with labeled ticks at 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6.
- The drawing is a vertical slice through a hillslope hollow, built from three stacked layers separated by irregular hand-drawn boundaries:
  - An upper layer stippled with small open circles (a coarse, clast-rich fill). Its top surface is the ground surface, starting at about 3.6 m elevation at x = 0, rising irregularly to a peak of about 5.1 m at roughly x = 2.4, then dropping and declining gradually to about 3.4 m at the right-hand end near x = 5.6.
  - Beneath it, a layer hatched with short parallel oblique lines. Its upper boundary — the base of the circle-stippled fill — starts near 3.0 m at x = 0, dips and rises irregularly, reaches a low of about 0.7 m at roughly x = 3.1–3.2, then climbs back to about 2.7–2.8 m at the right end.
  - At the far left, between about x = 0.2 and x = 0.55, a narrow vertical block filled with cross-hatching (a diamond mesh) runs from the base up to about 3.0 m, set into the hatched layer.
- The stippled fill is thickest in the middle of the section, where the underlying hatched surface is lowest, and thins toward both ends. No further text labels appear inside either panel.

**What it demonstrates.** The scatter plot presents the raw relationship as measured: soil thickness varies enormously at any given hillslope angle, but the envelope curves show that the bulk of the observations sit in a band that thickens on gentle-to-moderate slopes and thins toward the steepest ones. The cross-section supplies the physical picture that goes with the band — soil accumulates as a wedge in the hollow, thick where the underlying rock surface is low and thin where it is high.

*Schimel's own graduate-school anecdote about advisor Mary Firestone* (p. 12–13). Schimel recounts bringing what he thought were simple questions to Firestone and having conversations spiral into topics that seemed irrelevant — he gives the example of the kinetics of bacterial glutamine synthetase coming up in a discussion of plant-root competition with microorganisms for soil nitrate. He came to understand, over years of working with her, that she was teaching him to explore the fringes of a problem: "She was exploring the issues to deepen our thinking, to ensure we found the story that tied together the sometimes apparently contradictory data, and to identify issues that might trip us up later" (p. 13). He draws the general lesson: resist the pressure to publish prematurely, since "one good paper can launch a career; many mediocre ones build a rather different one" (p. 13).

*The three aspects of storytelling* (p. 14). Schimel closes the chapter by naming the three components of effective storytelling that structure the rest of the book: content — what makes a story engage and stay with the reader; structure — how to arrange content so it is easy to grasp; and language — how to write the story in the most compelling way. He states directly: "This book is about these three issues" (p. 14).

**EXERCISES** (p. 14–15).

*Exercise 2.1, "Analyze published papers"* (p. 14). Pick several papers from the primary literature that you will return to across later chapters. Suggested selections:

- A paper from a specialist journal written by a leader recognized as a strong writer.
- A "normal" paper from a specialist journal.
- A review or synthesis paper.
- A paper from *Nature* or *Science* or some journal that targets a broad audience.

For each: identify the key story points; assess whether the authors highlighted that story well; determine how far along the data-to-understanding flow the paper went; and consider whether it could have gone further.

*Exercise 2.2, "Write a short article"* (p. 14–15), adapted from an exercise developed by Ruth Yanai at SUNY-ESF (credited by her to Bill Graves, Dick Gladon, and Mike Kelly at Iowa State University, p. 14 n. 4). It has three steps:

Step 1 — Identify the Key Story Points for your work. For each of the following, write a short paragraph of no more than two to three sentences:

1. What is your opening? This should identify the larger problem to which you are contributing, give readers a sense of the direction your paper is going, and make it clear why it is important. It should engage the widest audience practical.
2. What is your specific question or hypothesis?
3. What are the key results of your work? Identify these in a short list of no more than two to three points.
4. What is your main conclusion? What did you learn about nature? This should use the results from item 3 to answer the question from item 2, and should address the larger problem identified in item 1.

Step 2 — Write the article. Target audience: scientists who are not specialists in your discipline. The goal is to tell the story of your work and engage and educate readers, not write a technical paper; tone can range from somewhat technical to more casual but must interest technical readers. Use the answers from Step 1 to frame the story. The word limit is strict: 800–850 words.

Step 3 — Analyze your writing. Circulate articles within a writers' group of three to four people, have members analyze and edit each other's work, then discuss using these questions:

1. What did the author do well? (It's always good to start positive.)
2. Was the topic interesting? Was it cast at the right level and hit the right audience? Could you have rewritten it to engage a wider audience? Did it make you want to read the rest of the piece?
3. Was the specific question clear?
4. Were the results clear? Did they relate to the topic and the specific question?
5. Were the conclusions true conclusions, or were they merely a restatement of the results? Did they relate to the large issues raised in the opening? Did they answer the specific question asked? Did they clearly grow from the results presented in the piece?
6. What did you get as the "take-home" message of the story? Do you believe that this was the message the author was trying to give you?
7. Was the writing clear? If not, can you figure out why and identify ways to make it clearer?

**Terms introduced.**

- **Story (as applied to a paper).** A paper "tells a story about nature and how it works; it builds the story from the data but the data are not the story" (p. 8).
- **Data → Information → Knowledge → Understanding.** Schimel's model of the progression a scientist moves through: raw data from an instrument are converted into information, which is transformed into knowledge, which is synthesized into understanding (Figure 2.2, reproduced above under Mechanism) (p. 11). A paper's value depends on how far along this progression it carries the reader.
- **Characters.** In Schimel's adaptation of Lamott's fiction-writing advice, the molecules, organisms, ecosystems, or concepts a scientist studies, which the scientist must "listen to" through careful, open-minded data analysis rather than force into a predetermined plot (p. 9).
- **Elaborate and slash.** The two-stage process of developing a story from data: first explore the data expansively, overinterpreting them and considering their farthest-fringe implications, then apply "Occam's razor and slash away to find the simple core" (p. 12).
- **Content, structure, language.** The three aspects of effective storytelling Schimel identifies as organizing the rest of the book: what makes a story engage and stay with the reader (content), how the content is arranged for the reader to follow easily (structure), and how it is phrased for maximum effect (language) (p. 14).

**Connections.** Chapter 2 establishes the content/structure/language framework that Schimel states organizes the remainder of the book, and its data-to-understanding model and "data are not the story" formula recur as the conceptual basis for the structural guidance (e.g., how far a paper's Discussion should push toward understanding) given in later chapters. Exercise 2.1's paper selections and Exercise 2.2's short article are both designed to be reused in exercises attached to later chapters.


### Chapter 3 — Making a Story Sticky

**Claim.** The chapter opens with an epigraph attributed to Chip Heath and Dan Heath: "A sticky idea is an idea that is more likely to make a difference" (p. 16). A paper's value depends on whether it is remembered and used after it is read — whether it has "legs." Chapter 3 imports the Heaths' *Made to Stick* framework for what makes an idea memorable and applies its six factors to science writing, arguing that they are fundamental to good storytelling and thus to good science writing (p. 16–17).

**Mechanism.** The chapter organizes the Heaths' six stickiness factors under the mnemonic **SUCCES** (p. 17):

- **S**: Simple
- **U**: Unexpected
- **C**: Concrete
- **C**: Credible
- **E**: Emotional
- **S**: Stories

Schimel states he introduces these factors briefly in this chapter and returns to them repeatedly through the rest of the book (p. 17).

*Simple (3.1, p. 17–21).* A simple idea "contains the core essence of an important idea in a clear compact way" (p. 17). The chapter distinguishes simple from *simplistic*: simplistic messages are "dumbed down, trivialize the issue, or dodge the core of the problem, rather than targeting it" (p. 17), whereas a genuinely simple message condenses a complex argument without avoiding it. Most science, the chapter argues, is driven by simple core ideas that carry a large explanatory swath, and a scientist's job is to find the simple core within complexity — "It takes no special talent to see the complex in the complex. Cutting through the clutter to see the simple in the complex is what distinguishes great scientists from the merely competent" (p. 18). A subsection (3.1.1, p. 20–21) explains one route to simplicity: building on **schemas** — a term the Heaths borrow from psychology for "ideas we bring with us to a problem" (p. 20). New material is easiest to learn when it attaches to an existing schema; communicating effectively requires knowing what schemas the audience already holds and building from them, since assuming schemas readers lack confuses them, while over-explaining schemas they do hold reads as condescending (p. 21). Because writers take their own core ideas for granted, they tend to write to the schemas of their closest colleagues, which narrows their reach; reaching a broader audience requires checking whether one's terms connect to the target audience's schemas and, if not, redefining ideas in simpler, more broadly held terms (p. 21).

*Unexpected (3.2, p. 21–22).* Papers that merely add another data point, a slight methodological variant, or confirmation of existing dogma are forgettable; most papers are incremental in this way. Truly sticky papers say something new and unexpected (p. 21). The chapter locates novelty in the questions asked and the interpretations developed, not necessarily in the data itself, and invokes the **knowledge gap theory of curiosity** (attributed to Heath and Heath): identifying and highlighting an unknown within a mass of existing knowledge creates unexpectedness and engages a reader's curiosity (p. 21). Knowledge gaps need not be large — "it is better to write about a small knowledge gap than about no knowledge gap at all" (p. 22). The chapter warns that scientists, wanting to show off what they know, often default to presenting facts rather than first establishing why the reader needs them, quoting Heath and Heath: "Our tendency is to tell people the facts. First, though, they must realize they need them" (p. 22). The recommended move is to frame a knowledge gap using what is already known to mark its boundaries — "like framing a window — build the structure to support the area you will fill in" (p. 22).

*Concrete (3.3, p. 22–23).* Epigraph: "If those who have studied the art of writing are in accord on any one point, it is this: the surest way to arouse and hold the reader's attention is by being specific, definite, and concrete." — Strunk and White, *The Elements of Style*, quoted p. 22. Concreteness is presented as adding force to simplicity: "A balloon is simple, but you notice more when you get hit in the head by a brick" (p. 22). The chapter frames a structural tension: science is grounded in concrete data but organized by abstract ideas, and expertise itself consists partly of converting concrete detail into abstraction. It introduces Roy Peter Clark's **Ladder of Abstraction** (from *Writing Tools*, 2006) as a model of this continuum (p. 23): at the top are the widest abstractions — simple, broadly understandable ideas such as survival of the fittest or plate tectonics; at the bottom are the physical facts, the actual data collected. Both extremes are described as tractable for most readers. The "danger zone" is the middle rung — small-scale abstractions that are neither concrete details nor high-level schemas, populated by the field-specific concepts (sexual selection, Hardy-Weinberg equilibria, genetic drift, knockout mutations, ribozymes, transcriptional silencers) that outsiders experience as jargon (p. 23). Because scientists are drawn to write at this middle level, papers often become accessible only to a narrow expert audience; the chapter's recommendation is to ground middle-rung concepts either in widely held schemas or in explanatory concrete detail, with fuller treatment deferred to chapters 11 and 14 (p. 23).

*Credible (3.4, p. 23–24).* "Science writing that isn't credible is science fiction" (p. 23). Credibility is presented as running hand in hand with concreteness and built as a chain: credibility of ideas comes from grounding them in and citing previous work; credibility of data comes from describing methods, presenting data clearly, and using appropriate statistics; credibility of conclusions comes from showing they grow from that credible data. "A break anywhere in that chain makes the whole endeavour lose credibility" (p. 23).

*Emotional (3.5, p. 24).* The chapter identifies curiosity as the one emotion that is both acceptable and fundamental to science — "We became scientists because we are curious — we are driven to solve the puzzles that nature presents. To engage us in your work, you need to engage our curiosity. You do that by asking a novel question" (p. 24). Offering new information without an engaging question appeals only to a weaker emotion — "our inner nerd and our love for accumulating trivia" — which the chapter states will not get a paper published or a proposal funded (p. 24). The Emotional factor is described as closely tied to Unexpected, since novelty generates curiosity; the recommended reframing is to shift from "what information do I have to offer?" to "what knowledge do I have to offer?", or from "what's my answer?" to "what's my question?" (p. 24). For proposals specifically, the chapter states that because roughly twice as many proposals are judged fundable as there is money to fund, a proposal needs at least one panelist excited enough to become its advocate; without such an advocate, the outcome is a "fundable but not funded" letter. Excitement is named as the second acceptable emotion in science, growing out of curiosity (p. 24).

*Stories (3.6, p. 24–25).* The chapter states that the whole book concerns telling stories, but that stories are modular: "a single large story is crafted from a collection of smaller story units, threaded together," and writing a good paper requires thinking about internal structure and how to integrate story modules (p. 24–25).

The chapter closes (p. 25) by restating that the six SUCCES elements are integral to effective storytelling and science writing, and recommends working out in advance how to weave them into a piece of writing — particularly finding the simple story, built around the key questions that engage Unexpected and Emotional, which then guide the material selected to make the story Concrete and Credible.

**Evidence and examples.**

1. *Lincoln and Vallandigham (p. 17).* During the Civil War, Lincoln faced criticism after Clement Vallandigham, an antiwar Democrat, was arrested for treason for urging soldiers to desert. Lincoln reframed the complex legal and political dispute with a single question:

   > "Must I shoot a simple-minded soldier boy who deserts, while I must not touch the hair of a wily agitator who induces him to desert?" (p. 17)

   The chapter explains this collapsed the controversy into a simple moral dilemma, shifting the sympathetic victim from Vallandigham to the soldier at risk, and thereby refocused and shut down the controversy.

2. *"It's the economy, stupid" (p. 17, reused p. 22).* Cited as an even simpler message than Lincoln's, used by Bill Clinton in his presidential campaign. Later reused in the Concrete section as an example of a simple message with concrete force, and as the tool Clinton used to pull himself back to his core message whenever discussion drifted (p. 22).

3. *"You pay too much in taxes" (p. 17).* Given as an example of a simplistic rather than simple message — "catchy, appealing, and might even be true," but it avoids the underlying issues of what services taxes fund, whether those services are wanted, and whether they represent good value, rather than condensing that argument.

4. *Simple ideas driving whole fields (p. 18).* Natural selection ("fit organisms survive and pass on their genes while unfit ones don't") is described as driving biology; plate tectonics as driving modern geology, explaining landmass shape, mountain-range rise and fall, and long-term planetary geochemistry; atomic orbital theory and hybrid orbitals as driving organic chemistry; the double helix and genetic code as driving molecular biology. The chapter states these simple ideas provide the large structure onto which more complex dynamics elaborate, without explaining the fine detail themselves.

5. *Colleague's remark (p. 18).* A colleague, described as having been elected to the U.S. National Academy of Sciences on the strength of this ability, is quoted: "I have to make things simplistic enough that I can understand them" — glossed by Schimel as meaning the colleague looks for the simple explanation that captures a problem's essence so others can apply the insight to their own systems.

6. *Schimel's nitrogen mineralization paper (p. 18).* Described as Schimel's most highly cited paper, a synthesis proposing a new hypothesis for how soil's physical structure regulates microbial nitrogen use and thus the nitrogen forms available to plants (Schimel and Bennett, "Nitrogen Mineralization: Challenges of a Changing Paradigm," *Ecology* 85 (2004): 591–602, cited p. 18). The chapter states the paper's essence is captured in a cartoon illustrating the interactions among chemicals, organisms, and spatial soil patches — Figure 3.1.

   #### Figure 3.1 (p. 19)

   **Caption.** Figure 3.1. Changing patterns of N-flow in soil as N-availability increases. From Schimel and Bennett (2004).
   Copyright © 2004 Ecological Society of America. Reprinted with permission.

   **What it shows.** One large framed figure divided into four equal panels in a 2 × 2 grid (Case A top left, Case B top right, Case C bottom left, Case D bottom right). Every panel repeats the same underlying cartoon and differs only in which arrows are drawn and how heavy they are.

   The elements common to all four panels:

   - A plant root drawn as a broad, pale gray tapering shape entering from the top-right corner and pointing down and to the left, its tip near the panel center. Fine black root hairs branch off it, with a few small barbs along its upper edge.
   - At the left, a shape outlined with a dotted line, labeled beneath it "Relatively N rich microsite". Inside it, a vertical chain of italic labels connected by short solid gray arrows pointing downward: "protein" → "amino acids" → "microbes".
   - At the lower right, a second dotted-outline shape, labeled beneath it "Relatively N poor microsite". Inside it: "protein" with a short gray downward arrow to "amino acids", and a short gray arrow pointing left from "amino acids" to "microbes".
   - A panel title in the upper-left corner, in plain (non-italic) type.

   Panel by panel:

   **Case A — title "Case A: amino acids are used-not mineralized".** In the N rich microsite the chain runs protein → amino acids → microbes, with "microbes" in bold italic. A medium gray arrow leaves the N rich microsite at the level of "amino acids", travels right, and reaches a junction point near the center of the panel; from that junction two gray arrows continue — one up and to the right into the root tip, and one down and to the right into the N poor microsite, ending at "microbes". In the N poor microsite "microbes" is set in plain italic, not bold, and receives the short arrow from "amino acids". No NH4+ or NO3- labels appear in this panel. All arrows are of uniform, moderate weight.

   **Case B — title "Case B: N rich sites mineralize N poor sites immobilize".** The N rich chain is extended: protein → amino acids → microbes (bold) → NH4+ (set with subscript 4 and superscript +). A thin, pale gray arrow runs from NH4+ diagonally right and down to NO3- (set with subscript 3 and superscript −). A heavy dark gray arrow starts at NH4+, runs up and to the right to the central junction, and continues from the junction up into the root tip. A second, thinner light gray arrow runs from NO3- up to that same junction. From the junction a heavy dark gray arrow also branches down and to the right into the N poor microsite, ending at "microbes", which here is set in bold italic. The N poor microsite otherwise shows protein → amino acids → microbes as before.

   **Case C — title "Case C: N rich sites mineralize N poor sites meet needs".** The N rich chain is again protein → amino acids → microbes (bold) → NH4+, with a thin pale gray arrow NH4+ → NO3-. A heavy dark gray arrow runs from NH4+ up and to the right, through the junction, and on into the root tip; a thinner light gray arrow runs from NO3- up to join it at the junction. There is no arrow branching down into the N poor microsite — the downward limb present in Cases A and B is absent here. The N poor microsite contains protein → amino acids → microbes with "microbes" in bold italic, fed only from its own amino acids.

   **Case D — title "Case D: N meets biotic needs nitrification dominates".** The N rich chain is protein → amino acids → microbes (bold) → NH4+, and the NH4+ → NO3- arrow is now drawn heavy and dark, the same weight as the transport arrows, rather than thin and pale. A single long dark gray arrow runs from the bottom of the N rich microsite, at the NO3- level, straight up and to the right into the root tip, with no intermediate junction and no downward branch. The N poor microsite again shows protein → amino acids → microbes with "microbes" in bold italic, connected to nothing else.

   **What it demonstrates.** The four panels are the same soil system at four increasing levels of nitrogen availability, and the changing arrows carry the argument. At the lowest availability (A), nitrogen never becomes mineral at all — amino acids move directly to the plant and to microbes in the poor patch. As availability rises (B), rich patches mineralize to ammonium and nitrate while poor patches still capture and immobilize the surplus. Higher still (C), the poor patches meet their own needs, so the surplus flows only to the plant. At the highest availability (D), biotic demand is fully satisfied, so nitrification takes over and the dominant flow is ammonium to nitrate to plant. The heavy versus light arrow weights mark which pathway dominates in each case.

   Schimel adds that reading Heath and Heath helped him realize he had been searching for the simple explanation, and that as a visual person he looks for it in a picture (p. 18).

7. *Freeze-thaw tundra soils paper (p. 18–19).* A contrasting case illustrating the simple/simplistic distinction, drawn from Schimel and Clein, "Microbial Response to Freeze-Thaw Cycles in Tundra and Taiga Soils," *Soil Biology and Biochemistry* 28 (1996): 1061–66 (cited p. 18). Freeze-thaw cycles increased microbial respiration relative to a control in some arctic tundra soils and decreased it in others; the initial submitted version of the paper reported this apparent inconsistency, with no discernible pattern, as the "simple" story. Reviewers judged that story simplistic. Schimel and his coauthor then found the underlying pattern: in nitrogen-rich soils, freeze-thaw cycles reduced respiration, while in nitrogen-poor soils they enhanced it — a pattern suggesting mechanisms for future testing (p. 19). The chapter states the revised paper has since been cited over 100 times, credited to the reviewers having pushed for a better analysis. Schimel draws a maxim from the episode: "The pain of an embarrassing review lasts a few days, the pain of an embarrassing paper lasts a lifetime" (p. 19).

8. *Alligator meat (p. 20).* A before/after pair illustrating schema use:

   > "It's a light-colored, finely textured meat, with very little fat. It cuts easily and is moist if not overcooked. The flavor is mild." (p. 20)

   versus

   > "It tastes like chicken, but a little meatier." (p. 20)

   The chapter explains the first version lists traits but is not evocative; the second grounds the new taste in an already-known schema (the taste of chicken), getting the reader most of the way to understanding even though alligator does not taste exactly like chicken.

9. *Science curricula as sequential schema-building (p. 20).* First-year inorganic chemistry introduces electron orbitals as energy bands; second-year organic chemistry modifies that schema with hybrid orbitals and resonance structures; third-year physical chemistry introduces the Schrödinger equation, treating orbitals as probabilistic electron distributions. Similarly, molecular biology begins with the simple transcription/translation model (DNA → RNA → protein) and the one-gene/one-product idea, before introducing post-translational modification and overlapping reading frames. The chapter uses this to illustrate that each teaching step takes a simple schema and elaborates it, and that starting simple — even in a way an expert would consider a "ghastly misrepresentation" — is necessary before building up nuance (p. 20).

10. *Reviewed proposal (p. 23–24).* Schimel recounts reviewing a proposal whose introduction was "loaded... up with boldface, buzzwords, and hype" rather than fleshed out, leading him to expect it was weak — "I was sure that with that much lipstick, the proposal had to be a pig." The lack of concreteness undermined credibility. He reports being surprised that the body of the proposal was in fact stellar, well thought out, and addressed all program goals, and states that the proposal only became credible once it became concrete — the point that converted him from skeptic to supporter (p. 23–24).

11. *Chapter 2's story modules, recalled as an example of modularity (p. 25).* Schimel describes his own chapter 2 narrative as built from three linked story modules: one centered on Elizabeth Kolbert and her observation that scientists don't tell stories, with Kolbert, scientists, and the idea of "story" itself as characters; one using the story of the discovery of the structure of DNA to illustrate the progression from data to understanding; and one using the stories of Bill Dietrich's doctoral work and Schimel's own work to illustrate "listening to your characters." He states the aim was for each short module to be sticky on its own and for the three together to create a sticky overall story (p. 25).

**Terms introduced.**

- **Sticky / stickiness idea** — from Chip and Dan Heath, *Made to Stick* (2007): "an idea that is more likely to make a difference" (epigraph, p. 16); the property of being remembered well after first encounter.
- **SUCCES** — mnemonic organizing the six Heath-and-Heath stickiness factors: Simple, Unexpected, Concrete, Credible, Emotional, Stories (p. 17).
- **Simple vs. simplistic** — simple: "contains the core essence of an important idea in a clear compact way" (p. 17); simplistic: "dumbed down, trivialize[s] the issue, or dodge[s] the core of the problem, rather than targeting it" (p. 17).
- **Schema** — term borrowed by Heath and Heath from psychology for "ideas we bring with us to a problem" (p. 20); the pre-existing intellectual structure new information is attached to during learning.
- **Knowledge gap theory of curiosity** — attributed to Heath and Heath: curiosity is engaged by identifying and highlighting an unknown within a mass of known material (p. 21).
- **Ladder of Abstraction** — Roy Peter Clark's model (*Writing Tools*, 2006), describing a continuum from widest abstractions (top) to physical facts/data (bottom), with a "danger zone" of narrow, expert-only abstractions in the middle rungs (p. 23).

**Connections.** Builds directly on chapter 2: the DNA-discovery story and the Kolbert and Bill Dietrich material used there are cited back as examples of story modularity (p. 25), and the "data through information and knowledge to understanding" discussion from chapter 2 is invoked to illustrate concreteness (p. 23). The chapter states its six SUCCES factors recur throughout the rest of the book (p. 17), and explicitly defers fuller treatment of grounding middle-rung, jargon-level concepts to chapters 11 and 14 (p. 23).

**EXERCISES** (p. 25).

3.1. *Analyze published papers.* Return to the papers being analyzed from earlier exercises. Identify how the authors used each SUCCES element; assess whether they did a good job and how they could have done better; try rewriting key passages to enhance their SUCCES power. Also identify what schemas the authors used in building the story, and whether those schemas are held only by a narrow subdiscipline or by a wider community.

3.2. *Write a short article.* Analyze the short article(s) written (by the reader and writing-group colleagues) for the chapter 2 exercise. Identify how well SUCCES elements were used; assess whether a better job could have been done; rewrite key passages to enhance their SUCCES power.


### Chapter 4 — Story Structure

**Claim.** All stories, including scientific ones, share four functional elements — Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution (OCAR) — and different genres and audiences call for different sequences of these elements. Understanding the elements and the structures built from them gives a writer a deliberate tool for matching a piece of science writing to its audience's patience, rather than defaulting to one structure without knowing why.

**Mechanism.** Schimel defines the four elements that underlie every story (p. 26–27):

1. **Opening (O):** "Whom is the story about? Who are the characters? Where does it take place? What do you need to understand about the situation to follow the story? What is the larger problem you are addressing?" (p. 26)
2. **Challenge (C):** "What do your characters need to accomplish? What specific question do you propose to answer?" (p. 27)
3. **Action (A):** "What happens to address the challenge? In a paper, this describes the work you did; in a proposal, it describes the work you hope to do." (p. 27)
4. **Resolution (R):** "How have the characters and their world changed as a result of the action? This is your conclusion — what did you learn from your work?" (p. 27)

Together these form the acronym OCAR, which Schimel states "echoes throughout this book, whether we are talking about whole papers, sections, paragraphs, or even individual sentences" (p. 27). A story missing any one of the four elements is, in his words, "unsatisfying, ineffective, and slippery, rather than sticky" (p. 27).

Section 4.1 sets out four core story structures, ordered along a continuum defined by how patient the audience is willing to be before reaching the point of the story:

- **OCAR structure:** Taking the four elements in strict sequence — opening, then challenge, then action, then resolution. Schimel calls it "the simplest, but also the most slowly developing structure" (p. 27). It is, he states, "the structure we use most frequently in science because readers are patient — they want to assess your ideas and results as they develop. They want to see the evidence and the arguments clearly laid out before any conclusions are presented." (p. 27) In OCAR, a paper's challenge sits at the end of the introduction and its conclusion comes at the end.
- **ABDCE structure:** For a less patient audience. Its elements (p. 27–28):
  - **Action (A):** "Start with a dramatic action to immediately engage readers and entice them to keep reading."
  - **Background (B):** "Fill the readers in on the characters and setting so they can understand the story as it develops."
  - **Development (D):** "Follow the action as the story develops to the climax."
  - **Climax (C):** "Bring all the threads of the story together and address them."
  - **Ending (E):** "What happened to the characters after the climax? (This is the same as the resolution.)"

  Schimel states this is "the structure that modern fiction writers and scientific proposal writers use most frequently" (p. 27). The relationship to OCAR: "the difference between ABDCE and OCAR is that ABDCE front-loads the story by moving the challenge up and collapsing it into the opening to create the initial 'action' ... Thus, A and B together comprise the O and C elements." (p. 28) He notes the trade-off: ABDCE "gets the reader into the story faster ... But it is less efficient than OCAR in moving the story forward — after the initial action, you have to back up and fill in the background. That inefficiency is a fair trade, however, if it gets readers committed to the rest of the story." (p. 28)
- **LD structure (Lead/Development):** For an audience too impatient to stay for a resolution. Schimel names this after the newspaper reporters' "inverted pyramid," relabeling it Lead/Development "to highlight its key functional elements" (p. 29). "In LD structure, the core of the story is in the first sentences (the lead, L) and the rest fills out and develops the story (the development, D). In LD structure, the lead collapses the opening, challenge, and resolution into a single short section, possibly as little as a single sentence." (p. 29) He attributes its origin to the newspaper business's physical space constraints and to the risk that editors cut a story's final paragraphs to fit layout, meaning "writers cannot put the point at the end of the story — if they did, readers might never see it. It must go at the beginning." (p. 30)
- **LDR structure (Lead/Development/Resolution):** Used by magazine journalists, whose readers are "tuned to a slightly more patient audience" than newspaper readers (p. 30). "The lead must be engaging enough to commit you to the story, but the writer can realistically hope that if you start reading, you may actually finish." Because magazines are not under newspaper-style production pressure, "magazine writers can afford to worry about ending well with an effective resolution." (p. 30)

Schimel summarizes the continuum (p. 30):

1. **OCAR:** "Slowest — take your time working into the story."
2. **ABDCE:** "Faster — get right into the action."
3. **LDR:** "Faster yet — but people will read to the end."
4. **LD:** "Fastest — the whole story is up front."

He adds a diagnostic use for the structure: "You should be able to read the O, C, and R of a paper, and still get its key points. If you know the problem, the specific questions, the general approach to answering them, and the conclusions, you may have gotten all you need from that paper. You'll certainly know whether you need to go back and read it fully." (p. 30)

Section 4.2 applies the structures to specific science-writing contexts. Specialist journals generally use straight OCAR, "framing the challenge at the end of the introduction," because their readers are patient and want the full progression from ideas through results to conclusions; Schimel adds that in these journals "a lead-based paper might be considered suspect" because front-loading conclusions can look like the writer "knew the story you wanted to tell and were simply forcing the data into that story — that is, you are trying to prove rather than test your ideas." (p. 31) Generalist journals such as *Nature* or *Science* need a faster structure, "often closer to LDR," because their editors are professional generalists who must triage a large volume of submissions quickly to decide what to send for review (p. 31). Proposals sit at the least-patient end of the continuum: "A proposal must convince reviewers that the topic identified in the opening is important and then compel them with the excitement of the questions posed in the challenge. If it fails to do this, it is dead." (p. 31–32) Schimel states his own proposal-reviewing practice: "I make a 'no/maybe' cut by the end of the introduction, and if it's a 'no,' that is irrevocable. I then only read the rest to be able to give feedback on how to improve the proposal for resubmission. A good experimental design can never compensate for boring questions." (p. 32) To succeed with this kind of reader, a proposal needs "a front-loaded structure: either ABDCE or LDR." (p. 32)

Section 4.3 maps OCAR onto the physical IMRaD structure (Introduction, Methods, Results, and Discussion) that most science papers actually use. Schimel states the reason for distinguishing the two: "While IMRaD is a rule, OCAR is a principle" (p. 32) — even when a paper's labeled sections follow IMRaD, its conceptual structure follows OCAR; IMRaD itself has many field-specific permutations, and some forms (review papers, proposals) do not follow IMRaD at all, yet must still "cover the OCAR bases" (p. 32). The mapping he gives (p. 32–33):

- **Introduction**, with three unlabeled subsections:
  - **Opening:** "typically the first paragraph that introduces the larger problem the paper is targeting. What is the context, and what are the characters we are studying?"
  - **Background:** "What information does the reader need to understand the specific work the authors did, why it is important, and what it will contribute to the larger issue?" Schimel treats this as "an extension of the O, as it fleshes out introducing the characters."
  - **Challenge:** "What are the specific hypotheses/questions/goals of the current work?"
- **Materials and Methods:** "begins describing the action — what did you do?"
- **Results:** "continues the action by describing your findings."
- **Discussion:** "develops to the climax and the resolution. What did it all mean, and what have you learned? It often ends with a conclusions subsection that is the resolution."

Summary of the mapping: "opening and challenge block the beginning and the end of the Introduction. Action encompasses the M&M, Results, and much of the Discussion. The resolution is the last section of the Discussion." (p. 33) Schimel restates the stakes of the resolution for science: "for science, at the end, we want to know how our understanding of the world has changed as a result of your work, or in the case of a proposal, how you think our understanding of the world will change. The resolution must map back to the opening. It must say something about the larger problem you identified there." (p. 33)

A related structural claim: scientific papers have an "hourglass shape" to their content (Figure 4.2). They open on a problem of wide interest, narrow to the specific questions that make up the challenge — "that is as narrow as it is going to get" — stay narrow through Methods and Results, then widen again through the Discussion toward conclusions "as 'wide' as the opening," which is "what makes the story circular — in the resolution, you come back to the issue targeted in the opening." (p. 33)

The chapter also states that a resolved OCAR or ABDCE story is circular but not a true circle — because the characters and their understanding have changed, "the 'beginning' has moved. Thus, a story isn't truly a circle, but a spiral (see figure 4.1)." (p. 29) Schimel calls a story lacking a resolution a fall into "the 'Theater of the Absurd'" for fiction, or in science, into "the reject bin." (p. 29)

#### Figure 4.1 (p. 29)

**Caption.** Figure 4.1. How an OCAR story makes a spiral: the story comes back to its starting point, but that point has moved.

**What it shows.** A single continuous line drawing of a loop with one crossing, like a lasso or a slipknot, drawn in plain black line with no shading, no box, and no axes. Four capital letters label it, and they are the only text inside the figure: **O** top left, **R** top right, **A** lower left, **C** lower right.

The crossing point sits at the top center of the drawing. Four line ends meet there:

- A nearly horizontal line enters the crossing from the left. Its left end carries a heavy solid fletching — a thick, filled arrow tail shaped like a barbed feather, wider than the line itself — and the letter **O** sits immediately to the left of that fletching, at the top left of the figure.
- From the crossing, a curve descends to the lower left, sweeps down the left side of the drawing, and stops just above the letter **A**. **A** sits at the lower left, in the break in the line.
- The line resumes just below **A**, curves along the bottom of the drawing as a shallow U, and rises on the right to stop just below the letter **C**. **C** sits at the lower right, at the same height as **A**, also in a break in the line.
- The line resumes just above **C**, rises up the right side, and returns to the crossing at the top.
- From the crossing, a nearly horizontal line continues to the upper right and terminates in a solid filled triangular arrowhead pointing right. The letter **R** sits immediately to the right of that arrowhead, at the top right.

The body of the loop is an oval, taller than it is wide, open at the top where the two ends cross and run past each other to O on the left and R on the right. The O-to-crossing segment and the crossing-to-R segment lie almost on the same horizontal line, with O and R sitting on the same baseline; the arrow tail at O is drawn markedly thicker than the arrowhead at R. Following the stroke from the fletching at O, it runs right through the crossing, descends the right side of the oval to C, passes along the bottom to A, climbs the left side, and exits through the crossing to the arrowhead at R — path order O, C, A, R, the sequence the acronym names.

**What it demonstrates.** The story travels from the opening, down and around through the action and the challenge, and comes back up to the top of the loop — but instead of closing onto O, the path crosses over itself and runs out to R, a point displaced from where it began. The near-closure plus the crossing is the figure's whole argument: a story is not a circle that returns to its starting point, it is a spiral that returns to the starting place only after that place has moved.

#### Figure 4.2 (p. 33)

**Caption.** Figure 4.2. The hourglass structure of a paper. It starts wide with the opening, narrows with the challenge and action, and widens back out again at the resolution.

**What it shows.** A line-drawn hourglass on the left of the figure, with four lines of italic annotation stacked to its right. All shapes are open outlines, black on white, no fill or shading.

The hourglass, from top to bottom:

1. **Top trapezoid** — a wide horizontal line across the top, and two sides sloping inward as they descend, so the shape narrows downward. The word **Opening** is set inside it, near the top.
2. **Challenge box** — a small horizontal rectangle, drawn with all four sides, spanning the full width of the narrowed neck where the top trapezoid ends. It contains the word **Challenge**. The sloping sides of the top trapezoid land on the top corners of this box.
3. **Neck** — two vertical parallel lines dropping straight down from the bottom corners of the Challenge box, holding the width constant.
4. **Action bar** — a tall, narrow vertical rectangle drawn inside the neck, centered, running from just below the Challenge box down past the point where the neck flares. It contains the word **Action**, rotated ninety degrees so it reads bottom-to-top.
5. **Bottom trapezoid** — partway down, the two neck lines break outward and slope away from each other as they descend, widening the shape back out, and close on a wide horizontal base line at the bottom. The word **Resolution** is set inside this bottom trapezoid, just above the base line and below the lower end of the Action bar.

The top horizontal line and the bottom horizontal base line are drawn at roughly the same width, so the hourglass is as wide at the bottom as at the top.

To the right of the hourglass, four italic annotations are set at heights that align with the parts of the shape:

- Aligned with the top trapezoid: *Introduction: introduce characters and question. Narrow down to your specific questions*
- Aligned just below the Challenge box: *M&M and Results: What you did and what you found*
- Aligned with the middle of the Action bar: *Discussion: What it means*
- Aligned with the bottom trapezoid: *Conclusions: Take home message*

**What it demonstrates.** The figure maps the four OCAR elements onto the physical width of a paper's content. The paper starts wide at the opening, contracts to its narrowest point at the challenge and holds that narrowness through the action, then flares back out to the resolution — and because the base is drawn as wide as the top, the conclusions are shown as addressing a topic as broad as the one the opening claimed. The right-hand annotations attach the IMRaD sections to that shape: Introduction to the narrowing top, M&M and Results to the neck, Discussion to the length of the action, Conclusions to the widening bottom.

**Evidence and examples.**

- *J. R. R. Tolkien's* **The Lord of the Rings**, offered as Schimel's example of OCAR's slow development in fiction: "Only in chapter 2 do we get the first inkling of Frodo and Sam's challenge — to take the Ring of Power to Mt. Doom to destroy it. It takes until halfway through the first book to learn the full challenge — take the ring to the fire and restore Aragorn to the throne of Gondor." (p. 27)
- **James Bond movies**, cited as the ABDCE apex in film: "always start with wild action sequences — boat chases, gunfights, explosions." (p. 28)
- **Sue Grafton, *C Is for Corpse*** — quoted in full as an ABDCE opening (p. 28):

  > I met Bobby Callahan on Monday of that week. By Thursday, he was dead. He was convinced someone was trying to kill him and it turned out to be true, but none of us figured it out in time to save him. I've never worked for a dead man before and I hope I won't have to do it again. This report is for him, for whatever it's worth.
  >
  > My name is Kinsey Millhone. I'm a licensed private investigator.

  Schimel's analysis: "we are thrown immediately into the action and introduced to the key characters, in this case the dead Bobby Callahan and the living Kinsey Millhone. We are also given the challenge that Kinsey faces: who killed Callahan, and why?" (p. 28)
- **Homer, *The Odyssey*** — quoted as an older instance of the same in medias res move (p. 28):

  > All the survivors of the war had reached their homes by now and so put the perils of battle and the sea behind them. Odysseus alone was prevented from returning to the home and wife he yearned for by that powerful goddess, the Nymph Calypso, who longed for him to marry her, and kept him in her vaulted cave.

  Schimel: "The classical in medias res structure of the epic is straight ABDCE." (p. 28)
- **Anne Lamott, *Bird by Bird*** — epigraph quoted at the head of the resolution discussion (p. 29), attributed to Lamott, not Schimel:

  > "The climax is that major event, usually toward the end, that brings all the tunes you have been playing so far into one major chord, after which at least one of your people is profoundly changed. If someone isn't changed, then what is the point of your story?"

- **William Zinsser, *On Writing Well*** — two separate epigraphs, both attributed to Zinsser:
  - On the lead (p. 29): "The most important sentence in any article is the first one. If it doesn't induce the reader to proceed to the second sentence, your article is dead. And if the second sentence doesn't induce him to continue to the third sentence, it's equally dead. Of such a progression of sentences, each tugging the reader forward until he is hooked, a writer constructs that fateful unit, the 'lead.'"
  - On the resolution (p. 30): "Often it takes just a few sentences to wrap things up. Ideally they should encapsulate the idea of the piece and conclude with a sentence that jolts us with its fitness or unexpectedness."
- **Samuel Beckett, *Waiting for Godot***, cited as a story without a resolution, placed in "the 'Theater of the Absurd'" (p. 29).
- **Watson and Crick, "Molecular Structure of Nucleic Acids: A Structure for Deoxyribose Nucleic Acid,"** *Nature* 171 (1953): 737–38, cited as "a famous use of a lead-based structure in science" (p. 31). Opening, quoted verbatim: "We wish to suggest a structure for the salt of deoxyribose nucleic acid (D.N.A.). This structure has novel features which are of considerable biological interest." Resolution, quoted verbatim: "It has not escaped our notice that the specific pairing we have postulated immediately suggests a possible copying mechanism for the genetic material." Schimel's comment: "They didn't bother to elaborate on that mechanism — it was obvious enough that they didn't need to." (p. 31)
- **D. A. Crossley** (University of Georgia), a saying Schimel reports hearing attributed to him, on proposal writing: "If you haven't told them in the first two pages, you haven't told them." (p. 32)

**Terms introduced.**

- **OCAR** — Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution: the four functional elements present in every story, taken in sequence as the base structure most used in science writing (p. 26–27).
- **Opening (O)** — establishes the characters, setting, and larger problem (p. 26).
- **Challenge (C)** — the specific question or task the characters/writer must address (p. 27).
- **Action (A)** — what is done (or will be done) to meet the challenge (p. 27).
- **Resolution (R)** — how the characters/understanding have changed as a result of the action; the conclusion (p. 27).
- **ABDCE** — Action, Background, Development, Climax, Ending: a front-loaded structure that opens with dramatic action, then backfills background, for a less patient audience; "A and B together comprise the O and C elements" (p. 27–28).
- **LD (Lead/Development)** — Schimel's relabeling of the newspaper "inverted pyramid"; the lead collapses opening, challenge, and resolution into a very short opening section, with no separate resolution (p. 29).
- **LDR (Lead/Development/Resolution)** — the magazine-journalism variant of LD that retains a distinct resolution, for a somewhat more patient audience than newspaper readers (p. 30).
- **Spiral structure** — Schimel's term for a resolved story's circularity: it returns to its starting point, but that point has moved because the characters/understanding have changed (p. 29; Figure 4.1).
- **IMRaD** — Introduction, Methods, Results, and Discussion: the common physical section structure of science papers, distinguished from OCAR as "a rule" versus OCAR as "a principle" (p. 32).
- **Hourglass structure** — the shape of a paper's content: wide at the opening, narrowing through the challenge and action (Methods/Results), widening again through the resolution back to a scope matching the opening (p. 33; Figure 4.2).

**Connections.** References chapter 2's writing exercise, noting that its questions were "asking you to define your OCAR elements" (Exercise 4.1.C, p. 34). States that "the following chapters" take up each OCAR element — O, C, A, and R — in turn (p. 33), setting up the chapters that follow this one.

EXERCISES (p. 34):

- **4.1. Analyze published papers**
  - A. Evaluate papers you are reading: which story structure do they use, where are the OCAR elements, and are they effective?
  - B. Pick a proposal and analyze it the same way.
  - C. Return to the chapter 2 exercise (the short piece you started writing) and notice that its questions were asking you to define your OCAR elements.
- **4.2. Write a short article** — Evaluate your own short article using the same analysis: what story structure did you use, was it appropriate for your intended audience, and did you have the OCAR elements in place? If you or your writing group find the OCAR elements underdeveloped, rewrite the piece to make the opening, challenge, and resolution sections clearer and more effective.


### Chapter 5 — The Opening

> The most important sentence in any article is the first one.
> — William Zinsser, *On Writing Well*, quoted p. 35

**Claim.** The opening — from the first sentence through the first paragraph and sometimes several more — is a "power position" that must accomplish three things at once: identify the problem driving the research, introduce the paper's characters, and target an audience (p. 35).
