An opening fails in one of exactly two ways, by giving readers misdirection or by giving them no direction, and the width of the opening's promise must match the width of the paper's actual resolution or readers end up cheated or unimpressed.

**Mechanism.**

*The three goals of an opening.* Schimel states the requirement directly:

> "Your first words have great leverage, making the beginning of a paper a 'power position.' You must use that power to accomplish three goals: identify the problem that drives the research, introduce the characters, and target an audience." (p. 35)

A short paper for a narrow expert audience can remind readers of a known problem quickly. A paper for a broader audience, whose readers hold different schemas, needs a longer, more complex opening (pp. 35–36).

*Two ways an opening fails.* Because the opening's job is to give direction, it can fail only by giving the wrong direction or none:

- **Misdirection (§5.2.1).** The opening sets up one story, then the paper turns out to be about something else, giving readers "mental whiplash" (p. 35) when the direction changes without warning.
- **No direction (§5.2.2).** The opening explains background/textbook material — a schema the target readers already hold — without ever indicating what the paper is actually about.

*Targeting the audience (§5.3).* Whether an opening works depends on who is reading it. The same underlying fact (only a small fraction of ocean bacteria have been cultured) is opened one way for a specialist journal and a different way for a generalist one (Examples 5.7–5.8). The same holds for proposals, where audience mismatch is described as "life or death" (p. 41): the redwood-fog proposal pair (Examples 5.9–5.10) shows that the identical research can be framed to attract one funding panel and to repel another.

*The two-step opening (§5.4).* For a broad audience that doesn't share the writer's schemas, Schimel recommends opening with an issue that engages the target readers and then modulating, in a second step, to the narrower topic the paper is actually about:

> "I call this a two-step opening for two reasons. One is to highlight that it does take two steps, but also to highlight that, like the dance, it must be quick — if you take more than two steps, you will stumble." (p. 44)

*Matching opening width to resolution width — the hourglass (§5.6).* The opening is the top of the "hourglass" (a structural image used elsewhere in the book); the paper's resolution is the bottom. Schimel's rule:

> "You should set your opening, the top of the hourglass, to draw in as broad a readership as you can manage.... To achieve that, the bottom of the hourglass should be the same width as the top." (p. 45)

Three outcomes follow, corresponding to Figure 5.1:

1. Opening wider than the resolution — **overpromising**. Readers feel cheated.
2. Opening the same width as the resolution — **on target**. Readers are satisfied.
3. Resolution wider than the opening — **underpromising**. Readers never learn the story would have interested them.

#### Figure 5.1 (p. 45)

**Caption.** Figure 5.1. Matching the opening to the resolution.

**What it shows.** Three line-drawn hourglass outlines, side by side in a row, each with a multi-line caption beneath it. The outlines are drawn as bare profiles only — no interior labels, no words inside any of the three shapes. Each profile is a top trapezoid narrowing downward, a short straight-sided neck, and a bottom trapezoid widening downward, closed by a horizontal line at the top and a horizontal line at the bottom. All three shapes have the same overall height and the same neck width; only the top and bottom widths differ.

Panel by panel, left to right:

- **Left (A).** Top line wide; sides slope sharply inward; bottom line distinctly narrower than the top. The mouth is much wider than the base. Caption beneath: *A. Opening wider than resolution: overpromising. Your readers will feel cheated.*
- **Center (B).** Top line and bottom line drawn at the same width, so the shape is symmetric top to bottom about its neck. Caption beneath: *B. On target. Your readers will be satisfied.*
- **Right (C).** Top line narrow, about half again the width of the neck; bottom line the same width as the centre panel's base, and distinctly wider than this panel's own top. The base is wider than the mouth. Caption beneath: *C. Resolution wider than opening: underpromising. Your readers won't ever see that you are telling a story that would interest them.*

The left panel's top and the center panel's top are drawn at the same width as each other; the center panel's bottom and the right panel's bottom are drawn at the same width as each other. The right panel's caption runs to five lines and so extends further down the page than the other two.

Note on labeling: the running text on the same page refers to these panels as "figure 5.1a" and "figure 5.1b," while the panels themselves are lettered A, B, and C.

**What it demonstrates.** The relative width of the top and bottom of the hourglass is the whole variable. Promise a wider audience in the opening than the resolution delivers and readers feel cheated; promise less than you deliver and the readers who would have wanted the story never come; match the two and the compact set up in the opening is honored.

If a writer must err, Schimel advises erring wide: an oversold opening can still "filter down quickly," while an opening framed too narrowly loses readers immediately and, once lost, they don't come back (p. 46).

*Positioning statements: pawn-pushes versus queen-launches (§5.7).* Schimel offers a chess analogy for how an opening sentence can be simultaneously modest in appearance and doing real structural work. A "pawn push" is an undramatic first sentence that is nonetheless carefully positioned to set up what follows; a "queen launch" dives straight into the story's center. Which to use depends on the paper's overall structure:

> "When you write a straight OCAR story, as is common for specialist journals, you can use a pawn push — an opening that unfolds for a patient audience. If you're writing for *Nature* or the National Institutes of Health, however, you are likely using an ABDCE or LDR structure that starts with action, so you had better launch a queen." (p. 47)

#### Figure 5.2 (p. 48)

**Caption.** Figure 5.2. A pawn push versus a queen launch. A queen launch isn't possible in chess, but it is in writing.

**What it shows.** Two black-and-white photographs stacked vertically, each showing the same wooden folding chessboard resting on a wood-grain table, photographed from above and slightly from the near side so the board recedes toward the top of the frame. The hinge seam of the folding board runs horizontally across the middle of each board, between the fourth and fifth ranks. Dark pieces are on the far side of each board, light pieces on the near side. Each photograph carries a white rectangular label box overlapping its upper-left corner, in sans-serif type. There is no other text, no arrows, and no annotation within the images themselves.

**Top photograph — label box: "A. A Pawn Push."**
The dark side is in the full starting position: eight pieces on the far back rank (rook, knight, bishop, queen, king, bishop, knight, rook) and eight pawns on the rank in front of them. On the near side, the light back rank is also complete, all eight pieces in place. The light pawn rank has seven pawns and one gap — the fifth file from the left of the frame is empty. That pawn stands alone in the middle of the board, two squares forward on the same file, on a light square immediately on the near side of the hinge seam. Every other square between the two armies is empty. This is the position after a single opening move of a center pawn two squares.

**Bottom photograph — label box: "B. A Queen Launch."**
The dark side is again in the full starting position, eight pieces and eight pawns. On the near side, all eight light pawns are still on their home rank, untouched. The light back rank has a gap: the fourth square from the left of the frame is empty, and the pieces around it read rook, knight, bishop, [empty], king, bishop, knight, rook. The missing piece stands alone in the middle of the board, on a light square immediately on the near side of the hinge seam — the same central square the pawn occupies in the top photograph. It is visibly taller than that pawn, with a stacked, tiered body and a crowned, coronet-shaped top: it is the light queen. It has reached that square with no pawn having moved, so no legal sequence of chess moves produced this position.

**What it demonstrates.** Set side by side, the two photographs contrast the piece you lead with. The pawn push commits the smallest piece on the board to the center and lets everything else follow; the queen launch puts the most powerful piece in the center on the first move, past a wall of pawns that has not moved at all. The second position is impossible under the rules of chess and is shown as impossible — nothing has cleared a path for the queen — which is the figure's point: writing has no such rule, and an opening can lead with its strongest piece.

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 5.1* (p. 36), from Schimel's own synthesis paper on soil nitrogen cycling:

> "Since the late 1800s, N mineralization has been the perceived center point of the soil N cycle and the process that controls N availability to plants." (p. 36)

Analysis: the word *perceived* is "distinctive and unusual" and signals that the paper will challenge that perception; the phrase "since the late 1800s" signals a historical framing (p. 36).

*Example 5.2* (p. 36), from a study on folic acid supplementation and childhood asthma:

> "Current public health guidelines in the United States, the United Kingdom, and Australia recommend that women consume a supplemental dose of 400 μg of folic acid per day in the month preceding and during the first trimester of pregnancy to reduce the risk of neural tube defects in children." (p. 36)

Analysis: the sentence itself doesn't state the challenge, but combined with the paper's title (which names childhood asthma), the reader can infer the whole story — that folic acid supplementation may increase asthma risk (p. 36).

*Example 5.3* (p. 37), from a geomorphology paper on river incision:

> "The topography of mountainous landscapes is created by the interaction of rock uplift and erosion. River incision into bedrock is the key erosional process that controls the rate of landscape response to changes in rock uplift rate and climate." (p. 37)

Analysis: this opening needed two sentences. The first frames a broadly interesting focus (mountain topography) and introduces erosion as a character; the second narrows to the specific focus, river incision. Schimel calls the first sentence an "initial 'positioning sentence' from which they could launch to make their specific point" (p. 37).

*Example 5.4* (p. 37), from a materials chemistry paper on conjugated polymers, given as first and last sentence of the opening paragraph:

> "Conjugated polymers are novel materials that combine the optoelectronic properties of semiconductors with the mechanical properties and processing advantages of plastics. . . . Thus, conjugated polymers offer the possibility for use in devices such as plastic LEDs, photovoltaics, transistors, and in completely new applications such as flexible displays." (p. 37)

Analysis: the first sentence frames the abstract topic; the last sentence grounds it in concrete applications. From the gap between them, the reader can infer that the rest of the paper concerns perfecting the polymers for those devices (p. 37).

*Example 5.5* (p. 38), Schimel's own paper on methane flux from Arctic tundra, offered as a worked case of misdirection — first paragraph plus the first sentence of the second paragraph:

> "Plants are a critical control of CH4 dynamics in wetland ecosystems. They supply C [carbon] to the soil methanogenic community both through production of soil organic matter, and as fresh exudates and residues. Fresh plant material may be an important CH4 precursor even in an organic matter–rich peat soil. Strong correlations between net primary productivity and system-level CH4 fluxes across a wide range of ecosystems highlight the importance of plant C inputs.
>
> Vascular plants, however, also transport CH4 out of soil and sediment, effectively bypassing the aerobic zone of CH4 oxidation." (p. 38)

Analysis: the first paragraph sets up plants controlling methane by feeding carbon to methanogens, leading readers to assume that is the whole story. The second paragraph opens with a different mechanism — plant transport of methane — which "cuts the readers adrift," leaving them unsure whether the first paragraph was a false lead being contradicted or whether a second, additional mechanism is being introduced (p. 38). Schimel supplies the fix, an unnumbered revision of the same opening's first sentence:

> "Plants control CH4 dynamics in wetland ecosystems by two mechanisms. The first is to supply C to the soil methanogenic community . . ." (p. 39)

He explains that this "almost trivial change" would have told readers up front that both mechanisms were coming, so the second paragraph would read as completing the direction rather than reversing it (p. 39).

*Example 5.6* (p. 39), offered as a case of no direction:

> "In meiosis, genes that are always transmitted together are described as showing 'linkage.' Linkage, however, can be incomplete, due to the exchange of segments of DNA when chromosomes are paired. This incomplete linkage can lead to the creation of new pairings of alleles, creating new lineages with distinct sets of traits." (p. 39)

Analysis: Schimel poses three unrelated possibilities the paper could be about — sex chromosome evolution in guppies, Tay-Sachs distribution among Louisiana Cajuns, or potato blight ecology — and notes the opening gives no basis for choosing among them; it explains a schema (basic eukaryotic genetics) that the target readers already hold rather than pointing anywhere (p. 39). This is described as a common flaw of developing scholars, who are still assimilating the material into their own schemas and so linger on it rather than assuming readers already have it. Schimel's fix for a draft that opens this way: figure out where the real story starts and delete everything before it, citing a "well-known author" at a writers' conference who said he sometimes deletes several chapters to reach where the story begins (pp. 39–40).

*Example 5.7* (p. 40), a specialist-journal opening on marine microbial culturing:

> "Epifluorescence microscopy and direct viable counting methods have shown that only 0.01 to 0.1% of all the microbial cells from marine environments form colonies on standard agar plates. Much of the discrepancy between direct counts and plate counts has been explained by measurements of microbial diversity that employed 16S rRNA gene sequencing without cultivation. The present consensus is that many of the most abundant marine microbial groups are not yet cultivated." (p. 40)

Analysis: this makes the point — that the relevant organisms haven't been cultured on standard agar — that is fundamental to the story and foreshadows that the authors will grow new organisms on nonstandard agar; its characters (counting/culturing methods, marine microbes) are ones environmental microbiologists already care about (p. 40).

*Example 5.8* (pp. 40–41), the same underlying fact opened for *Science*, a generalist journal, by Farooq Azam and Alexandra Worden:

> "Antonie van Leeuwenhoek (1632–1723), the first observer of bacteria, would be surprised that over 99% of microbes in the sea remained unseen until after Viking Lander (1976) set out to seek microbial life on Mars." (pp. 40–41)

Analysis: Schimel reads this against the SUCCES elements — it is simple, unexpected (searching Mars for life while missing 99 percent of Earth's own), concrete and credible (specific names and dates), and emotional (curiosity and amazement at 300 years of limited discovery). He judges it "a powerful start for a *Science* paper" and notes it would likely make readers of a specialist microbial ecology journal uncomfortable, since it makes a point that community already knows and "launches the story so flashily that it would stick out" (p. 41).

*The redwood-fog proposal pair (p. 41).* Schimel describes a project studying coastal redwood forests dependent on summer fog, submitted as similar proposals to two different funders: NSF's Ecosystem Science Program and a management-focused agency he labels, for the exercise, CalEPA. Each got a different opening.

*Example 5.9* (for NSF):

> "The influence of fog on ecological and hydrological processes in coastal zones has long intrigued scientists." (p. 41)

*Example 5.10* (for CalEPA):

> "California's coastal forests are among its most distinctive and treasured natural resources." (p. 41)

Analysis: Schimel imagines each reviewer's reaction. An NSF reviewer reading Example 5.9 might think "I never thought about fog being that important in ecology; I should read further" (p. 42), while a CalEPA reviewer reading the same sentence might dismiss it as "Ivory Tower academe that is irrelevant to my mission; I can ignore this one" (p. 42). The responses reverse for Example 5.10: an NSF reviewer sees "regional interest and an environmental protection focus — this isn't NSF science," while the CalEPA reviewer thinks "I'd better read further to find out how this research may help me" protect a resource CalEPA is responsible for (p. 42). Schimel's conclusion: "An effective first sentence might open the door to funding. An ineffective one could close it" (p. 42).

*Example 5.11* (pp. 42–43), the two-step opening from a paper by Schimel's doctoral student Mike Weintraub on decomposition controls in Arctic tundra soils — narrow work, wide opening:

> "The Arctic has become a focus of attention because global warming is expected to be the most severe at extreme latitudes. The thick organic soils of the tundra contain large stocks of carbon (C), and these soils may act as either a source or a sink for atmospheric carbon dioxide (CO2). It has been suggested that as the climate warms, increased organic matter decomposition will release CO2 to the atmosphere, contributing to warming and creating a positive feedback that results in further increases in atmospheric CO2. Alternatively, it has been argued that increased decomposition will release bound nitrogen (N) and other nutrients in the soil and thereby enhance plant growth, since plant growth is nutrient-limited in arctic tundra. Increased plant growth would allow the tundra to be a sink for atmospheric C because plant material has a wider C/N ratio than soil organic matter. Thus, the direction the C balance of the arctic will shift with warming is unclear and depends on interactions between soil C and N cycling that we still do not understand in the tundra." (pp. 42–43)

Analysis: Weintraub works down in scale from the global carbon cycle to the specific issue (soil C and N cycling interactions), reached only at the paragraph's end. He wrote for an audience of global-change scientists rather than the roughly dozen tundra soil ecologists worldwide. The broad framing won the paper the 2003 Arctic Consortium of the United States award for "Best Student Paper in Interdisciplinary Arctic Science" — a result, Schimel says, "of effective storytelling, rather than inherently interdisciplinary measurements" (p. 43).

*Example 5.12* (p. 43), the opening of a proposal Schimel wrote on plant succession and tannin effects on soil microbes in Alaskan floodplain forests, using the same two-step strategy for an ecology review panel:

> "Succession has been a central theme in ecological research for almost a hundred years. Two questions have directed much of that research:
> What causes the shifts in communities?
> How do ecological processes change as a result of these community shifts?
> These questions are linked through a feedback loop: plants affect soil processes which in turn affect plant community structure." (p. 43)

Analysis: although soil microbes were the story's central characters, Schimel deliberately withheld them from the first sentences because he knew the ecology-program reviewers were more likely to be plant ecologists than microbial ecologists. The opening engages their interest in plant succession before transitioning to the actual topic, tannin effects on soil microbes (pp. 43–44).

*Example 5.13* (p. 44), from another Azam paper, given as a contrast in register to Example 5.8, targeting marine ecologists rather than a generalist *Science* readership:

> "Larry Pomeroy's seminal paper revolutionized our concepts of the ocean's food web by proposing that microorganisms mediate a large fraction of the energy flow in pelagic marine ecosystems. Before 1974, bacteria and protozoa were not included as significant components of food web models. Pomeroy argued forcefully that heterotrophic microorganisms, the 'unseen strands in the ocean's food web,' must be incorporated into ecosystem models." (p. 44)

Analysis: this uses more technical language than Example 5.8 and assumes a schema (how energy flows through the marine food web) that a physicist, for instance, would lack. Schimel reads it as engaging SUCCES elements even so: concrete (dates, direct attribution to Pomeroy), emotional (words like *revolutionized* and *argued forcefully*), and using contrast (before/after 1974). He notes that naming "Larry Pomeroy's seminal paper" in the first sentence functions as "the secret password" that admits readers who already know that paper while not actively courting the wider readership *Science* would reach (pp. 44–45).

*Example 5.14* (p. 46), a worked case for the hourglass/width-matching rule, with two alternative resolutions:

> Opening: "The Arctic is important in the global climate system because tundra soils store a large amount of carbon that may be released to the atmosphere as CO2. An important recent discovery is that wintertime CO2 fluxes from soil are large." (p. 46)
>
> Resolution 1: "Developing a reliable model of CO2 fluxes in the Arctic therefore requires a better model of winter C cycling processes." (p. 46)
>
> Resolution 2: "In the arctic tundra, microbial community composition changes little through the winter." (p. 46)

Analysis: Resolution 1 matches the opening's width — the story promised (wintertime tundra CO2 fluxes and global climate) is the story delivered. Resolution 2 would leave readers dissatisfied: the paper turns out to be about soil microbial communities, which Schimel calls "a bait and switch that wastes readers' time" (p. 46). He gives the reverse failure — narrow opening, wide story — as an unnumbered example: if the paper were actually about modeling CO2 fluxes (Resolution 2's territory) but opened with "Bacteria living in tundra soils are well acclimated to surviving the cold conditions of the Arctic winter," it would promise a story about bacterial physiology and turn off readers interested in the global carbon cycle (p. 46). He then revisits Example 5.11 (Weintraub) as a case of erring correctly on the wide side: the broad opening drew in readers across microbial ecology to global biogeochemistry, and the paper was well cited across that range, even though the first paragraph narrows quickly to carbon-nitrogen interactions specifically (p. 46).

*Chess analogy and the OCAR/pawn-push, ABDCE-LDR/queen-launch pairing (pp. 46–47).* Schimel revisits Example 5.3 (Sklar and Dietrich on river incision) as a "pawn push": an undramatic but carefully designed first sentence that positions "erosion" in the power position at the sentence's end, preparing for the queen-launch-adjacent narrowing that follows. He contrasts this with Example 5.8 (Azam and Worden), which "launched a queen" immediately with van Leeuwenhoek and the Viking Lander (p. 47). He closes the chapter by reconciling Zinsser's claim that the first sentence is the most important in the piece with a student's complaint that most opening sentences are platitudes: both are right, because most openings are unnecessary pawn pushes done poorly, while a skilled pawn push — like a chess master's — is the deliberate first step of a strategy. He ends by quoting Aristotle: "Well begun is half done" (p. 47).

**Terms introduced.**

- **Power position.** The opening's leverage from readers' strong, lasting first impressions: "making the beginning of a paper a 'power position'" (p. 35).
- **Misdirection.** An opening that sets up one story and then the paper switches direction without warning (§5.2.1, pp. 38–39).
- **No direction.** An opening that explains background/schema material the target reader already holds, without indicating where the paper is going (§5.2.2, p. 39).
- **Two-step opening.** An opening structure for a broad audience: engage with an issue the target readers care about, then modulate to the paper's actual, narrower focus, in exactly two moves — "like the dance, it must be quick" (§5.4, p. 44).
- **Positioning sentence / positioning statement.** A first sentence that looks modest but is deliberately placed to launch the sentences that follow, as opposed to a platitude (pp. 37, 46–47).
- **The hourglass (opening/resolution width-matching).** The requirement that the breadth of the opening's promise equal the breadth of the paper's resolution; mismatches produce overpromising (opening wider than resolution) or underpromising (resolution wider than opening) (§5.6, pp. 45–46; Figure 5.1).
- **Pawn-push.** An undramatic, patient opening move — appropriate to a straight OCAR structure — that is nonetheless carefully positioned by a skilled writer (§5.7, pp. 46–47; Figure 5.2).
- **Queen-launch.** An opening that dives directly into the center of the story, appropriate to ABDCE or LDR structures that start with action (§5.7, pp. 46–47; Figure 5.2).

Terms used in this chapter but defined elsewhere in the book — SUCCES, schema, OCAR, ABDCE, LDR — are applied to the openings above without being redefined here.

**Connections.** Builds directly on the book's earlier treatment of story elements (SUCCES, schema) and paper structures (OCAR, ABDCE, LDR), which this chapter applies to real openings rather than redefines. The pawn-push/queen-launch pairing with OCAR versus ABDCE/LDR (p. 47) presumes those structures are already established. The chapter's hourglass width-matching rule (§5.6) extends a structural image (top/bottom of the hourglass) used elsewhere in the book for the paper as a whole. The chapter's own exercises (5.1–5.3) ask readers to apply its opening analysis to published papers, to their own writing-group drafts, and to two supplied passages, carrying its method forward into practice.

**EXERCISES**

5.1. **Analyze published papers.** Evaluate the openings of the papers you are analyzing. Did they do a good job of identifying the larger issue? What style of opening did they use? Was it a pawn push or a queen launch? Did they dive straight in, targeting a narrow audience, or did they use a two-step approach to engage a wider audience? (p. 47)

5.2. **Write a short article.** Evaluate the opening of your short piece and those of your writing group members. Who is the intended audience? Was the opening effective? If not, can you rewrite it to make it so? Is the opening a pawn push or a queen launch? If it was a pawn push, could you write it as a queen launch? (p. 49)

5.3. **Revise the following to make them more effective openings.** Make the direction clearer and more engaging: (p. 49)

> A. "The rates of all chemical reactions increase with temperature. This phenomenon grows directly from physical chemistry's transition state theory and the Arrhenius equation. However, respiration in soil doesn't always appear to follow this pattern. Some studies have shown no respiration response to increasing temperature, while a few have even reported a negative response." (p. 49)

> B. "Chemotherapy is a dominant treatment approach for many types of cancer, and with the development of new targeted-delivery systems has the potential to become even more widespread and efficacious. A common constraint to effective chemotherapy is, however, patient resistance to the treatments. Such resistance is often closely associated with the activity of the enzyme γ-glutamyl transpeptidase (GGT), which acts to increase intracellular concentrations of glutathione and thereby block the apoptotic cascade in tumor cells. Inhibiting GGT before chemotherapy would therefore reduce tumor cell resistance and increase treatment effectiveness." (p. 49)


### Chapter 6 — The Funnel: Connecting O and C

> "Our task, your task . . . is to try to connect the dots." — Donald Rumsfeld (epigraph, p. 50)

**Claim.** The body of an Introduction — the material between the opening and the challenge — must connect the two by narrowing from a large problem to a specific, answerable question. This narrowing forms the funnel of the story hourglass, and its job is to frame the knowledge gap: to establish what is not known, and why filling that gap matters to the large problem stated in the opening.

**Mechanism.** Framing the knowledge gap taps the U (unexpectedness) and E (emotion) elements of the SUCCES stickiness formula (p. 50): naming a hole in the wall of knowledge creates unexpectedness ("I didn't realize that we didn't know that!"), and closing on a question creates curiosity, which the paper then satisfies. In an OCAR structure the background material that frames the gap flows seamlessly out of the opening, as an extension of introducing the problem and the characters — which is why Schimel does not treat it as its own section (OCAR rather than OBCAR, p. 50). In an ABDCE structure, by contrast, the writer must back up after the initial action to fill in this background before moving into the development, making it a distinct story element.

Schimel gives a stepwise test for whether the body is doing its job: at each step, is the text developing the logical connection to the next step in the funnel, rather than "telling us everything you know about a topic" (p. 53)? The chapter also gives a diagnostic for the most common Introduction failure — offering a solution before the problem is defined, which it calls the "bizzwidget problem" (p. 54–55) — and a diagnostic for distinguishing an Introduction from a literature review (p. 55–56):

- A literature review builds a wall — it describes knowledge.
- An Introduction describes the hole in that wall — it describes ignorance.
- The test: when you describe something known, do you use it to identify the boundary of that knowledge? If so, you are writing an Introduction. If not, you are writing a literature review.
- A second, more mechanical flag: check where citations sit in a sentence. "Smith (2003) found X" makes the researcher the subject and signals a data dump. "X occurs (Smith 2003)" makes the finding the subject, which is almost always what the reader needs.

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 6.1 — the funnel at work (p. 51–53).* Schimel reproduces, with reference numbers removed, the Introduction of a *Nature* paper on the rate constant of the reaction between methane and the hydroxyl radical (OH), citing it as "an important paper in atmospheric chemistry" and "an extreme example of narrowing the funnel" because it opens at the scale of global warming and closes on a single chemical reaction. He numbers key points in curly brackets. The full extracted passage (p. 51–52):

> {1} Of all the trace tropospheric species (that is, excluding H₂O and CO₂) methane contributes most to the infrared heating of the atmosphere. {2} Methane is also the most abundant hydrocarbon in the troposphere where it modulates the concentration of the OH free radical and serves as a source of CO. {3} Transport of methane to the stratosphere provides a termination step, via the Cl + CH₄ reaction, for the chlorine-catalyzed destruction of ozone. The oxidation of methane in the stratosphere is an important source of water vapour in this region. During the past decade the abundance of methane in the troposphere has been increasing at a rate between 16 and 13 parts per 10⁹ volume (p.p.b.v.) per year. {4} The total input and the identities and strengths of the different atmospheric methane sources are not clearly defined. {5} To understand the atmospheric effects of methane, and possibly to regulate it, we need these parameters. {6} At present, the total flux of methane into the atmosphere is estimated from the measured steady-state abundance and the known removal rate of methane. It has been generally accepted that the only process by which methane is chemically degraded in the troposphere is the reaction with OH. {7} Therefore, the rate coefficient, k₁, for the reaction OH + CH₄ → CH₃ + H₂O (1) is important in estimating the total flux of methane. The other loss processes, which are expected to be minor pathways, are surface deposition and reaction with Cl atoms in the lower stratosphere and upper troposphere. {8} A close examination of the available data shows that only in three investigations was k₁ measured below 298 K, the temperature region most important to the atmosphere. Only Davis et al. measured k₁ down to 240 K. Reaction 1 is slow. Therefore, at low temperatures, the presence of reactive impurities and occurrence of secondary reactions in laboratory systems can result in an overestimate of k₁. {9} We studied reaction 1 using an experimental method in which secondary chemistry could be minimized and the systematic errors reduced.

Schimel's annotation of each numbered point (p. 52–53):

1. This is the opening, framing a story about atmospheric methane and the greenhouse effect. It "reaches for a wide audience — it includes anyone interested in global warming, which definitely includes *Nature* editors and readers."
2. The second sentence introduces OH, "the other critical character in this story." By saying CH₄ "modulates" OH without explaining OH, the authors assume the reader already knows why OH matters — "a necessary weakness in a paper this short."
3. This adds background on why CH₄ matters in the global system. Because the main story line runs from CH₄ to OH, this material "may seem out of place," returning to the opening's point about CH₄'s importance — evidence that this passage runs an ABDCE structure: the first two sentences form the A, and this section backs up to fill in the B.
4. This is "a critical statement in laying the base of the knowledge gap": total input and source strengths "are not clearly defined." The paper is going to define them more clearly.
5. This establishes the importance of the research — filling the gap helps manage sources and sinks and mitigate CH₄'s role in global warming.
6. Another critical point in establishing the gap: sources of CH₄ are hard to measure directly, but the major sink — reaction with OH — is not, so flux can be estimated from the loss rate. This brings OH, "a central character," back into play.
7. Here the authors narrow all the way to the molecular scale: to know total CH₄ flux we need the rate of its reaction with OH, which means we need the rate constant for that reaction.
8. Here they finish defining the knowledge gap: only three studies measured k₁ at realistic (below 298 K) temperatures, and only one measured it as low as 240 K. "We need better measurements of k₁ at realistic temperatures." Schimel notes the authors "only tell us what we know to define the limits of that knowledge, rather than for its own sake."
9. This is the statement of the challenge, and Schimel calls it "a dud": after clearly framing the gap, the authors write only "We studied Reaction 1 . . . ," leaving the actual question — "What is the value of k₁?" — implicit. He suggests it would have been more concrete as "We measured the value of k₁ at temperatures down to 230 K using an experimental . . ." He draws the lesson that "you don't need to be perfect to be successful. This was an important paper."

Schimel notes this was a *Nature* paper, so it had to narrow quickly with broad strokes; papers in specialist journals have more room to narrow "more gently and thoroughly" and most writers will not face as extreme a scale jump (global to molecular). The stepwise narrowing process, however, is the same: develop the logical connections between each step rather than reciting everything known about the topic (p. 53).

*The "bizzwidget problem" (p. 54–55).* Schimel names the failure of pitching a solution before establishing the problem after a door-to-door salesman scenario:

> "Hi, ma'am, I'm selling the new Buzco Bizzwidget. The Bizzwidget is the most amazing tool you've ever seen — why, I don't know how you've ever lived without it! So here, let me show you some of the wonderful things it does."

He explains that the audience ("ma'am") is already trying to get rid of the salesman because she has not yet been shown she has a problem the product solves. The strategy only works on people already in the "Bizzwidget community." He then gives the corrected version:

> "Hi, ma'am — have you experienced problem X? You have? Do you have a solution? You don't? Well I do — let me show it to you; we call it the Buzco Bizzwidget."

This version opens with a concern many people share (defining the audience in the opening), shows why a Bizzwidget is needed (the body), and only then introduces the product (the challenge) — engaging curiosity ("do you have a solution? how does it work?") and staying concrete.

*Example 6.2 (p. 55).* Schimel gives a science-writing instance of the same failure:

> "Addressing complex interactions among chemistry, physics, and biology in climate systems requires an interdisciplinary approach. We propose to address this challenge by using Complex Systems Modeling Theory (CSMT). CSMT has been used in chemical systems to model molecular reaction mechanisms and in cell biology to model physiological pathways. It has been used . . ."

He explains this "assumes that we know the problem that CSMT is the solution to and so doesn't define it." It never describes the complex interactions, how other approaches have struggled with them, what CSMT actually is, or why it beats alternatives. The reader may learn later in the paper that CSMT solves a problem worth caring about, "but unless our neighbor already has told us about CSMT, we've probably closed the door on this one. To sell us a solution, first sell us a problem."

*Literature-review sentence patterns (p. 56).* To illustrate the citation-placement flag for a data dump versus a synthesized Introduction, Schimel contrasts:

> "Smith (2003) found X"

against

> "X occurs (Smith 2003)"

He explains the former "tells a story about Smith and what she did," the latter "about nature and how it works," and that the important information is almost always what Smith found, not that Smith found it. He then gives the phrasing to use when highlighting a live disagreement between researchers:

> "Although Smith (2003) reported X, Jones (2005) found Y."

and, where one researcher is challenging an accepted dogma:

> "While most reports suggest X (e.g., Smith, 2003, Xu 2004), Jones (2005) found the opposite, arguing . . ."

and, where two camps each have multiple supporting papers, the recommended condensed synthesis:

> "There is still uncertainty about the nature of X, with some reports suggesting it is Y (Smith 2003, Xu 2004) and others suggesting it is Z (Arif 2005, Masukawa 2006)."

Schimel adds that the "Smith found X" pattern is common — "I do it all the time in my early drafts" — but signals the material hasn't yet been synthesized and needs at least one more major revision (p. 56).

**Exercises.**

1. **6.1. Analyze published papers.** Go back to the papers you've been analyzing. Look at their Introductions and determine whether they frame the knowledge gap effectively. Does the Introduction have a clean funnel that flows from the opening problem to the specific questions? (p. 57).
2. **6.2. Write a short article.** Look at your short article and those of your group. Evaluate the funnel part of the Introduction — does it frame the knowledge gap? If not, revise it so that it does. (p. 57).

**Terms introduced.**

- **The funnel.** The narrowing portion of the Introduction, between the opening and the challenge, that leads readers from the general to the specific while framing the knowledge gap (p. 50).
- **Knowledge gap.** The specific thing not yet known that the paper's background material establishes and that the challenge will address; framing it well requires being concrete about what specifically is unknown, not asserting broadly that "little is known" (p. 50, 54).
- **The bizzwidget problem.** Offering a solution before the problem that motivates it has been defined, named after a door-to-door-salesman scenario (p. 54–55).
- **Literature review vs. Introduction (the wall/hole distinction).** A literature review "builds a solid wall — describing knowledge"; an Introduction "focuses on the hole in that wall — describing ignorance" (p. 56).

**Connections.** Depends on Chapter 4's definition of the OCAR and ABDCE story structures, which this chapter uses to explain why the background material sits inside the Introduction in one structure and forms a separate story element in the other. Depends on Chapter 3's SUCCES stickiness formula, whose U and E elements this chapter invokes to explain why framing a knowledge gap works. Depends on Chapter 5's treatment of the opening, since this chapter's funnel begins at the opening and the width-matching logic of Chapter 5's hourglass (opening width matched to resolution width) carries over to how far the funnel must narrow. Feeds forward into later treatment of the challenge and into proposal-writing chapters, since the chapter notes that most proposals use an ABDCE-like structure rather than the simple OCAR funnel used by most papers.


### Chapter 7 — The Challenge

**Claim.** The challenge is the part of a paper or proposal that states the specific knowledge the author hopes to gain, built from the question that drove the research. A challenge that states objectives — what data will be collected — without stating the question — what knowledge will be gained — is weak science and weak storytelling, because it leaves the reader to reconstruct the point of the work.

**Mechanism.** Schimel opens the chapter (p. 58) by glossing the Latin root of "science": "Scientia (Latin): knowledge." From there: "In the challenge, you describe the specific knowledge you hope to gain. This starts with the question that drove you to do the research. You did the work to discover the answer. From the question, we sometimes formulate a hypothesis and we usually state specific objectives, which describe the information we will present. Some authors only pose the question, whereas others do all three, offering a question, framing it into a hypothesis, and then describing specific research goals. Each approach has its place, but the question is the core of it all. If you don't have a question, you are not doing good science. If readers can't tell what it is, you are not writing good science." (p. 58)

*7.1 Questions versus hypotheses.* Some hold that without a hypothesis a study isn't science, a view Schimel traces to "Popper's argument that a theory is only scientific if it can be falsified" (p. 58, citing K. Popper, *The Logic of Scientific Discovery*, 1934/1959). He counters this with the Vaghjiani and Ravishankara rate-constant study introduced in chapter 6: they "had no hypothesis about the rate constant for the reaction between methane and hydroxyl radicals; they did have a question, 'what is the rate constant?'" (p. 59). Different fields have different conventions — Schimel notes that proposals to the National Science Foundation's Biology Directorate demand hypotheses while Geosciences reviewers are "slightly baffled by biologists' obsession with them" (p. 59). His resolution: "a hypothesis merely takes your question and makes into a falsifiable prediction. The question, defining the knowledge gap, is still the key" (p. 59). Hypotheses are useful for testing whether a relationship exists (his example: "hummingbirds prefer red flowers," which is testable and falsifiable), but not every good question reduces to one — Vaghjiani and Ravishankara knew methane reacts with hydroxyl radicals and has some rate constant; there was nothing to hypothesize, only something to measure. Schimel declines to give universal advice on question versus hypothesis, stating researchers must "know the culture of your field," but insists "the question comes first and must be clear" (p. 59).

*7.2 Questions versus objectives.* Many authors frame their challenge as "Our objectives were" rather than "Our question was" — describing the information they will collect rather than the knowledge they hope to gain, on the assumption that the question is already obvious from the introduction. Schimel states they "are almost always wrong" (p. 59). Leaving the question implicit and jumping to data-collection goals "violates principle 1 — it is the author's job to make the reader's job easy" (p. 59). It also fails to engage SUCCES: readers left to guess at the underlying question will instead wonder about the author's motives — "Is it possible that you were just aping experiments published by others without a real question of your own?" — which Schimel characterizes as "not questions about your science but about you and your motivation," carrying the implicit accusation "why are you wasting my time with this?" (p. 59).

*7.3 What comes after stating the question.* After the question, a good challenge briefly lays out the research approach — the specific objectives and information to be generated (his examples: mechanistic toxicology versus epidemiology for a carcinogenicity study; culturing bacteria versus sequencing environmental DNA; gas-phase versus aqueous measurement of a rate constant). Stated objectives function as "a map that helps readers assess the rest of the paper" against three questions: "Were your methods appropriate for the question? Did you learn what you hoped to? Where are the remaining gaps?" (p. 60). Some papers also preview the Conclusions directly after the Introduction, typically beginning "In this paper we show . . ." Schimel calls this "telegraphed OCAR" — more frontloaded than ordinary OCAR structure but less frontloaded than ABDCE or LDR — and notes it is common in the biomedical literature, which he suspects evolved the form "to help readers screen papers quickly" (p. 60).

*7.4 Good challenges.* In specialist-journal papers, Schimel states that a good challenge "almost always condenses conceptually to 'to learn X, we did Y'" (p. 60) — the question paired with the approach to answering it.

*7.5 Bad challenges.* An unclear challenge leaves readers adrift: "If they don't know where the paper is going, how will they know whether they got there?" (p. 63). The most common failure is stating "we did Y" and omitting "to learn X" — focusing on objectives because the author knows the material so well the question feels obvious, when in fact "No reader ... knows as much about your work as you do, and your thinking is rarely completely apparent" (p. 63). Schimel's conclusion: "The challenge is too important to leave it to assumption, hope, or chance. You must make the question clear. If you fail to do this, your papers will lack power, and your proposals will likely lack funding" (p. 63). The chapter's closing statement of the rule: "A good challenge must define not only the data you collect but the knowledge you hope to gain. ... Remember that the critical part of the challenge is not 'we did Y' but 'to learn X'" (p. 65).

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 7.1* (p. 60), from a paper on captivity and hippocampal development in mountain chickadees:

> Our goal in this study was twofold. First, we tested whether an animal's physical environment would affect hippocampal attributes. Specifically, we tested whether food-caching mountain chickadees (*Poecile gambeli*) housed in captivity differed in hippocampal volume, hippocampal neuron number and neuronal density as compared with fully developed wild-caught conspecifics. We predicted that captivity, with reduced environmental complexity and restricted memory-based experiences (compared with memory-based experiences afforded in the natural environment), would reduce hippocampal volume, neuron number and, potentially, neuron density.

(Citation: L. D. LaDage, T. C. Roth, R. A. Fox, and V. V. Pravosudov, "Effects of Captivity and Memory-Based Experiences on the Hippocampus in Mountain Chickadees," *Behavioral Neuroscience* 123 (2009): 284–91.)

Schimel calls this "long and technical, without linguistic flourish" but effective (p. 61): the authors connect a narrow question about captive birds' hippocampuses to the larger, widely interesting problem of controls on brain development, then go further by framing "a hypothesis that defines their measurements and the data that would falsify or support it." He adds that the narrow question alone "might seem more a candidate for a Golden Fleece award for pointless Ivory Tower science than for the Faculty of 1000 website of interesting and important papers, which is where I found it" (p. 61).

*Example 7.2* (p. 61), from a paper on the chemical signal underlying light perception in marine invertebrate photoreceptors:

> Despite the tantalizing evidence for DAG and/or its downstream products in visual transduction and the synergistic role of calcium, in no instance has application of such chemical stimuli fully reproduced the remarkable size and speed of the photocurrent. This may imply that yet another signal may be missing from the proposed schemes. In other systems PIP₂ has been shown to possess signaling functions of its own, independent from those of its hydrolysis products. . . . These observations prompted the conjecture that in microvillar photoreceptors PIP₂ may help keep the channels closed and its hydrolysis could promote their opening. In the present report, we examined the consequences of manipulating PIP₂ on membrane currents and light responsiveness in isolated photoreceptors from *Pecten* and *Lima*.

(Citation: M. Del Pilar Gomez and E. Nasi, "A Direct Signaling Role for Phosphatidylinositol 4,5-Bisphosphate (PIP2) in the Visual Excitation Process of Microvillar Receptors," *Journal of Biological Chemistry* 280 (2005): 16784–89.)

Schimel: the authors "clearly lay out the problem — DAG can't explain existing observations," hypothesize the PIP₂ mechanism, and describe the test — "to learn X, we did Y." He notes that although the venue is a specialist journal, the authors "use strong words to engage curiosity and attention, notably *tantalizing* and *remarkable*" (p. 61).

*Example 7.3* (p. 62), a telegraphed-OCAR example from a paper on tRNA gene transcription and chromosome breakage:

> Although Rrm3 and Tof1 might collaborate to set the rate of fork progression through tRNA genes, there is no evidence that this rate is subject to physiological regulation by mechanisms that determine the balance of activity between Rrm3 and Tof1. Furthermore, tRNA gene regulation is not known to be tied to fluctuations in the rate of DNA replication other than by mechanisms that generally tune the proliferation rate to nutrient availability and overall cellular fitness for growth and division. However, there is evidence that the appearance of double strand breaks (DSBs) in DNA can trigger repression of the tRNA genes. Specifically, tRNA gene transcription is actively repressed in cells treated with UV light or the alkylating agent methane methylsulfonate (MMS). The secondary DNA lesions generated in UV-irradiated and MMS-treated cells include DSBs. This fact, as well as the observation that tRNA gene repression requires a protein kinase (CK2) previously implicated in adaptation to chromosome breakage, led us to hypothesize that the canonical DNA-damage response (DDR) checkpoint pathway controls tRNA gene transcription. The aim of the present study was to test this hypothesis. In pursuing this aim, we discovered an unexpected system of regulation in which checkpoint proteins specialized for signaling replication stress repress tRNA gene transcription during normal proliferation. These proteins also convey repressive signals to tRNA genes in cells exposed to genotoxins that cause replication interference. These data provide the first evidence that the fork-pausing activity of tRNA genes is regulated by the checkpoint system that has evolved to control replication.

(Citation: V. C. Nguyen, B. W. Clelland, D. J. Hockman, S. L. Kujat-Choy, H. E. Mewhort, and M. C. Schultz, "Replication Stress Checkpoint Signaling Controls tRNA Gene Transcription," *Nature Structural and Molecular Biology* 17 (2010): 976–81.)

Schimel: the authors synthesize existing knowledge into a clear hypothesis and "could have stopped by saying that their aim was to test that hypothesis," which alone "still would have been a fine challenge." Instead they go on to state that testing it led them to discover a new regulatory system, which "both prepared you for the story to come and raised the curiosity factor to intensify the challenge" (p. 62).

*Example 7.4* (p. 63), from an LDR-structured physical chemistry paper in *Science* that uses no subheadings, ending the lead with a question:

> However, three decades of work in the gas phase have explored how the specifics of the forces between atoms involved in isolated chemical reactions determine the final energy partitioning as the reaction moves from the transition state. Is knowledge of these specifics completely immaterial to reaction dynamics in solution?

(Citation: A. C. Moskun, A. E. Jailaubekov, S. E. Bradforth, G. H. Tao, and R. M. Stratt, "Rotational Coherence and a Sudden Breakdown in Linear Response Seen in Room-Temperature Liquids," *Science* 311 (2008): 1907–11.)

Schimel: the authors "define the knowledge gap and ask an interesting question that confronts the reader." He reads it as engaging SUCCES: "it draws on simple by asking a clean and straightforward question, and it draws on unexpected and emotion by using the highly charged phrase 'completely immaterial' to challenge decades of high-quality science" (p. 63).

*Example 7.5* (p. 63–64), a weak challenge from an immunology paper on anergic T-cells:

> Some T-cells may be anergic — that is, unable to proliferate after being restimulated with an antigen. Some anergic T-cells are unable to link to the T-cell–antigen presenting cell (APC) interface. Here we examined the structural characteristics of anergic mouse T-cells and we tested their functional response to being rechallenged with antigen-loaded APCs. (p. 63–64)

Schimel's diagnosis: "the authors tell us which data they will collect, but they don't specify the knowledge gap." He infers the implicit question — what makes a T-cell anergic, and by extension why immune systems break down — but says "that thought process is opaque — the implicit question is too deeply buried" (p. 64). His revision:

> "To determine what causes mouse T-cells to be anergic, we evaluated the structural characteristics of T-cells and how they responded to being rechallenged with antigen-loaded APCs." (p. 64)

He comments: "That simple addition would have clarified the question and made it stronger" (p. 64).

*Example 7.6* (p. 64), a weak challenge from an environmental-science paper on hares and shrubland structure:

> We evaluated the possibility that hares influence the structure of shrublands by acting as keystone herbivores that maintain gaps between the shrubs and so influence the competitive interactions of plants recruiting into those gaps. (p. 64)

Schimel: "the question itself is unclear. What do hares do that influences shrubland structure and competitive interactions? What does the investigator hope to learn?" (p. 64). He identifies three implied hypotheses buried in the sentence: "(a) hares control plant community structure, (b) they maintain gaps between shrubs, and (c) they influence competitive interactions within the gaps" (p. 64), and argues these should be made explicit and concrete, with the challenge also describing the experiment that will test them. His revision:

> "We hypothesized that hares control the structure of shrublands by foraging on shrub seedlings in the gaps between mature plants. If true, hares act as keystone herbivores by maintaining these gaps, in which grasses can outcompete shrub seedlings. We tested this hypothesis by following hare movement to determine where they eat and by analyzing their feces to determine what they eat." (p. 64)

Schimel: this version "states the hypothesis and suggests its larger implications — hares are keystone species that have a disproportionate impact on ecosystem structure and function," and identifies the experimental approach (p. 64).

*Example 7.7* (p. 65), from a grassland-ecology/global-change paper with two clear goals presented in the wrong order:

> The study had two goals. First, we aimed to constrain our estimates of grassland plant production by comparing measurements based on two techniques: maximum biomass at the end of the season and periodic measurements of photosynthesis. Second, we examined the response of grass growth to a combination of elevated CO₂ and increased temperatures, conditions that are expected to occur with climate warming. (p. 65)

Schimel's diagnosis: the goals are ordered "chronologically" rather than intellectually — the methods-validation goal comes first and the climate-response question, "the To learn Y part of the challenge," comes second, making it "seem subordinate to the methods comparison" (p. 65). He notes the language reinforces the imbalance: the first goal uses strong verbs ("constrain," "compare"), the second a weak one ("examine"). His revision reorders and re-weights the goals:

> "The primary goal of this study was to evaluate how grass growth responds to a combination of elevated CO₂ and increased temperatures, conditions that are expected to occur with climate warming. To validate our plant growth measures, we used two approaches to estimate plant production: maximum biomass at the end of the season and periodic measurements of photosynthesis." (p. 65)

Schimel: "Now this seems like a more interesting paper — the real question is clear and is unmistakably about a topic that is broadly interesting and relevant" (p. 65).

**EXERCISES** (p. 66):

1. **7.1 Analyze published papers.** "Evaluate the challenge of each paper. Does it clearly frame the question? Could you write it better? If so, how?"
2. **7.2 Write a short article.** "In your short article, did you clearly pose the question? If not, rewrite the piece to do so."

**Terms introduced.**

- **The challenge.** The story element in which the author "describe[s] the specific knowledge you hope to gain," built from the driving question and, optionally, a hypothesis and/or stated objectives (p. 58).
- **Question versus hypothesis versus objectives.** Schimel distinguishes three things a challenge can state: the *question* (the knowledge gap itself, always required), the *hypothesis* (the question turned into "a falsifiable prediction," used to test whether a relationship exists), and *objectives* (the specific data-collection goals or information to be generated) (p. 59–60).
- **Telegraphed OCAR.** A variant of OCAR story structure, common in biomedical literature, in which the challenge is followed by a brief preview of the Conclusions ("In this paper we show . . .") immediately after the Introduction — "more frontloaded than normal OCAR, but not as much as ABDCE or LDR" (p. 60).
- **"To learn X, we did Y."** Schimel's compressed formula for what a good challenge conceptually reduces to: the question paired with the method of answering it (p. 60, restated p. 65).

**Connections.** Builds directly on chapter 6 (the funnel introduction), reusing the Vaghjiani and Ravishankara rate-constant study as its opening example and continuing the OCAR/ABDCE/LDR story-structure vocabulary established there. Invokes principle 1 (making the reader's job easy) and the SUCCES framework, both defined earlier in the book, as the standards against which challenges are judged. Sets up chapter 8 (the Action, per Schimel's OCAR terminology), which follows the challenge in the paper's narrative arc.


### Chapter 8 — Action

**Claim.** Action is the main body of a paper's story — everything in OCAR between the challenge and the resolution, comprising the Materials and Methods, the Results, and most of the Discussion (in a proposal, the description of what the author intends to do). A paper can present experiments and data without embedding them in a story, but readers and journals are equally free to find the result aimless, incoherent, and dull; the chapter's governing instruction is to remember the last S in SUCCES — story (p. 67).

**Mechanism.**

*Two parts.* The action sections split into describing what was done (Materials and Methods) and what came of it (Results and Discussion) (p. 68).

*8.1 Methods.* Because most readers won't repeat the work themselves, what they need from Methods is an overview of the information sought and the approach used to get it, not the full protocol up front — many journals now place Methods and Materials at the end of the paper for this reason. The recommended structure is lead/development (LD): an initial overview for all readers, followed by the details for those who need them (p. 68).

A second technique for easing Methods is tapping into established schemas — naming a well-known method so the reader's existing knowledge of it does the work:

> We measured protein by the Lowry approach.
> We measured microbial biomass by the chloroform slurry approach as described by Fierer and Schimel (2003).
> We amplified DNA by hot-start PCR. (p. 69)

From that base the author can highlight only the differences from the standard approach: "We measured protein by a modification of the Lowry protein assay, in which sodium citrate, instead of sodium tartrate, is used in reagent A." (p. 69)

*8.2 Results and Discussion.*

*8.2.1 To separate, or not to separate.* However a paper organizes Results and Discussion, the choice should rest on two core principles:

1. Make the reader's job easy (principle no. 1): present results and interpretations in a way that best develops the story.
2. Readers must be able to distinguish what you found from what you think. (p. 70)

The book extends the second principle into a three-part taxonomy of material in a paper:

1. Data: your actual results.
2. Inference: the clear and robust interpretations of the data that almost any practitioner in the field would draw — sometimes so obvious that they get treated as data themselves.
3. Interpretation: your thoughts, hypotheses, and speculation about what the results may mean for the larger problem identified. (p. 70)

A paper should address each of these somewhere; balancing the mix and deciding how to structure the presentation is described as "a balancing act between the author's taste and confidence and those of the reviewers and editors" (p. 70).

Whether fields separate Results from Discussion or combine them tracks two things: paper length (short papers tend to integrate) and the nature of the data. Fields reporting straight data (biology, environmental science) lean toward separate Results and Discussion sections. Theoretical fields (physics), where raw data are processed through theory and models before being reported, tend toward integrated presentation because there is less separation between result and interpretation (pp. 70–71). Observational/analytical fields such as geophysics also favor combined presentation, describing a data set and its meaning before moving to the next line of evidence; modeling papers similarly break into modules rather than separating all results from discussion (p. 71). Even experimental fields integrate when experiments are sequential, each built on the last — such work calls for a chronological narrative organized by experiment, each section carrying both Results and Discussion (p. 71).

Combined Results and Discussion also suits work with strong integration of data and inference. Example 8.4 shows the four subsection headings a paper on a *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* transmembrane protein used for its combined Results and Discussion:

> The B and C Domains of *M. tuberculosis* Rv0899 Form Two Independently Structured Modules
> Three-Dimensional Structure of the B Domain of Rv0899
> Implications for the Biological Function of Rv0899
> Implications for the Organization of Rv0899 in the Mycobacterial Membranes (p. 71)

The authors could have split the first two sections into Results and the last two into Discussion, but didn't, because the first sections contain analyses of the proteins' biochemical functions that were necessary to discuss but didn't develop the larger story about pathogenicity (p. 71). Example 8.5 is the paragraph from the first section that weaves data and inference together:

> Notably, the spectra of Rv0899-B (Figure 3B, red) and Rv0899-C (Figure 3B, blue) form perfect complementary subsets of the spectrum from Rv0899-BC (Figure 3A), spanning both domains, with the exception of some peaks from residues in the BC connecting region. This demonstrates that the B and C domains constitute independently folded modules, as suggested by sequence homology. The resonance line widths measured in the three spectra are very similar, further indicating that all three polypeptides exist as monomeric species in solution. Since the line widths are not appreciably larger in the spectra of the BC polypeptide, the B and C domains may be significantly dynamically decoupled. (pp. 71–72)

The passage distinguishes results from interpretation by presenting the data first and using words like *demonstrates* and *indicating* to flag inference. The book contrasts this with how the same content would read as pure Discussion (Example 8.6):

> The B and C domains constitute independently folded modules, as indicated by the fact that their spectra form perfect complementary subsets of the spectrum from Rv0899-BC. (p. 72)

Schimel characterizes 8.6 as pure Discussion: though it refers to the data, it does so to support an argument, and the data come after the argument. "Whenever the data come first, it feels like you are drawing your interpretations from them, and so are obeying Lamott's dictum of 'listen to your characters.' When conclusions come before the data, it feels like you are imposing plot." (p. 72) The point generalizes: distinguishing results from discussion of them is always essential, but physically separating them is not — each discipline has its own norms, and the author chooses what best fits the field and the story (p. 72).

*8.2.2 Choosing data to present.* The central decision in Results is not how to present data but which data to present, since not all collected data is needed to build the story — a decision the book calls both intellectually and emotionally difficult ("Deleting data from a paper hurts — it feels like saying your work was wasted. But it wasn't," p. 72). The worked example is a former student's paper on seasonal nitrogen forms in Alaskan tundra soils. The student spent months developing an HPLC method for soil amino acids, generating and processing the data, and found that (1) all the amino acids followed the same trends through the year, and (2) a quick colorimetric measure of total free amino acids (TFAA) was a better estimate of bioavailable nitrogen than the full HPLC method — so the HPLC work was not wasted, since running both in parallel was the only way to learn this (pp. 72–73). Rather than present all the amino acid data, or simply state "all amino acids followed the same trends across time (data not shown)," the student collapsed years of work into subordinate panels of one figure:

#### Figure 8.1 (p. 73)

**Caption.** Figure 8.1. Seasonal dynamics of total free amino acids (TFAA), NH4+, and NO3− (a), and of the five most common amino acids (b) in tussock tundra soil. This presents the data for one of four soils analyzed.
Copyright © 2005 Springer. Reprinted with permission.

**What it shows.** Two stacked line-and-symbol panels sharing one x axis and one y-axis title. The y-axis title, set vertically and centered across both panels, is "µg-N/g dry soil." The shared x axis is a set of dates, rotated at roughly 45 degrees: 01-Jun-00, 11-Jun-00, 21-Jun-00, 01-Jul-00, 11-Jul-00, 21-Jul-00, 31-Jul-00, 10-Aug-00, 20-Aug-00, 30-Aug-00. Every plotted point carries a vertical error bar.

Panel (a), marked "(a)" at the upper right, is titled "Tussock" in bold at the upper left inside the plot frame. Its y axis is labeled 0, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25. Its legend, inside the panel at upper right, has three entries: a filled circle on a solid line, "NH4"; an open downward triangle on a dashed line, "TFAA"; a filled square on a dashed line, "NO3." The first plotted point in the panel sits at 11-Jun-00; the 01-Jun-00 tick carries no data. The TFAA trace starts near 17 there, falls to about 12 at 01-Jul-00, spikes to about 27.5 at 11-Jul-00 (the tallest point in the panel), drops to about 6.5 at 21-Jul-00, then runs at or just below 0 across the remaining dates. The NO3 trace begins near 3.5, stays near 3 to 01-Jul-00, peaks at about 5 at 11-Jul-00, then declines through roughly 2.5, 1.5, 2.5, 1, 1.5, 0.5 and ends near 2. The NH4 trace sits at essentially 0 for the first two plotted dates, rises to about 3.5 at 11-Jul-00, returns to 0, and then holds just above 0 (roughly 0.2 to 0.5) for the rest of the season, ending near 0.5.

Panel (b), marked "(b)" at the upper right, has its own y axis labeled 0, 2, 4, 6. Its legend, inside the panel at right, has five entries: a filled downward triangle on a dash-dot line, "Arg"; an open square on a dash-dot-dot line, "Gly"; a filled diamond on a solid line, "His"; an open upward triangle on a dashed line, "Ser"; a filled circle on a long-dash line, "Thr." At the first date the five sit between about 1.2 and 3.5 — Ser about 3.4, Arg about 3.1, Thr about 2.1, His about 1.7, Gly about 1.2. All five converge and then spike together at 11-Jul-00: Ser rises to about 7, Arg to about 5, Gly to about 4.3, Thr to about 2.9, His to about 1.9. After the spike every trace collapses toward 0 and all five run at or near 0 from 31-Jul-00 through 20-Aug-00, with a slight lift at the final date (Ser to about 0.4).

**What it demonstrates.** All the amino acids move together, so the whole HPLC data set can be collapsed to a single summary variable: panel (b) shows five amino acids tracing the same seasonal shape — flat, one sharp July spike, then near zero — which is the visual form of "they covaried." Panel (a) then carries the argument, putting the collapsed TFAA total alongside the two mineral N forms and showing that TFAA is by far the largest pool of plant-available N through the early season and at the spike.

The decision to cut so drastically was possible because the story was known: seasonal patterns of bioavailable N, not amino acids specifically (p. 74). The book invokes the writers' maxim: "Writers talk about having to 'murder your darlings' — the parts you adore but that don't contribute sufficiently to the overall piece. It's good advice for scientists, too." (p. 74) Separately, it recommends archiving all collected data electronically even when trimming it from the paper itself, since others may need it for modeling, meta-analysis, or other postpublication use, and funders and journals are increasingly requiring archiving (p. 74).

*8.2.3 Presenting data.* Having chosen which results to present, the author must present them so the reader understands why a given datum matters, not just its value — stating "X was 42" leaves the reader wondering what it means. Most results call for the same LD structure used for Methods: frame the major point or pattern first, then supply detail. "Don't present all the details and then synopsize them, or worse, present them without synopsizing or synthesizing at all. Without a framework, readers struggle with details." (p. 74)

Example 8.7, from a paper on the factors regulating soil bacterial diversity across North America, illustrates the LD approach:

> Soil bacterial diversity varied across ecosystem types (figure 8.2). Of all soil and site variables examined, soil pH was, by far, the best predictor of soil bacterial diversity (r² = 0.58, P < 0.0001) with the lowest levels of diversity observed in acid soils. Because soils with pH levels > 8.5 are rare, it is not clear whether the relationship between bacterial diversity is truly unimodal, as indicated in figure 8.2, or whether diversity simply plateaus in soils with near-neutral pHs. Likewise, because our fingerprinting method underestimates total bacterial diversity, we cannot predict how the absolute diversity of bacteria changes across the pH gradient. When we compare paired sampling locations with similar vegetation and climate but very different soil pHs, we find evidence for the strong correlation between bacterial diversity and soil pH at the local scale. For example, two deciduous forest soils collected in the Duke Forest, North Carolina, showed that the soil with the higher pH (Site DF2, pH = 6.8) had an estimated bacterial richness 60% higher than the more acidic soil (Site DF3, pH = 5.1). Similarly for two tropical forest soils collected <1 km apart in the Peruvian Amazon, the soil with the higher pH (Site PE8, pH = 5.5) had an estimated bacterial richness 26% higher than the more acidic soil (Site PE7, pH = 4.1). (pp. 74–75)

#### Figure 8.2 (p. 75)

**Caption.** Figure 8.2. The relationship between soil pH and bacterial and phylotype richness, defined as the number of unique phylotypes. Symbols correspond to general ecosystem categories, and labels denote individual soils.
Copyright © 2006 National Academy of Sciences, U.S.A. Reprinted with permission.

**What it shows.** A single scatterplot in a rectangular frame, with a heavy smooth curve fitted through the cloud. The x axis is titled "Soil pH" and is ticked and labeled 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9. The y axis is titled on two stacked lines, "Bacterial diversity" above "Phylotype richness" (the second line set in bold), and is ticked 0 at the origin then 20, 25, 30, 35, 40, 45 — the scale is broken between 0 and 20.

Symbols come in four shapes at several gray levels, standing for ecosystem categories: circles (light gray and dark gray), squares (light gray), upward triangles (light gray and dark gray), and open white downward triangles. There is no legend keying shape or shade to a named category; the caption alone says symbols correspond to general ecosystem categories.

Every point is labeled with a soil code. Reading from the low-pH, low-richness end upward and to the right: PE2, PE3, PE1 (open downward triangles below richness 20, at pH about 3.6, 4.0, 4.3); CF2 (light circle, pH about 3.6, richness 20); PE6 (open down triangle, pH about 3.7, about 21); PE4 (open down triangle, pH about 4.4, about 20.5); BF2 and CF1 (two overlapping light circles, pH about 3.6–3.7, about 24); PE7 with a second open down triangle just above it (pH about 4.3, about 23 and 23.5); BB1 (light circle, pH about 4.4, about 24); HF1 (light circle, pH about 4.4, about 23); DF3 (light circle, pH about 5.0, about 24); HF2 and BF1 (two light circles, pH about 4.2, about 26); PE9 and LQ3 (open down triangles, pH about 4.6, about 26.5 and 27); CC1 (dark circle, pH about 6.0, about 26); MP2 (light circle, pH about 4.4, about 29); BB2 (light circle, pH about 4.6, about 29); LQ2 (open down triangle, pH about 4.9, about 29); SK1 (light square with a small open down triangle at its lower-right corner) and PE8 (that small triangle), pH about 5.3, about 29; a dark circle just left of SK1 (pH about 5.2, about 29); TL2 (light square, pH about 4.2, about 30); SK2 (light square, pH about 5.6, about 30); H13 (open down triangle, pH about 4.6, about 31); AR1 (open down triangle, pH about 5.6, about 31); HJ1 (light circle, pH about 5.4, about 31.5); EC1 (open down triangle, pH about 6.4, about 31.5); TL1 (light square, pH about 4.4, about 32); SN1 (light circle, pH about 4.6, about 32); GB3 (light triangle, pH about 6.8, about 32); CA3 (dark triangle, pH about 6.7, about 31.5); MP1 (dark circle, pH about 4.3, about 33); LQ1 (open down triangle, pH about 4.6, about 33); DF1 (light circle, pH about 5.2, about 33.8); CL1 (light circle, pH about 5.6, about 32.5); CM1 (dark circle, pH about 7.8, about 39); HJ2 (light circle, pH about 5.3, about 39); IE3 (dark circle) and IT1 (light circle) overlapping at pH about 5.4, about 40; BZ1 (light square, pH about 5.0, about 40); BZ3 (light square, pH about 5.2, about 41); GB2 (light triangle, pH about 7.2, about 40); SA3 (dark triangle, pH about 6.8, about 40); GB4 (light triangle, pH about 6.5, about 40.5 — the code GB4 appears twice, this one and a dark triangle above it in the 43.5 cluster); SR1 (a light triangle and a dark circle overlapping on the fitted curve, pH about 6.9, about 39); HI2 (open down triangle, pH about 6.3, about 39); KP1 (dark circle, pH about 6.2, about 40); IT2 (light circle, pH about 5.4, about 42); IE5 (dark circle, pH about 5.6, about 43); VC1 (light triangle, pH about 5.5, about 44); VC2 (dark triangle, pH about 5.8, about 44); HI1 (open down triangle, pH about 6.2, about 43.5); MT2, GB4, SR3 (dark triangles clustered at pH about 6.5–6.7, about 43.5); GB6 (dark triangle, pH about 6.9, about 44); SB1 (dark triangle, pH about 7.6, about 43.5); SV1, GB5, SV4, SV2 (dark triangles, pH about 7.8–8.1, about 43.5–44.5); SV3 (dark triangle, pH about 8.0, about 41); SP1 (dark circle, pH about 6.2, about 47); SA1 (light triangle, pH about 6.5, about 46.5).

The fitted curve is unimodal: it rises steeply from about richness 19 at pH 3.6, passes through about 29 at pH 4.7 and about 36 at pH 5.5, flattens to a maximum near 39 between pH 6.7 and 7.0, then declines to about 33 at pH 8.9. No equation, r², or p value is printed inside the plot; the r² = 0.58, P < 0.0001 appears only in the quoted text above the figure.

**What it demonstrates.** Soil pH orders bacterial diversity across dozens of individual soils: richness climbs sharply through the acid range, peaks near neutral, and falls off again in alkaline soils. Labeling every soil puts the paired comparisons the text makes (DF2 versus DF3, PE8 versus PE7) inside the same picture that carries the overall pattern.

Schimel notes the authors open with a clear lead — diversity varied and pH drove that variation — reinforced elsewhere in the paper by showing the lack of relationship with other variables.
