After the lead, they offer caveats (whether the pH relationship is truly unimodal or a plateau; the fingerprinting method's limits) before moving into more detailed, confirming comparisons. "This was a very clear LD structure — you get the entire story in the first two sentences, even though the paragraph goes on for an additional five." (p. 76)

*8.2.4 Statistics and stories.* Statistics establish the credibility of conclusions, but "the story is not in the statistics — it is in the data themselves. When you tell the story through the lens of the statistics, by focusing on the statistical analysis rather than on the data, you steal both clarity and power from the story." (p. 76) The book illustrates with a fiction analogy: no one would write "John's affection for Jane was significantly greater than zero" — what matters is the amount, whether he likes the occasional coffee with her or is "desperately, madly, passionately in love" (p. 76). Applied to science: "warming significantly increased methane emissions (p < 0.05)" doesn't say whether the increase is a factor of 1.2 or of 12, which is what a climate model needs. The recommended alternative: "warming increased methane emissions by a factor of 3.4 (p < 0.05)." The p-value establishes that the difference isn't random variation; it says nothing about meaning (p. 76).

Figure 8.3 supplies three panels comparing difference and significance:

#### Figure 8.3 (p. 77)

**Caption.** Figure 8.3. Difference versus significance in data presentation.

**What it shows.** Three stacked bar-chart panels, A, B, and C from top to bottom, sharing one x axis and one y-axis title. The y-axis title, set vertically and centered on the middle panel, is "Response." Each panel holds two open, black-outlined bars with symmetric error bars. The x-axis labels, printed once below panel C, are "Control" (left bar) and "Treatment" (right bar).

Panel A, labeled "A" at the upper left inside the frame, has y ticks labeled 5, 10, 15 with unlabeled minor ticks between. Its annotation, in italics inside the panel, reads "Ratio = 2.3" over "p = 0.02". The control bar reaches about 5.5, error bars running from about 3.8 to about 7.3. The treatment bar reaches about 12.7, error bars from about 10.2 to about 15.2.

Panel B, labeled "B," has y ticks labeled 5, 10, 15 with unlabeled minor ticks. Its annotation reads "Ratio = 1.3" over "p = 0.02". The control bar reaches about 5.5 with short error bars from about 5.0 to about 6.0. The treatment bar reaches about 7.0 with short error bars from about 6.5 to about 7.5.

Panel C, labeled "C," has y ticks labeled 0, 5, 10, 15 with unlabeled minor ticks; the 0 tick is the baseline at the foot of the whole figure. Its annotation reads "Ratio = 2.3" over "p = 0.07". The control bar reaches about 5.5 with long error bars from about 1.7 to about 9.3. The treatment bar reaches about 12.7 with long error bars from about 9.2 to about 16.0.

**What it demonstrates.** Panels A and C plot identical bar heights — the same 2.3-fold difference between control and treatment — but different variability, so A comes in at p = 0.02 and C at p = 0.07. Panel B holds the p value of A while the difference shrinks to 1.3-fold. Size of effect and statistical significance are separate facts about a data set: a large difference can fail a significance threshold and a small one clear it, so a description reporting only whether p cleared the threshold throws the difference away.

In panel A the treatment is 2.3 times the control, unquestionably significant. Panel B has the same p-value (p = 0.02) but a smaller difference. Describing both panels as "The treatment significantly increased the response (p = 0.02)" would be true of both but would erase that A shows a strong effect and B a weak one. Schimel's preferred descriptions: for A, "The treatment increased the response by a factor of 2.3 (p = 0.02)"; for B, "The treatment increased the response by only 30 percent, but this increase was statistically significant (p = 0.02)." (p. 76)

Panel C carries the same factor-of-2.3 difference as panel A, but with more variable data, so the statistics are weaker — in this case, p = 0.07, above the conventional 0.05 threshold. The book flags several problems with the common description "There was no significant effect of the treatment (p > 0.05)": readers would infer no difference when in fact the difference was 2.3-fold and never "the same"; at p = 0.07 the probability the effect was due to treatment is still greater than 90 percent, so such a statement risks a Type II error — rejecting a real effect; and stating only whether a result is "significant" conflates result and interpretation, since the F and p values are the *results* and deciding whether the test counts as significant is *interpretation*, with any specific significance threshold being "an arbitrary choice with no fundamental basis in either science or statistics" (p. 78). Recommended treatments of panel C: "The response in the treatment was 2.3 times higher than in the control (p = 0.07)" or, more conservatively, "The response in the treatment was 2.3 times higher than in the control, but the difference was only significant at p = 0.07." (p. 78)

Figure 8.4 provides a further case — a relationship where p = 0.001 but R² = 0.10:

#### Figure 8.4 (p. 78)

**Caption.** Figure 8.4. Describing the strength of a relationship: fit versus significance.

**What it shows.** A single scatterplot in a rectangular frame with a straight regression line drawn through it. The x axis is ticked and labeled 0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100; the y axis is ticked and labeled 0, 100, 200, 300. Neither axis carries a title or units. An annotation block sits inside the plot at upper left, on three lines: "Slope = 0.79", "R² = 0.10", "p = 0.001".

Roughly 120 small filled black circles fill the frame, spread broadly at every x value: near x = 10 the points run from about 10 to about 210; near x = 50, from about 40 to about 250; near x = 80, from about 55 to about 285; near x = 95, from about 110 to about 325. A few points sit almost on the baseline (about 10 at x ≈ 2, x ≈ 9, and x ≈ 15; about 20 at x ≈ 30 and x ≈ 60). The highest points reach about 330 at x ≈ 80 and x ≈ 92. The regression line rises from about y = 110 at x = 0 to about y = 190 at x = 100.

**What it demonstrates.** The same relationship reads as strong or weak depending on which number describes it. The p value of 0.001 says the positive slope is highly significant; the R² of 0.10 says x accounts for only a tenth of the variation in y — and the scatter shows the second reading, since the vertical spread at any x dwarfs the line's total rise across the whole x range. Significance is about whether the slope differs from zero; fit is about how much of the variation the relationship explains.

Read through statistics alone, this looks strong ("there is a highly significant positive slope"); read through the data, it looks weak, since x explains only 10 percent of the variance in y. Both readings are correct in their own way — "this is a robust but weak relationship." The recommended description states both: "the relationship between x and y is weak (R² = 0.10) but statistically significant (p = 0.001)." (p. 79)

*8.3 Discussion.* The Discussion is where the author answers the questions posed in the challenge and shows the paper's contribution to the larger problem framed in the opening. Because disciplines differ widely in their norms here, the book declines to give formulaic advice, calling a good Discussion "the critical act of creativity in science that no book can teach" (p. 79). The first decision is which internal story structure to use, since the Discussion must build toward the paper's resolution while also forming a coherent story on its own. Two options: an OCAR structure, opening by reminding readers of the challenge and question and working toward the resolution; or an LDR structure, opening by framing the conclusion — what the paper showed — and using the rest of the Discussion to support that argument, building to the overall resolution (p. 79).

Four examples give opening and closing sentences of published Discussions. The first two use OCAR, laying out the issue without stating the conclusion up front. Example 8.8, from organic chemistry:

> Opening: It is well-known that factors such as the nature of the nucleophile, solvent, and leaving group directly affect the rate of the bimolecular nucleophilic substitution (SN2) reactions; yet, in the case of carbanions, little has been documented with absolute rate constants. . . .
> Closing: Photoinduced decarboxylation of suitable substituted carbanions provides a route for the formation of substituted cycloalkanes that proceeds in high yields in nonhydroxylic solvents and with good leaving groups such as bromide and iodide. (p. 79)

Example 8.9, on how poliovirus enters target cells despite having no membrane-fusing capsule:

> Opening: The extracellular forms of viruses face formidable challenges. The virion itself must be sufficiently stable to protect the viral genome during the passage from host to host and cell to cell, and yet, upon reaching the target cell and encountering the appropriate trigger, the virion must initiate programmed steps that result in the release of the viral genome into the appropriate compartment of the cell. For nonenveloped viruses, the conceptually simple mechanism of membrane fusion is not an option. . . .
> Closing: After binding the cell surface, the virus is internalized through a clathrin-, caveolin-, and flotillin-independent, but actin- and tyrosine kinase–dependent, pathway. After internalization (and only after internalization), the virus releases its RNA rapidly from vesicles that are located within 100–200 nm of the plasma membrane without requiring endocytic acidification or microtubule-dependent transport. Our results have settled the long-lasting debate of whether PV [poliovirus] directly breaks the plasma membrane barrier or relies on endocytosis to deliver its genome into the cell. These results have also opened interesting questions for this important virus that await further investigation, including what characteristic of these endocytic vesicles near the plasma membrane triggers RNA release; and after release near the cell surface, how is the released RNA transported to replication sites. (p. 80)

This Discussion "starts by reiterating the problem and reenergizing curiosity before going on to nailing down the answer with the resolution" (p. 80).

The next two examples use LDR, opening with the conclusion. Example 8.10, on a nontoxic inhibitor of gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase (GGT) for cancer therapy:

> Opening: We have identified a novel class of GGT inhibitors that are not glutamine analogues. Kinetic studies of the lead compound OU749 revealed that the mechanism of inhibition was uncompetitive relative to the γ-glutamyl substrate, indicating that the inhibitor bound the enzyme-substrate complex. In contrast to competitive inhibitors, which lose potency as substrate concentration builds, uncompetitive inhibitors become more potent as the substrate concentration rises in an inhibited open system. . . .
> Closing: Development of less toxic GGT inhibitors, such as OU749, holds great promise for enhanced cancer therapy. (pp. 80–81)

The opening sentence addresses the paper's specific challenge and names the key result — a new class of inhibitors; the Discussion elaborates the group's properties and benefits, building to a resolution that closes the circle back to the opening's concern with new chemotherapeutic agents (p. 81).

Example 8.11, from theoretical physics on string theory D-branes:

> Opening: In this paper, we have shown how to construct various D-branes in the type IIB plane-wave background that preserve half the dynamical supersymmetries of the background. . . .
> Closing: The connection of the instantonic branes we have constructed with instantonic branes in AdS5 × S5 is more obscure. Understanding this could lead to an understanding of the relation between the D-instanton and instanton effects in the dual Yang-Mills field theory. It would also be interesting to understand the effect of the D-instanton on the plane-wave dynamics. Finally, one should be able to analyse the D-instanton contributions by considering the effects of the R4 and related terms in the effective low energy IIB action in this background. (p. 81)

Schimel writes of this example: "To be honest, I haven't a clue what any of this means, yet the story structure and the message are clear. That is a testament to the writing." (p. 81) This Discussion identifies the paper's main contribution first, develops and elaborates it, then resolves by framing new questions growing from the work.

Both OCAR and LDR are presented as viable Discussion structures, each giving "a coherent structure that allows you to develop a clear and compelling story." LDR is described as more common, and some books call it the "right" way to write a Discussion, but the book characterizes that as "a rule and so is malleable" — the author chooses the structure that fits the story (p. 81).

**Evidence and examples.**

- Examples 8.1–8.3: three versions of a Methods opening for the same enzyme-inactivation procedure, used to compare no-overview, detail-only, and LD-structured presentation (pp. 68–69).

  > Example 8.1 — Enzyme inactivation associated with 3-HPAA metabolism was measured by the method of Turman et al. (2008). (p. 68)

  > Example 8.2 — PGHS-1 or PGHS-2 was incubated with 25 μM 3-HPAA. When oxygen uptake was complete, arachidonic acid (25 μM) was added, and the maximal rate was determined as described above and normalized to the DMSO control. The concentration dependence of PGHS-2 inactivation was analyzed in a similar manner with varying concentrations of 3-HPAA (from 10 nM to 25 μM). (pp. 68–69)

  > Example 8.3 — To characterize the extent of enzyme inactivation associated with 3-HPAA metabolism, PGHS-1 or PGHS-2 was incubated with 25 μM 3-HPAA. When oxygen uptake was complete, arachidonic acid (25 μM) was added, and the maximal rate was determined as described above and normalized to the DMSO control. The concentration dependence of PGHS-2 inactivation was analyzed in a similar manner with varying concentrations of 3-HPAA (from 10 nM to 25 μM). (p. 69)

  Schimel judges 8.1 "truly miserable" (no method detail, forces the reader to track down the original paper); 8.2 "imperfect" (goes straight to detail with no framing overview, acceptable only under a page limit); and 8.3 "the best," the version Turman et al. actually used, adding a brief overview before the detail (p. 69).
- Schema-naming examples for Methods: the Lowry, chloroform slurry, and hot-start PCR lines quoted above, plus the modified-Lowry sentence (p. 69).
- Example 8.4: the four subsection headings of a combined Results-and-Discussion structure (p. 71).
- Example 8.5 / 8.6 pair: the same finding written as integrated data-and-inference versus as pure Discussion, contrasting where the data fall relative to the claim (pp. 71–72).
- Weintraub tundra-nitrogen case study and Figure 8.1: choosing to collapse years of amino-acid data into two panels once the seasonal-covariation pattern was established (pp. 72–74).
- Example 8.7 and Figure 8.2: the Fierer and Jackson soil-pH/bacterial-diversity paragraph as a model of LD structure in Results (pp. 74–76).
- Figures 8.3 and 8.4: three-panel and single-panel demonstrations of writing results by data magnitude rather than by significance alone (pp. 76–79).
- Examples 8.8–8.11: four Discussion openings/closings, two OCAR and two LDR, across organic chemistry, virology, biochemistry, and theoretical physics (pp. 79–81).

**Exercises.**

1. **8.1. Analyze published papers.** Go through the papers you have been analyzing. Are all the data they presented necessary for the story? Did the authors do a good job of structuring how they present the data — did they use an effective story structure? (p. 82).
2. **8.2. Write a short article.** Revisit your short article. Did you use an effective LD structure in presenting the results? (p. 82).

**Terms introduced.**

- **LD (lead/development) structure.** For Methods: an initial overview for all readers, followed by details for those who need them (p. 68). Applied identically to Results: frame the major point first, then supply supporting detail (p. 74).
- **Schema-tapping.** Naming an established, recognized method or technique so the reader's existing knowledge fills in the procedural detail, with the author noting only departures from the standard approach (p. 69).
- **Three types of material in a paper.** Data (actual results); Inference (interpretations of the data any practitioner in the field would draw, sometimes treated as data themselves); Interpretation (the author's own thoughts, hypotheses, and speculation about broader meaning) (p. 70).
- **"Murder your darlings."** Writers' term, applied to cutting data the author is attached to but that doesn't sufficiently serve the paper's story (p. 74).
- **Type II error.** Rejecting a real effect — the risk the book attributes to describing a p = 0.07 result as simply "no significant effect" (p. 78).
- **LDR structure (for Discussion).** Opening the Discussion by framing the conclusion — what the paper showed — then using the remainder to support that argument, building to the overall resolution; contrasted with an OCAR Discussion that opens by restating the challenge (p. 79).

**Connections.** Builds on OCAR and the SUCCES acronym established earlier in the book (last S, "story"). The LD structure applied here to Methods and to Results presentation is the same lead/development structure used elsewhere for abstracts and paragraph-level writing. The Discussion's choice between OCAR and LDR structures anticipates the book's later treatment of the paper's overall resolution and ending.


### Chapter 9 — The Resolution

**Claim.** The resolution is a power position: readers remember the last thing a paper says, so whatever occupies that spot is accepted as the take-home message whether or not the author intended it that way (p. 83). A good resolution earns that position by showing how the paper has advanced understanding of nature — closing the story's "spiral" back to the opening while showing how the starting point has moved. Anything placed in the resolution other than that new insight undercuts the paper, because readers will treat it as the most important point regardless of its actual weight (p. 83).

**Mechanism.** A good resolution steps backward through OCAR: it reiterates the Action, answers the questions raised in the Challenge, and demonstrates how those answers contribute to the larger problem raised in the Opening (p. 83). This closes the circle the paper opened.

Concluding with a genuine new question is an acceptable alternative to a flat statement, provided the question is concrete and the paper makes clear how it grew out of the reported work — not that the authors failed to answer their own question, but that they identified a new one (p. 86).

Resolutions fail in three distinct ways (p. 87–88):

1. **Weak.** The resolution synopsizes the results and asserts that they are important without clarifying how — it doesn't answer the questions posed in the challenge or synthesize the findings into knowledge (p. 88).
2. **Distracting.** The resolution includes material that belongs in the Introduction, restates textbook truisms rather than the study's own results, or — worse — introduces genuinely new information or a new direction never raised in the opening, so the paper ends with a "plot twist" instead of closing its circle (p. 90–91).
3. **Undermining your conclusions.** The resolution actively weakens what the paper just established, typically with a variant of "more research is needed to clarify our findings." This is called the most common failure and the worst one: it takes a power position and uses it against the paper's own science (p. 91).

The general fix for a bad resolution (p. 92): first cut the fluff, the distractions, and any new ideas — relocating important ones elsewhere in the paper — then condense the resolution to do three things:

1. Synopsize the key results.
2. Synthesize those results — show how they answer the question.
3. Show what this contributes to solving the larger problem.

Achieving these three "clearly and concretely" produces a resolution with maximum punch. The chapter frames the recurring failure as usually a missing **C** — concrete — in the SUCCES formula (p. 92).

For proposals (p. 92–93), the same requirement holds but is more often skipped: many proposal authors invest all their craft in the lead and then let the proposal run out on a pure LD structure — Lead, Development, no Resolution — ending as soon as the proposed experiments have been described. The fix is to budget space for a resolution paragraph that reiterates the big issue, explains how the proposed components work together to address it, and makes the final case for funding, because reviewers' judgments are often not fixed until they reach that closing paragraph (p. 93).

**Evidence and examples.** The chapter works through ten examples, numbered 9.1 through 9.9 and then 9.11 — the printed text has no Example 9.10 between 9.9 and 9.11.

The first good resolution is from a molecular biology paper on the cell-cycle inhibitor p27 (p. 84):

> {1} In conclusion, {2} our data suggest that Y-phosphorylated p27 can inhibit cyclin D-cdk4 complexes by two independent mechanisms: blocking access to the T-loop and disrupting the cdk4 active site directly. {3} Our model suggests that p27 Y phosphorylation is a molecular "switch" that would help turn cdk4 activity on or off. {4} Modulation of Y kinase activity would permit activation of preformed, inactive p27-cyclin D-cdk4 complexes by cdk7 and may be used to regulate cdk4 activity throughout the cell cycle. (Example 9.1, p. 84)

Schimel's own bracket numbers mark the moves: {1} "In conclusion" flags the resolution and orients the reader; {2} states the key result, answering the challenge's question; {3} interprets that result into a simple model — p27 phosphorylation as a molecular "switch" — which begins widening the hourglass; {4} completes the widening by returning to "the cell cycle," the same phrase that opened the paper's first sentence, closing the circle (p. 84–85).

A second good resolution, from materials science, on templated semiconducting films (p. 85):

> {1} These templated nanostructured frameworks thus hold several advantages for the design and synthesis of devices. {2} Films can be selectively deposited through solution phase routes using the chalcogenide affinity to bind to gold. {3} Furthermore, the ability to control the elemental compositions of the nanostructured films allows the band structure of the inorganic framework to be tailored for specific applications. {4} Current research is underway to create composite materials using an organic semiconductor as the structure directing agent. Such materials would make good candidates for device applications such as photovoltaics. Moreover, it is likely that these same band energy trends will hold for nontemplated versions of chalcogenide glass semiconductors synthesized using Zintl cluster precursors. {5} As a result, the data presented here provide a basis to predicatively synthesize a broad range of semiconductors with desired band properties using Zintl cluster precursors and simple solution phase methods. (Example 9.2, p. 85)

Schimel reads its five moves as: {1} a statement of the overall accomplishment, signaling the resolution; {2} the key result; {3} a first widening into a general interpretation; {4} continued widening into implications beyond the specific study; {5} the most general statement of application. Unlike Example 9.1, this one uses a full framing sentence rather than a single flag word, but performs the same functions — key result, widened hourglass, tie back to the paper's opening (p. 85–86).

**9.1.1. Concluding with a question.** Sometimes the resolution's most important discovery is a new, unanticipated question. Schimel allows this provided the question is concrete and its origin in the reported work is clear — the paper hasn't failed to answer its own question but identified a new one (p. 86).

The first such example, from arctic climate science on the 2007 Arctic sea-ice loss (p. 86–87):

> {1} There was an extraordinarily large amount of ice bottom melting in the Beaufort Sea region in the summer of 2007. Solar radiation absorbed in the upper ocean provided more than adequate heat for this melting. An increase in the open water fraction resulted in a 500% positive anomaly in solar heat input to the upper ocean, triggering an ice–albedo feedback and contributing to the accelerating ice retreat. The melting in the Beaufort Sea has elements of a classic ice–albedo feedback signature... The positive ice–albedo feedback can accelerate the observed reduction in Arctic sea ice. {2} Questions remain regarding how widespread this extreme bottom melting was, what initially triggered the increase in area of open water, and what the summer of 2007 portends for 2008 and beyond. (Example 9.3, p. 86–87)

{1} states findings and conclusions in clear, unhedged language — "no hesitation or weakness," in Schimel's phrase. {2} frames questions that reinforce and extend the conclusions rather than undermine them, engaging the reader's interest; Schimel notes even the word "portend" is chosen for its ominous charge (p. 86–87).

A second question-ending example, from astrophysics, on rotating neutron stars and accretion disks (p. 87):

> {1} Finally, while the details of the solutions that we have discussed specifically apply to the case of a rotating NS accreting from a disk fueled by a companion star, the general feature of a multiplicity of states available for a given mass inflow rate of matter can probably be generalized to other accreting systems in which recycling occurs. {2} An example is that of an accretion disk around a rotating black hole. Numerical simulations show that while a fraction of the accreting mass is ejected through a jet, another fraction... falls back into the disk, getting recycled. {3} It would be interesting to include this mass feedback process into numerical simulations of accretion disks around black holes and to investigate whether the discontinuous states and cyclic behavior might ensue in those cases as well. (Example 9.4, p. 87)

{1} opens the hourglass by suggesting the result generalizes beyond the specific system studied; {2} develops that by naming a specific analogous system, the black hole's accretion disk; {3} poses the new question growing from the work. Schimel calls this "nicely done" — the question extends the paper's accomplishment rather than undermining it (p. 87).

**9.2. Bad resolutions.** Despite their different approaches, the four good resolutions above all carry out the same core functions: identifying the main results and their implications. Bad resolutions fail those functions in one of three ways — weak, distracting, or actively undermining the paper's own conclusions (p. 87–88).

**9.2.1. Weak.** Weak resolutions synopsize the results and assert their importance without clarifying how — they don't answer the questions posed and don't synthesize the findings into knowledge. The first example is from proteomics, on hummingbirds under simulated migratory stress:

> A proteomic evaluation of hummingbirds under simulated migratory conditions revealed evidence of several stress-associated processes: protein degradation in wing muscle tissues, depletion of metabolic cofactors, and enhancement of stress-response proteins. These results suggest that changes in the hummingbird proteome may provide new insights into the complex physiology of avian systems biology. (Example 9.5, p. 88)

Schimel judges that this synopsizes the results well but then stumbles: instead of synthesizing new knowledge, it merely asserts that the work "may provide new insights . . . into avian systems biology" without saying what those insights are. He calls it "a train wreck of a resolution" — the claim of wider relevance is true but empty, since any scholar studying bird migration would already expect a hummingbird paper to be relevant; asserting relevance accomplishes nothing without the concrete substance to illustrate it (p. 88).

To fix a weak resolution like this, Schimel identifies the actual new insight:

> A proteomic evaluation of hummingbirds under simulated migratory conditions revealed evidence of several stress-associated processes: protein degradation in wing muscle tissues, depletion of metabolic cofactors, and enhancement of stress-response proteins. While hummingbirds migrate long distances over water without feeding or resting, it is physiologically stressful, and the birds' ability to manage this stress may limit the distance they can migrate. (p. 88)

This version ends with a clear conclusion about what the data mean — migrating is stressful — rather than a vague methodological gesture toward birds in general (p. 88–89). To widen the hourglass explicitly to other migratory birds, Schimel offers a further variant:

> While many birds, such as hummingbirds, migrate long distances without feeding or resting, it is physiologically stressful, and birds' ability to manage such stress may limit the distance they can migrate. (p. 89)

Adding "such as hummingbirds" marks them as an example of the larger group, and condensing "the birds'" to "birds'" shifts the reference from these specific birds to birds generally — but Schimel cautions this stretch is only valid if the Introduction already built the argument that migration is stressful in other birds (p. 89).

A second weak resolution, from medical microbiology, on the flesh-eating bacterium *Streptococcus pyogenes*:

> In summary, we show that X7 alters the expression pattern of extracellular proteases in the "flesh-eating bacterium" *Streptococcus pyogenes*, which causes necrotizing fasciitis. If the function of X7 can be fully established, it would likely deepen our understanding of this destructive disease. (Example 9.6, p. 89)

Schimel reads this as authors who knew they needed a wrap-up but produced only "a throw-away line that included a patently obvious truth and a back-handed slap at their own data" — of course understanding X7's function would deepen understanding of the disease, and reminding readers that the function isn't yet established undercuts the paper rather than closing it (p. 89). His fix:

> In summary, we show that X7 alters the expression pattern of extracellular proteases in the 'flesh-eating bacterium' Streptococcus pyogenes, which causes necrotizing fasciitis. This research may offer a route to developing therapeutic agents that would minimize tissue damage while antibiotic treatments were directly attacking the bacterium itself. (p. 89)

This version identifies the work's real conclusion — a possible route to therapeutic agents — closing the circle to the paper's opening on necrotizing fasciitis and potential therapies, while staying carefully constrained: it says only that the research "may offer" a route, not that X7 itself is the drug (p. 90).

**9.2.2. Distracting.** Some resolutions include material that belongs in the Introduction, or that restates textbook truisms rather than the study's own results. The example is from forest tree nutrition, on mycorrhizal fungi:

> The mycorrhizal fungal hyphae extending out from tree roots can comprise more than 1/3 of the total biomass of microbes in the soil. They greatly extend the absorptive surface area of the root system and enhance total nutrient uptake by the trees. Additional work, however, is required to assess how much mycorrhizal fungi enhance the uptake of organic N forms in forest soils. (Example 9.7, p. 90)

Schimel calls the first two sentences "truisms that have been known for decades — textbook material, rather than results of this particular study." The only statement about the study itself is that more work is needed to assess organic-N uptake — an instance of the undermining failure layered onto the distraction. He notes the paper did establish results, but this resolution reports none of them; "it resolves nothing and merely distracts from the story" (p. 90).

A second, subtler form of distraction is introducing genuinely new information at the resolution. The example, from savanna ecology on termite mounds:

> In arid environments such as East Africa, termites are critical "ecosystem engineers." They collect resources such as nitrogen and phosphorus from far afield and accumulate it in and near their mounds, creating nutrient hot-spots on the landscape. These hot-spots may be sites for colonization by new seedlings of both the native savanna trees and for novel invasive plant species. (Example 9.8, p. 90)

Invasive plants were never raised in the Introduction, so the resolution "goes haring off in a new direction" instead of closing the circle back to the opening (p. 90–91). Schimel allows that the idea likely occurred to the authors while writing — citing Montgomery's observation that "clear thinking can emerge from clear writing" — but the fix is to carry a late insight back into the opening and Introduction, not to leave it as a surprise at the end: "You are not writing a 'whodunit' mystery where shocking plot twists are expected. You are writing science, where such plot twists are forbidden" (p. 91). He adds a citation-based cost: points raised only in the resolution won't surface in literature searches, so the intended audience — here, plant ecologists working on invasion — will miss the finding by accident, costing the authors citations (p. 91).

**9.2.3. Undermining your conclusions.** The worst way to end a paper, and the one Schimel calls perhaps the most common, is to actively undermine the conclusions — typically some variant of "more research is needed to clarify our findings." This takes the resolution's power position and uses it against the paper's own science; the humility behind the impulse is understandable, but the resolution is not the place to air it (p. 91). The example is from a paper on a thyroid cancer therapy:

> To conclude, 3-methyl-ambrosia offers a new approach for thyroid carcinoma therapy. Our data provide evidence on safety and in vivo activity of this compound in patients with this condition, although the proof for clinical benefit remains to be established in future clinical trials. (Example 9.9, p. 91)

Schimel reads the first part as a genuine claim — a new, apparently safe and active therapy — undercut by a take-home message that amounts to "we don't know whether it really works." He calls it "an egregious example of undermining the conclusions": "Talk about destroying the story" (p. 91).

Schimel's fix reorders the same content to lead with the positive claim:

> While further clinical trials will be necessary to establish the full benefits of 3-methyl-ambrosia as a therapeutic agent, our data provide evidence that it is safe and shows in vivo activity against thyroid tumors. 3-Methyl-ambrosia therefore may offer a new approach for treating patients with thyroid carcinoma. (p. 91–92)

He notes this "says the same things as the original, but strongly and positively" — the need for further trials and the uncertain efficacy are still stated (it only "may offer" a new approach), but the sentence ends on the authors' intended message rather than on their doubt (p. 92). He points out the Example 9.6 flesh-eating-bacterium resolution undermined itself the same way, with "if the function of X7 can be fully established." He distinguishes this failure from "concluding with a question" (Examples 9.3 and 9.4): a question that grows from the work poses new questions about the world, while "more research is needed" poses a question about the paper itself and implies the authors didn't finish. Other undermining phrases he lists: "but the importance of this has yet to be assessed," "we hope that this review will stimulate further research to answer the many unanswered questions," and "this topic deserves more research" — fuzzy expressions that suggest weakness rather than stating substantive conclusions or new questions (p. 92).

**9.4. Resolutions in proposals.** Most proposal authors invest their craft in the lead, then, having described the proposed experiments, stop — running out on a pure LD (Lead, Development) structure with no resolution. Schimel calls this a mistake: reviewers have just worked through pages of dense, detailed material and are still assembling how the pieces fit together and what to write in their review, so the resolution paragraph is "your last opportunity to give them the words" — to convince a reviewer to check "excellent," or to tell a program officer "must fund" (p. 92–93). The fix is to budget space for a resolution that reiterates the big issue and explains how the proposed components work together to address it. The example is from a proposal on why nitrogen availability crashes in arctic tundra soils partway through the growing season:

> The Arctic tundra is one of the world's major stores of C and the possibility that the temperature-decomposition-CO2 flux positive feedback may accelerate climate warming is a concern in Arctic and global climate studies. The "spin-off" feedbacks via nutrient effects on vegetation change may further accelerate the climate-warming feedback. However, biogeochemical models assume that decomposition is limited by C-availability but regulated by temperature. Thus, the assumption is that if temperatures rise and the snow-free season lengthens, decomposition and CO2 release will increase dramatically. However, C cycling is N-limited in tundra, at least later in the growing season after the nutrient crash, challenging these ideas. We propose research that will span multiple scales to evaluate the mechanisms causing the "nutrient crash," how they are driven by seasonal weather patterns and plant phenology, and what the effects of the nutrient crash on C-cycling will be.
> This work will require intense mechanistic work focusing on transitions and transformations that occur over only a few weeks at most, but which have profound impacts on the tundra ecosystem. We will scale this mechanistic work to the intermediate spatial scale by doing transect measurements along the Kuparuk Basin to validate that patterns that occur locally are robust. We will scale to the whole Arctic system by integrating these mechanisms, and importantly the N-effects on decomposition, into the MEL model that is designed to explore multiple limiting resource effects on ecosystem function. As an integrated package, this research will explore how the changing seasonal pattern that drives the crash in nutrient availability in tundra soils will alter overall tundra C-cycling and its role as a source or sink of C and through this its role in the global climate system. (Example 9.11, p. 93–94)

This paragraph reiterates the entire proposal — problem, challenge, research, and how the research will solve the problem — in about half a page, which Schimel acknowledges "seems like a lot when you're struggling with a page limit." He reports that the program officer's feedback suggested this resolution barely pushed the proposal over the line into the funded category; while he can't know for certain it supplied the deciding margin, he notes a reviewer's opinion is sometimes not fixed until the very end — hence his advice to "end strong" (p. 94).

**Terms introduced.**

- **Weak resolution.** A resolution that synopsizes the results and asserts their importance without clarifying how — it neither answers the challenge's questions nor synthesizes the findings into knowledge (p. 88).
- **Distracting resolution.** A resolution that includes Introduction-level or textbook material instead of the study's own results, or that introduces genuinely new information or direction never raised in the opening — a "plot twist" the circular structure forbids (p. 90–91).
- **Undermining resolution.** A resolution that actively weakens the paper's own conclusions, typically via some form of "more research is needed" — named the most common and worst failure, because it takes the resolution's power position and turns it against the paper's own science (p. 91).
- **Concluding with a question.** An acceptable resolution strategy in place of a flat statement: ending on a new, concrete question that clearly grew out of the reported work, rather than an admission that the authors' own question went unanswered (p. 86).

**Connections.** Depends on Chapter 4's OCAR structure and its "spiral" image of a story that returns to its starting point after that point has moved (p. 29), which this chapter's good resolutions (Examples 9.1–9.4) are read against. Depends on Chapter 3's SUCCES stickiness formula: the chapter frames the recurring resolution failure as usually a missing C, concrete (p. 92). Depends on Chapter 5's hourglass image of matching the opening's breadth to the resolution's breadth, which this chapter's "widening the hourglass" language (Examples 9.1 and 9.2) applies directly. Connects to Chapter 8's treatment of LDR as a Discussion structure, since a paper's resolution is described as the close of the Discussion. Feeds forward into the book's proposal-writing material by extending the resolution requirement to proposals, diagnosing the common LD-only proposal structure as missing its resolution (§9.4).

EXERCISES (p. 94):

- **9.1. Analyze published papers** — Examine the resolutions of the papers you are evaluating. Do they effectively resolve? Do they briefly sum up the most important results? Do they answer the question? Do they close the circle by returning to the big problem identified in the opening? If not, how would you rewrite the resolution to achieve these goals?
- **9.2. Write a short article** — Do the same exercise for the short articles you and your peers are writing.


### Chapter 10 — Internal Structure

> I figured out, over and over, point A, where the chapter began, and point B, where it ended, and what needed to happen to get my people from A to B.
> — Anne Lamott, *Bird by Bird* (p. 95)

**Claim.** A paper's overall arc is built from smaller arcs nested inside it — sections, subsections, paragraphs, and sentences each form their own complete opening-challenge-action-resolution structure. Writing that lacks these nested arcs becomes an incoherent mass even when every individual sentence is clear, because coherence lives in the structure connecting sentences, not in the sentences themselves.

**Mechanism.** The chapter extends OCAR (opening, challenge, action, resolution) from the whole-paper level, where earlier chapters applied it, down through every smaller unit of the text. The Introduction opens, narrows, and resolves with the paper's overall challenge; the Materials and Methods opens with the study system, then measurements, then analysis; the Discussion opens by restating the issue, discusses evidence, and resolves with the conclusion (p. 96). Below the section level, subsections should each "package complete ideas — that is, form story arcs of their own" (p. 96): a method subsection states what information was sought and how it was obtained; a results subsection gives overview, specifics, and significance. Below that, individual paragraphs, sentences, and clauses each form their own arc, producing "a hierarchical structure, with small arcs nested within larger ones, ultimately creating the whole" (p. 96) — explicitly compared to "quarks within protons within atoms within molecules" (p. 96).

#### Figure 10.1 (p. 96)

**Caption.** Figure 10.1. A story arc.

**What it shows.** A single heavy black arc — one smooth symmetrical hump — with no box, no axis line, and no arrowheads. The arc rises from a low left endpoint, crests at a rounded peak in the middle, and falls to a matching low right endpoint; at each end the line straightens into a short leg that angles steeply down and outward, so the shape reads as a rise and a fall rather than a closed dome.

Five text labels, all outside the curve, in a sans-serif face:

- **Development: action** — centered above the crest of the arc, in roman type, the largest label together with the two at the bottom.
- **Build tension** — italic, set on two lines ("Build" / "tension"), placed at the left, level with the arc's descending left leg. The label sits in a white gap in the line: the arc's left leg is interrupted where the words fall, so the leg appears above the words and resumes below them.
- **Relieve tension** — italic, set on two lines ("Relieve" / "tension"), the mirror of the above on the right, with the same white break in the arc's right leg.
- **Opening** — roman, at the bottom left, directly below "Build tension" and below the lower stub of the left leg, which points down toward it.
- **Resolution** — roman, at the bottom right, directly below "Relieve tension" and below the lower stub of the right leg, which points down toward it.

Reading left to right along the curve: Opening at the low left → Build tension on the rise → Development: action at the top → Relieve tension on the fall → Resolution at the low right. There are no numbers, tick marks, units, or scaled axes anywhere in the figure; height on the page stands for tension and horizontal position for progress through the story, but neither is drawn as a labeled axis.

**What it demonstrates.** A story has three parts arranged as one continuous shape: it opens low, climbs through action that builds tension, and comes back down to a resolution that relieves it. The single unbroken line makes the point that these are not separate blocks but one motion, and that a story ends where it started in height — at rest — having passed through a peak.

#### Figure 10.2 (p. 96)

**Caption.** Figure 10.2. A story is a set of nested arcs.

**What it shows.** Four tiers of arcs drawn on a single common baseline, all sharing the same left and right endpoints, and distinguished from one another by line weight and by how many humps each tier is divided into. There is no axis, no scale, and no numbering; the four tiers are identified by four sans-serif labels placed at the heights of their respective arcs.

The four tiers, from largest to smallest:

1. **Paper** — one very thick arc spanning the full width of the figure, from the far left endpoint to the far right endpoint, cresting highest of all. Label "Paper" sits above its crest, near the middle of the figure.
2. **Sections** — three medium-weight arcs of equal width, laid end to end across the same span. Each one starts on the baseline where the previous one ended, so their meeting points fall at one-third and two-thirds of the width. Each crests at about two-thirds the height of the Paper arc. Label "Sections" sits at upper left, at the height of the first section arc's rising left flank.
3. **Paragraphs** — six thin arcs of equal width laid end to end across the same span, two nested inside each section arc. Their crests reach roughly half the height of a section arc, and their meeting points on the baseline fall at every one-sixth of the width, coinciding with the section boundaries at one-third and two-thirds. Label "Paragraphs" sits mid-figure at the height of the paragraph crests.
4. **Sentences** — twelve very fine broken-line arcs of equal width laid end to end across the same span, two nested inside each paragraph arc, twelve meeting points at every one-twelfth of the width. They are the lowest and smallest humps, rising only slightly above the baseline. The left half of this tier is drawn with a dashed line and the right half with a finer dotted line. Label "Sentences" sits low, at the level of the sentence humps, left of center.

Because all four tiers share the same two endpoints and the same baseline, the larger arcs cross over the smaller ones: the thick Paper arc cuts across the first section arc on the left and across the last section arc on the right, and section arcs cross the paragraph arcs beneath them. The three labels "Sections," "Paragraphs," and "Sentences" are each set in a small white gap that interrupts the lines running behind them.

Counts, exactly as drawn: 1 paper arc, 3 section arcs, 6 paragraph arcs, 12 sentence arcs — each level splitting the level above it in two.

**What it demonstrates.** A paper's story arc is not a single curve but a hierarchy of the same curve repeated at every scale. Each section is a complete arc inside the paper's arc, each paragraph a complete arc inside its section's, each sentence a complete arc inside its paragraph's — all beginning and ending on the same line, so the small arcs assemble into the large one rather than competing with it.

Creating arcs works, the chapter argues, because readers learn by placing new information into an already-built framework: each point supplies context, then information, forming a small arc that becomes the context for the next point (p. 97). The analogy given is computer programming: "Good programs are built from subroutines or objects, each of which is internally complete; they interact with others by passing specific pieces of processed information. Bad programs look like plates of spaghetti" (p. 97).

Arc structure also matters because beginnings and endings are "power positions" — they emphasize whatever occupies them, signaling importance to the reader. Without discrete arcs, readers cannot distinguish what matters more from what matters less (p. 97).

#### Figure 10.3 (p. 97)

**Caption.** Figure 10.3. Complete versus broken story arcs: beginnings and endings are power positions.

**What it shows.** Two stacked panels of equal width, occupying the same horizontal span as each other, each containing three arcs distinguished by line style: one **solid**, one **fine-dotted** (short, closely spaced dots), and one **long-dashed** (long dashes separated by short gaps). No axes, no numbers, no arrowheads; each panel is introduced by a sans-serif label above it.

**Top panel.** Label above it: **Complete, linked arcs:**

Three complete arcs of equal width and equal height, laid end to end left to right on a common baseline, each touching its neighbor at a single point on the baseline:

- first arc, on the left: solid line
- second arc, in the middle: fine-dotted line
- third arc, on the right: long-dashed line

Each rises from the baseline to a rounded crest and returns to the baseline. The result is three clean humps in a row with four distinct points on the baseline: the far-left start, the two junctions where one arc ends and the next begins, and the far-right finish.

**Bottom panel.** Label above it, on two lines: **Incomplete arcs—ideas are** / **discussed in multiple places:**

The same three arcs — same three line styles, same total width, same arc height — but each one is chopped into three pieces and the pieces are interleaved, so no arc is ever drawn continuously and no two pieces of the same arc are adjacent. Left to right, the nine visible fragments are:

1. **solid** — a short piece rising off the baseline at the far left
2. **fine-dotted** — a rising piece, climbing from the baseline
3. **long-dashed** — a short rising piece off the baseline
4. **solid** — a longer rising piece that climbs to just below crest height
5. **long-dashed** — a crest piece: the rounded top of an arc, over the middle-left of the panel
6. **solid** — a long descending piece running from crest height all the way down to the baseline
7. **fine-dotted** — a crest piece: the rounded top of an arc, right of center
8. **long-dashed** — a short descending piece down to the baseline
9. **fine-dotted** — a descending piece running down to the baseline at the far right

So the solid arc appears as rise / rise / fall, separated by other arcs' material; the fine-dotted arc as rise / crest / fall, its three pieces scattered to the far left, right of center, and far right; the long-dashed arc as rise / crest / fall, likewise scattered. The fragments overlap and cross in the middle of the panel, and the panel's beginning and end are cluttered by several different arcs' fragments starting and stopping near the same place, so no single clean start or finish point stands out. Both panels cover the same width and the arcs reach the same height in each, so the two patterns take up the same amount of space.

**What it demonstrates.** The same three ideas, given the same room on the page, can be drawn as three clean linked arcs or as nine scattered fragments. The top pattern is readable and gives four clear power positions — the start, the finish, and the two junctions where one idea closes and the next opens. The bottom pattern, in which each idea is broken up and discussed in several separate places, is visually chaotic and loses those positions: even its beginning and ending are muddled among competing fragments.

The chapter reduces evaluation of internal structure to a checklist to be applied paragraph by paragraph and section by section (p. 102–103):

1. Does each unit make a single, clear point?
2. When several paragraphs together form a section, are the linkages among them clear?
3. Has every extraneous thought that breaks the serial arc structure been removed?
4. When you introduce a topic, do you resolve that discussion before introducing a new topic?
5. Is every major unit of the work defined by either a subhead or clear opening text?

"If you can't answer 'Yes' to each of these questions, then you haven't finished working on the structure" (p. 103).

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 10.1 — a complete arc at subsection scale.* Drawn from a paper on nitrogen retention in Eastern U.S. watersheds, which found that southern watersheds retained more nitrogen and attributed this to warmer-climate denitrification. The excerpted subsection develops one supporting piece of that argument — the effect of human population density on the proportion of nitrogen exported — with the book's own bracketed numbers marking structural moves (p. 98):

> {1} Population density
>
> {2} Increased N export has been shown to be related to increased human population density. {3} If northern watersheds had higher population densities than more southern systems, it is possible that the attendant increase in N input could potentially result in less watershed processing and hence an increase in proportionate riverine export in northern systems. {4} However, the ranges of population densities in northern and southern watersheds overlapped considerably, and, for a given density, the proportion of N export was always higher in northern systems than in southern ones. When all watersheds were considered together, there was a significant relationship between population density and riverine export due only to the influence of the three most densely populated watersheds (the Charles, Blackstone, and Schuylkill). {5} We conclude that population density cannot explain the difference in N export between northern and southern watersheds, but it is a factor to consider for explaining high N export in some watersheds.

Source: S. C. Schaeffer and M. Alber, "Temperature Controls a Latitudinal Gradient in the Proportion of Watershed Nitrogen Exported to Coastal Ecosystems," *Biogeochemistry* 85 (2007): 333–46 (p. 98, footnote 1).

The book's annotation of its own bracketed markers (p. 98–99):

> {1} The passage starts with a subhead to identify the issue: human population density.
>
> {2} This is the opening (O), which identifies the topic and characters: N export and human population density.
>
> {3} This poses the challenge (C): if northern watersheds have more people they might export more N.
>
> {4} Here, the action (A) starts. The authors briefly analyze the data before coming to the climax: the apparent relationship between export and population density was driven by just three rivers.
>
> {5} The story resolves (R) with the statement "We conclude that population density cannot explain the difference in N export between northern and southern watersheds." The phrase "We conclude" is a flag that this is a resolution point.

The chapter notes this passage completes an entire OCAR arc and closes the discussion of population density permanently — "this is the last time the paper discusses it" (p. 99) — before the authors move to other explanatory factors.

*Example 10.2 — a two-paragraph arc, split by character.* From a second paper on global nitrogen cycling, addressing why plants are nitrogen-limited despite an atmosphere that is 78 percent N2. The excerpted section, titled "Stoichiometry," concerns element ratios between organisms (p. 99):

> Organisms use essential elements at characteristic ratios, and these ratios differ systematically among different groups of organisms. Element ratios are widely used in the analysis of marine ecosystems. Their application is usually less explicit in terrestrial ecology, but they provide the basis for using critical element ratios to predict element mineralization or immobilization during decomposition. One general feature of terrestrial ecosystems is that C:element ratios in plants, especially trees, are much wider than those in other organisms as a consequence of plants' use of C-based compounds (cellulose, lignin) to provide structure. For N in particular, soil bacteria generally have a C:N ratio near 6, while plants often have C:N ratios > 100. Even the leaf litter produced in forests on infertile soils can have C:N ratios in excess of 100.
>
> Consequently, relative to their own requirements, animals and microbes live in a C-rich, N-poor world. Animal nutrition and growth are often constrained by the N content of their food, and protein deficiency is widespread. This difference in stoichiometry can sustain N limitation to animals even where plants are not limited by N supply. Microbes also encounter little N (relative to their requirements) in the plant litter they decompose, and so they retain the N they obtain from their substrate and acquire more directly from inorganic pools in the soil. As a result, N cycling from organic matter back to biologically available forms lags behind the decomposition of plant litter.

Source: P. M. Vitousek, S. Hättenschwiler, L. Olander, and S. Allison, "Nitrogen and Nature," *Ambio* 31 (2002): 97–101 (p. 99, footnote 2).

The chapter's analysis (p. 100): the passage opens by defining the issue and characters (organisms, essential elements, characteristic ratios) and resolves by explaining nitrogen limitation: "As a result, N cycling from organic matter back to biologically available forms lags behind the decomposition of plant litter." What distinguishes this example from Example 10.1 is that the authors split one story into two paragraphs, each forming its own arc by character focus. The first paragraph is established as being about plants from its first sentence — "One general feature of terrestrial ecosystems is that C:element ratios in plants, especially trees, are much wider than those in other organisms" — and even a sentence that opens by mentioning bacteria closes by returning stress to plants ("while plants often have C:N ratios > 100"); the final sentence, opening "Even the leaf litter," keeps plants as subject and closes that arc. The second paragraph opens with "Consequently, relative to their own requirements, animals and microbes," making animals and microbes — the consumers of plants — the paragraph's characters, so that "all the sentences in this paragraph are about plant consumers, rather than about plants" (p. 100). The two paragraphs are linked by the word "consequently" and by the second paragraph picking up the first paragraph's C-rich, N-poor framing; the book states the authors could have written this as one long paragraph but that it is stronger as two independent arcs (p. 100).

*Example 10.3 — arcless writing, deliberately constructed.* The chapter presents a paragraph it says was written to be incoherent, about California fisheries and ocean acidification (p. 100–101):

> California supports rich fisheries off its coast. The high productivity of fish is supported by high rates of algal production. Algal growth in the ocean is typically limited by nitrogen supply, but this is high off California because N-rich deep water wells up to the surface along the coast. This upwelling is driven by winds that push the south-flowing surface water away from the shore, allowing deep water to rise to the surface. These off-shore winds are driven by regional climate patterns, including El Niño, that are being intensified by the greenhouse effect, which results from increased CO2 in the atmosphere. Increased CO2 in the atmosphere also increases the amount of CO2 dissolved in the ocean, which reacts with water to form carbonic acid (H2CO3), reducing the ocean's pH. This reduced pH makes it hard for shell-forming organisms to make calcium carbonate shells, and so may reduce the productivity of important marine species such as abalone, oysters, and even sea urchins. Thus, increasing atmospheric CO2 is going to have many important effects on marine ecosystems.

The book states this incoherence is deliberate and does not stem from unclear sentences — "each is a simple declarative statement" — nor from a failure of the sentences to link; rather, each sentence builds tightly off the idea preceding it (p. 101). It illustrates that tight local linkage with a chain of the topic words each sentence starts and ends with (p. 101):

> California . . . fisheries
> Fish . . . algae
> Algae . . . nitrogen . . . upwelling
> Upwelling . . . winds
> Winds . . . climate
> Climate . . . greenhouse effect . . . CO2
> CO2 . . . acid . . . reduced pH
> Reduced pH . . . damage to shell-forming organisms
> Thus, . . . CO2 will affect marine ecosystems.

The diagnosis: "the sentences flow, they don't flow anywhere in particular" (p. 101) — the paragraph keeps introducing new characters (nitrogen, upwelling, winds, climate, CO2, acidification, shellfish, marine ecosystems) rather than developing one, and its resolution statement, though true, has no closure back to the opening. The book calls this failure of "thematic coherence" and states it "fails to develop the OCAR functions into an effective structure. There is no clear point and no arc to the story. It drifts" (p. 101).

*Example 10.4 — the same material restructured into linked arcs.* The revision breaks the material into two connected arcs (p. 102):

> California supports rich fisheries off its coast. The high productivity of fish is supported by high rates of algal production. Algal growth in the ocean is typically limited by nitrogen supply, and is high off the California coast because N-rich deep water wells up to the surface along the coast. This upwelling is driven by winds that push the south-flowing surface water away from the shore, allowing deep water to rise to the surface. These winds are driven by regional climate patterns, including El Niño, that are being intensified by the greenhouse effect. Thus, the productivity of California fisheries will likely change as a result of climate warming, and the changes may result via complex and unexpected mechanisms such as changes in ocean circulation patterns.
>
> In addition, increasing CO2 is causing the pH of the ocean to decline, and this may have separate but important effects on California fisheries. As CO2 increases in the atmosphere, more dissolves into the ocean as carbonic acid (H2CO3) . . .

The book explains that the first, main arc concerns fishery productivity driven by ocean circulation and its sensitivity to climate change; the acidification material is pulled into a second, separate arc that connects to the first through the shared climate change–CO2 link and the phrase "in addition." It states the result gains coherence and "SUCCES-type 'simplicity'" — instead of one complex mass of interwoven information, a series of simple messages adding up to one larger simple message: rising atmospheric CO2 will alter California fisheries (p. 102).

The chapter attributes arcless writing to "stream of consciousness" writing, in which an author records each thought as it arises, one idea stimulating the next, all flowing onto the page — a habit it attributes especially to undergraduate essays written the night before a deadline, and notes that even experienced writers get such stray thoughts, but that "those extraneous thoughts don't survive to the final draft. If they are interesting and germane, they go into their own arc elsewhere, otherwise they go in the trash" (p. 102).

*Exercises (p. 103).*

- **10.1, Analyze published papers.** Return to the papers being analyzed and examine their internal structure: block out sections that form complete arcs, identify how the authors signal the beginning or ending of an arc, identify the theme of each arc, and give it a subheader describing that theme.
- **10.2, Write a short article.** Return to the reader's own short article and evaluate its story arcs: whether they are complete, or whether ideas keep recurring in multiple places; then rewrite to produce clear, well-defined arcs.

**Exercises.**

1. **10.1. Analyze published papers.** Go back, once again, to the papers you've been analyzing. This time look at their internal structure. Can you block out sections that form complete arcs? How do the authors indicate that they are beginning or ending arcs? Identify the theme of each arc and give it a subheader that describes that theme. (p. 103).
2. **10.2. Write a short article.** Go back again to your short article. Evaluate your own story arcs. Do you form complete arcs, or do you have ideas that keep cropping up in multiple places? Rewrite to ensure clear, well-defined arcs. (p. 103).

**Terms introduced.**

- **Story arc (extended to internal structure).** A unit of the paper — section, subsection, paragraph, or sentence — that itself has an opening, a challenge, action, and a resolution, so that the paper's overall arc is built of nested smaller arcs (p. 96).
- **Power positions.** The beginning and end of an arc, which emphasize whatever information occupies them and signal to the reader what is important; without discrete arcs, readers cannot tell what matters more from what matters less (p. 97).
- **Arcless (and "artless") writing.** Writing whose sentences are individually clear and linked but which never resolves and never closes back to its opening, so it has no clear point or story (p. 100–101).
- **Stream of consciousness writing.** The book's term for writing in which the author records thoughts in the order they occur, one idea prompting the next, producing arcless prose (p. 102).

**Connections.** Builds directly on the OCAR structure developed for the Introduction in chapters 5–7 and touched on for the Discussion in chapter 8, and on the Discussion-as-story argument of chapter 9, applying the same structure below the whole-paper level. The chapter states it is a bridge to finer scales — paragraphs and sentences — treated in the chapters that follow.


### Chapter 11 — Paragraphs

> "Make the paragraph the unit of composition." — Strunk and White, *The Elements of Style*, quoted p. 104.

**Claim.** A paragraph becomes a genuine "unit of composition" when it tells a complete short story with a coherent structure that fits into and contributes to the larger work, not merely when sentences are grouped together until a break feels due (p. 104). The grade-school topic-sentence–development model is one valid paragraph structure, not the only one; paragraphs can be built point-first or point-last using any of the story structures introduced earlier in the book, and a paragraph that has no discernible structure has, in effect, no clear point (p. 105, p. 109).

**Mechanism.** Schimel frames paragraph structure as a choice, following a distinction he attributes to Joseph Williams: "point-first" versus "point-last" paragraphs (p. 105, citing Joseph Williams, *Style: Toward Clarity and Grace*, University of Chicago Press, 1981).

Point-first paragraphs make their point early and then develop it:
- **TS-D (topic sentence–development):** the simplified, grade-school form — a topic sentence states the point, and the rest of the paragraph develops it. "If a paper were written with only TS-D paragraphs, you could skip along, reading the first sentence of each paragraph, and still get its essence." It is simple, clean, works for most jobs, and "should dominate your writing" (p. 105).
- **LD (lead/development):** an extended point-first form in which the lead — the point-making portion — takes several sentences rather than a single topic sentence, before the paragraph develops it (p. 106).

Point-last paragraphs assemble an argument and pull it together at the end. These take one of two forms:
- **LDR (lead/development/resolution):** opens with an argument and develops it, similar to LD, but then adds a synthesizing resolution — strong at both opening and close (p. 107).
- **OCAR (opening/challenge/action/resolution):** the opening sentence sets the stage without yet making an argument; the paragraph builds through a challenge and action to a resolution that arrives, and is made, only in the closing sentence (p. 108).

Schimel treats these four forms — TS-D, LD, LDR, OCAR — not as discrete categories but as points on a single spectrum, "from paragraphs with all the power in the first sentence to those with it all in the last — pure TS-D to pure OCAR" (p. 109). Some paragraphs resist clean classification because the weighting of a given sentence, or a reader's interpretation of it, can shift where on that spectrum the paragraph sits (p. 109).

He gives proportions and placement guidance: point-last paragraphs are "not terribly common; they might account for 25–30 percent of a paper. Writing is dominated by point-first paragraphs, particularly by TS-D, which is the bread-and-butter paragraph" (p. 109). The more complex, point-last structures tend to appear "at critical story points — openings, resolutions, and transitions." Short paragraphs are usually TS-D; long paragraphs benefit from a resolution to tie them together and remind the reader of the point, so they lean toward LDR or OCAR (p. 109). The governing instruction: "It's okay to write point-first paragraphs, and it's okay to write point-last paragraphs, but don't write point-nowhere paragraphs" (p. 109).

For diagnosing and repairing a bad paragraph, Schimel gives a three-step procedure, generalized from a worked example: (1) identify the real story the paragraph should tell; (2) decide whether it needs a point-first or a point-last structure; (3) pull apart the different threads of the material to clarify their relationships (p. 110). He restates this as a general principle for paragraph writing: "Identify (1) who the story is about, (2) your point, and (3) where you should make it. Put the critical pieces of information in the right places, and use the rest of the text to tie them together smoothly" (p. 110). He also states a length principle used in the repair: "short is better than long (see chapter 16), but you should take the space necessary to frame critical ideas. If you confuse readers, you lose them. If you need to condense, condense elsewhere" (p. 110).

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 11.1 — a simple TS-D "Result" paragraph* (p. 105):

> A Result: Neither calculation reproduces the experimental strength distribution. The distribution for GXPF1A is closer to the data, but it pushes the strength up too high in excitation energy. An even more dramatic increase occurs for the calculation with KB3G, although the strength integrated up to 7.5 MeV reproduces the experimental value quite well. The summed B(GT) strength up to Ex = 7.5 MeV (a total of 48 states) for the KB3G interaction is Σ B(GT)KB3G = 2.02 (with a further 10% of that value located at energies up to 10.3 MeV) compared to the experimental value of 1.95 ± 0.14 up to that excitation energy. The summed strength up to Ex = 7.5 MeV with the GXPF1A interaction is Σ B(GT)GXPF1A = 2.65. A further 8% of that value is located at higher excitation energies, fragmented over many weak states.

Schimel offers this, along with Example 11.2, as a plain TS-D paragraph, noting he had already used this method to describe methods and results back in chapter 8 (examples 8.1 and 8.4) (p. 105).

*Example 11.2 — a simple TS-D "Argument" paragraph* (p. 106):

> An Argument: We conclude that the increase of the diurnal temperature range [DTR] over the United States during the three-day grounding period of 11–14 September 2001 cannot be attributed to the absence of contrails. While missing contrails may have affected the DTR, their impact is probably too small to detect with a statistical significance. The variations in high cloud cover, including contrails and contrail-induced cirrus clouds, contribute weakly to the changes in the diurnal temperature range, which is governed primarily by lower altitude clouds, winds, and humidity.

*Example 11.3 — an extended-lead LD paragraph on metal–PAH complexes* (p. 106–107):

> Metal-PAH [polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon] complexes are important as models for surface science and catalysis; PAHs may be used to model finite sections of a carbon surface. There is also evidence that metal-PAH complexes may be constituents of interstellar gas clouds; they have been implicated in the depletion of atomic metal and silicon in the ISM and as contributors to the DIBs [diffuse interstellar bands] and UIB [unidentified infrared bands]. Increasing interest in metal-PAH systems has thus motivated many groups to produce these species in laboratory experiments. Dunbar and co-workers were the first to observe metal-PAH ion complexes in gasphase experiments using FT-ICR mass spectrometry. From these experiments, they determined the binding kinetics of a variety of metal and nonmetal cations with PAHs. Our group has produced a variety of metal and multimetal-PAH sandwich complexes using laser vaporization of film-coated metal samples in a molecular beam cluster source. Competitive binding and photodissociation experiments were successful in determining structural information and relative bonding strengths of metals with benzene, C60, and coronene. In other experiments, we used a laser desorption time-of-flight mass spectrometer to produce a variety of metal oxide and halide PAH complexes as well as mixed-ligand complexes. Experimental work has stimulated new theoretical studies investigating metal binding sites and bond energies on PAHs. Dunbar, Klippenstein and co-workers and Jena and co-workers have been active in this area.

Schimel identifies the italicized sentence in the original ("Increasing interest in metal-PAH systems has thus motivated many groups to produce these species in laboratory experiments") as the point of the paragraph: it closes a three-sentence lead explaining why such complexes matter, and opens the discussion of the research groups that have made them. Because the paragraph is long, the authors used several sentences to build to the point rather than a single topic sentence (p. 107). He also points back to the first paragraph of example 10.2 (nitrogen stoichiometry) as another LD paragraph whose three-sentence lead narrows to a final clause stating that element ratios "may predict element mineralization or immobilization during decomposition" (p. 106).

*Example 11.4 — an LDR paragraph on morphine and PTSD prevention* (p. 107–108):

> Although much of the research in the field of pharmacotherapy for the secondary prevention of PTSD after trauma is speculative, there is theoretical evidence that early use of anti-anxiety agents can be effective. Pitman and Delahanty argued that pharmacotherapeutic interventions for the prevention of PTSD will be most effective if medication regimens are implemented after exposure to traumatic events. Morgan and colleagues and other investigators have hypothesized that opiates may interfere with or prevent memory consolidation through a beta-adrenergic mechanism. This theory also lends support to the idea that morphine and other opiates may prove effective in the secondary prevention of PTSD after trauma.

Schimel maps this paragraph explicitly onto the LDR structure: the lead is the general argument (anti-anxiety agents should block PTSD), stated in the first sentence; the development is the discussion of the Pitman and Delahanty and the Morgan et al. findings; the resolution is the last sentence, arguing that because morphine is an anti-anxiety agent as well as a painkiller, it should limit PTSD (p. 108). He also cites the second paragraph of example 10.2 (nitrogen stoichiometry) as another LDR paragraph: it opens with the strong statement that animals and microbes live in a C-rich, N-poor world and closes with the conclusion that N recycling lags behind decomposition (p. 107).

*Example 11.5 — an OCAR paragraph on mammoth migration* (p. 108):

> If the Great Plains mammoths routinely undertook long-distance migrations, then mammoths at all of the Clovis sites in this study should display similar 87Sr/86Sr ratios. However, the Dent mammoths display 87Sr/86Sr ratios that are distinct from those of mammoths at Blackwater Draw and Miami, demonstrating that the Dent mammoths belonged to a distinct population. Thus, we conclude that Great Plains mammoths did not routinely migrate between northern Colorado and the southern High Plains, which are separated by about 600 km.

Schimel explains that the point — mammoths did not migrate long distances — arrives only in the closing sentence, making this point-last. The first sentence poses the question (did they migrate?) and names the method (strontium isotope ratios); it serves as both opening and challenge but does not answer the question, so it does not resolve. It functions as a guide to the story, in the manner of an OCAR opening, rather than as an LDR lead (p. 108). He also cites example 10.1, on population density and watershed nitrogen export, as another point-last OCAR paragraph: it opens by suggesting the two might be related, poses the challenge of whether population density differences explain north–south differences in N-export, develops the action across several sentences, and resolves that population density cannot explain the observed patterns (p. 108).

*Example 11.6 — a deliberately bad paragraph, before revision* (p. 109), concerning restoration of P-limited California grasslands using compost fertilization and mycorrhizal inoculation of a native grass:

> Adding compost to soil promotes microbial growth, which then increases microbial production of phosphatase enzymes that release plant-available P from organic matter. *Bromus carinatus* is a native grass that can be used in reestablishing California grasslands. Its success in P-poor systems can be stimulated by inoculation with mycorrhizal fungi. However, the effects of mycorrhizal inoculation of *B. carinatus* on P uptake have not been assessed.
