Schimel calls this "about as bad as it is possible to write." He diagnoses it as having an opaque point and an opaque structure — neither clearly point-first nor point-last — with several threads of argument weaving aimlessly through it: (1) P availability is critical to growing plants and restoring degraded California ecosystems; (2) two approaches may enhance P-uptake, compost and mycorrhizal inoculation; (3) *B. carinatus* can be used to reestablish grasslands; (4) the effects of mycorrhizal inoculation of *B. carinatus* specifically are unknown (p. 109–110). He identifies the intended story as the integration of the first two ideas, with *B. carinatus* incidental — merely a useful species — and attributes the derailment to the curse of knowledge: "When authors know too much but write too little, ideas get overcondensed and jumbled" (p. 110).

*Example 11.7 — the same material, revised into two paragraphs* (p. 110):

> Restoring degraded California grasslands requires adequate supplies of P to support plant growth. Two management approaches have been proposed to achieve this: adding compost and inoculating grasses with mycorrhizal fungi. However, which approach is more effective in enhancing P-uptake and restoration is unclear, and they may work synergistically. Compost not only adds organic P to the soil; it also stimulates microbes that produce phosphatase enzymes and so increases P-release. P uptake by plants, on the other hand, may be stimulated by inoculating them with mycorrhizal fungi.
>
> *Bromus carinatus* is a native grass that may be useful in restoring degraded California grasslands as it grows extensively throughout the state and tolerates harsh conditions. If it can be supplied with adequate P, it establishes well and starts the restoration process. A question, however, is which approach to enhancing P supply will work best with it, or whether both are necessary.

Schimel describes the revision as breaking one dense, cryptic paragraph into two, adding explanation, and giving each a clear point and structure — LD for the first, LDR for the second — so that each is a coherent story in itself and together they define a direction for the paper (p. 110).

This same before/after pair connects back to a paragraph revised in chapter 10 (example 10.3): a "stream of consciousness" paragraph fixed by breaking it into two smaller paragraphs, the first restructured as OCAR and the second as TS-D (p. 109).

*Exercises* (p. 111): the chapter closes with three numbered exercises.
1. **11.1, Analyze published papers.** Return to papers previously analyzed. Select paragraphs at critical points — opening, challenge, resolution, transitions — plus a random sample of body paragraphs. Classify each as point-first (TS-D or LD) or point-last (LDR or OCAR). Evaluate where the authors use each structure, whether a pattern governs the choice, and why a particular structure was used in each case.
2. **11.2, Write a short article.** Return to the reader's own short article and classify the structure of every paragraph (TS-D, LD, LDR, or OCAR). Judge whether each structure suits its location and function; if not — or if a paragraph has no definable structure — rewrite it to give it an appropriate structure.
3. **11.3, Edit**, in two parts: (A) rewrite Example 11.2 (the contrails/diurnal temperature range paragraph) as a point-last, OCAR paragraph, and assess whether it works as well; (B) rewrite Example 11.5 (the mammoths paragraph) as a TS-D paragraph, and assess whether it works as well.

**Exercises.**

1. **11.1. Analyze published papers.** Go to the papers you have been analyzing. Choose a selection of paragraphs in the papers; pick the critical points of opening, challenge, resolution, and transitions, as well as a random sampling of body paragraphs. Define their structures: point-first versus point-last. If point-first, are they simple TS-D or more developed LD? If point-last, are they LDR or OCAR? Evaluate where they use each structure — is there a pattern of where the authors use each type of paragraph? Can you determine why they use a particular structure in each case? (p. 111).
2. **11.2. Write a short article.** Go back to your short article and analyze the structure of every paragraph: TS-D, LD, LDR, or OCAR? Do they seem appropriate for the particular location and function they serve? If not (or worse, they have no definable structure), rewrite to give them an appropriate structure. (p. 111).
3. **11.3. Edit.** A. Rewrite Example 11.2 about jet contrails and diurnal temperature variation as a point-last, OCAR, paragraph. Does it work as well? B. Rewrite Example 11.5 about mammoths as a TS-D paragraph. Does it work as well? (p. 111) — answered in Appendix A.

**Terms introduced.**
- **Point-first / point-last paragraphs.** Schimel's paragraph-level analogue of the book's story structures, credited to Joseph Williams: a point-first paragraph states its argument early and develops it; a point-last paragraph assembles material toward a conclusion delivered at the close (p. 105).
- **TS-D (topic sentence–development).** The grade-school paragraph model: a topic sentence makes the point, and the rest of the paragraph develops it. Described as a simplified version of the LD story structure from chapter 4, and as the paragraph structure that "should dominate your writing" (p. 104–105).
- **LD paragraph (lead/development).** A point-first paragraph in which the point-making lead extends across several sentences rather than a single topic sentence (p. 106).
- **LDR paragraph (lead/development/resolution).** A point-last paragraph that opens with an argument, develops it, and then closes with a synthesizing resolution (p. 107).
- **OCAR paragraph (opening/challenge/action/resolution).** A point-last paragraph whose opening sentence merely sets the stage, with the argument built and resolved only by the closing sentence (p. 108).
- **Curse of knowledge.** Invoked to explain incoherent paragraphs like example 11.6: "When authors know too much but write too little, ideas get overcondensed and jumbled" (p. 110).

**Connections.** Builds directly on chapter 4's lead/development (LD) story structure, applying it at the paragraph level (p. 104–105). Reuses and re-analyzes examples 10.1, 10.2, and 10.3 from chapter 10 as instances of OCAR, LDR, and a stream-of-consciousness paragraph requiring repair, respectively (p. 106–109). Refers back to chapter 8's treatment of methods and results paragraphs (examples 8.1 and 8.4) as earlier instances of the TS-D form (p. 105). Points forward to chapter 16 for the principle that short paragraphs are generally preferable to long ones (p. 110).


### Chapter 12 — Sentences

**Claim.** A sentence is the shortest possible story, and the same OCAR (opening–challenge/action–resolution) logic that governs paragraphs and papers governs sentences: the grammatical subject, verb, and object should map onto the story's opening, action, and resolution. Because a sentence is short, readers have little tolerance for information delivered out of order — where a writer places a piece of information (topic, middle, or stress) determines how readers interpret it, independent of what facts the sentence contains.

**Mechanism.**

Schimel maps grammar onto story structure directly (p. 112):

> O Opening: who is the story about? = Subject
> C/A Challenge/action: what happened = Verb
> R Resolution: what was its outcome? = Object

Two positions carry the interpretive weight, following terminology Schimel attributes to Joseph Williams (*Style: Toward Clarity and Grace*):

- **Topic** (12.1, p. 113) — whatever is placed at the beginning of a sentence, which readers interpret as what the sentence is about. The topic should be a schema or character readers already know (common knowledge or something introduced earlier); putting new information at the topic position confuses readers because it signals "old" when it is new.
- **Stress** (12.2, p. 113) — the ending of a sentence, the position of greatest emotional/interpretive weight, since "last words carry the greatest weight." In a multiclause sentence, the end of each clause is a minor stress position. The stress position should hold the main message, new ideas, or terms the writer wants to emphasize.

Combining topic and stress with the buried middle produces the chapter's central rule, **the 2–3–1 rule of emphasis**, which Schimel attributes to Roy Peter Clark (*Writing Tools*, p. 116): the stress carries the greatest emphasis (1st), the topic is next (2nd), and the middle of the sentence carries the least (3rd). Managing this weighting means choosing deliberately what goes in the topic, what goes in the stress, and pushing lower-priority material into the middle.

**Subject–verb connection** (12.4, p. 116): the verb should immediately follow the sentence's subject. The longer the gap between actor and action, the duller and more confusing the sentence becomes — inserted parenthetical phrases that separate subject from verb should be moved elsewhere in the sentence.

Section 12.5, "Managing Real Sentences" (p. 116), gives a three-part checklist for revising complex sentences, restated explicitly after example 12.10 (p. 120):

> 1. The topic should be short and clear.
> 2. The main verb should follow it immediately.
> 3. The key message should come at the stress.

These apply "to individual clauses as well as to the sentence as a whole" (p. 120).

Section 12.6, "Long Sentences" (p. 120), addresses length directly: short sentences are not inherently better than long ones — the "simplification you should unlearn." A good long sentence uses an **LD (loose–deductive) structure**: state the key point in a short initial main clause, then add subordinate clauses that add depth and nuance, rather than making readers wait to the end for the point. Schimel calls this, following Roy Peter Clark, a "right opening sentence."

**Evidence and examples.**

Example 12.1 (Churchill, p. 114), used to demonstrate the power of the stress position:

> "…and even if, which I do not for a moment believe, this Island or a large part of it were subjugated and starving, then our Empire beyond the seas, armed and guarded by the British Fleet, would carry on the struggle, until, in God's good time, the New World, with all its power and might, steps forth to the rescue and the liberation of the Old."

Schimel notes that reading it aloud shows the voice landing on the end of each clause — the stress positions — because Churchill deliberately placed the words and ideas he wanted emphasized there.

Example 12.2 (p. 114) — three sentences containing identical facts (viruses, 1989, most abundant biological entities in the sea) that tell different stories depending on topic/stress placement:

> A. "Viruses were not studied in the sea until 1989 yet are its most abundant biological entities."
> B. "The most abundant biological entities in the sea are viruses, yet they were not studied until 1989."
> C. "The most abundant biological entities in the sea were not studied until 1989: viruses."

In A, viruses are the topic (old information) and the stress reveals they are the sea's most abundant biological entities — the sentence expands understanding of where viruses matter. In B and C, "biological entities in the sea" is the topic, building on a "sea creature" schema (probably fish) rather than a "virus" schema; B stresses the surprising lateness of study (1989), while C stresses that the abundant entities are, surprisingly, viruses — the opposite story from A. Schimel notes that three facts placed into one sentence do not create one message; they create six potential messages, of which he showed three.

Example 12.3 (p. 115), showing topic choice controlling accessibility, given in four successive versions:

> "Net mineralization represents the nitrogen available to plants because it reflects the difference between microbial nitrogen release and uptake in soil."

Revision opening with a familiar concept (plant nitrogen need) instead of the unfamiliar term:

> "The amount of nitrogen available for plants is controlled by net mineralization — the difference between microbial nitrogen release and uptake in soil."

A further simplification for readers outside the field, dropping the term entirely:

> "The amount of nitrogen available for plants is controlled by the difference between microbial nitrogen release and uptake in soil"

A version that instead moves "net N mineralization" into the stress position to define and emphasize it as a new term (appropriate for a textbook, not a specialist journal):

> "The amount of nitrogen available for plants is controlled by the balance between microbial nitrogen uptake and release in soil; we define this balance as net N mineralization."

This progression illustrates the 2–3–1 rule of emphasis (named explicitly at p. 116 immediately after this example): stress carries the most weight, topic next, middle least.

Example 12.4 (p. 116), demonstrating broken subject–verb connection:

> "The pooled effect sizes, both with and without adjustment for environmental risk factors, were larger for DNA-based than RNA-based viruses."

The subject ("pooled effect sizes") and verb ("were") are separated by a parenthetical about risk-factor adjustment. Revision, moving the parenthetical to the end:

> "The pooled effect sizes were larger for DNA-based than RNA-based viruses, regardless of whether environmental risk factors were adjusted for."

Example 12.5 (p. 117), on picking the right topic when a sentence has two potential actors:

> "It has been predicted that the global average temperature will increase at a rate of 0.2˚C/decade."

Schimel identifies two competing actors/actions — the unnamed predictors, and the temperature itself — and argues the real topic should be global average temperature. Revision:

> "Global average temperature has been predicted to increase at a rate of 0.2˚C/decade."

This collapses both the prediction and the increase into a single, tighter action, giving the sentence a better internal arc.

Example 12.6 (p. 117), a case with only one potential actor but a buried topic caused by a long incidental qualifying opening clause:

> "In this study, taking advantage of a well-annotated genome map and effective targeted-mutagenesis techniques, we analyzed the role of Bac17 in pathogenesis by Candida albicans."

Revision, moving the real topic ("we") closer to the beginning:

> "We analyzed the role of Bac17 in pathogenesis by Candida albicans by taking advantage of a well annotated genome map and effective targeted-mutagenesis techniques."

Section 12.5.2, "Unburying the Stress" (p. 118), works through four examples of weak endings.

Example 12.7 (p. 118), a sentence ending in weak trailing words:

> "The number of commercial products containing nanomaterials has risen rapidly; in 2006 there were only 212 while in 2009 there were over 1000 products on the market."

Schimel identifies the point to emphasize as the number 1000, not "products on the market," which is redundant with the sentence's earlier content. Revision, cutting the trailing words:

> "The number of commercial products containing nanomaterials has risen rapidly; in 2006 there were only 212 while in 2009 there were over 1000."

Example 12.8 (p. 118), with dangling, ambiguous trailing words:

> "Plants can increase their resistance to bacterial pathogens by increasing leaf alkaloid concentrations and by synthesizing tannins to bind to bacterial enzymes within plant tissues."

The phrase "within plant tissues" is ambiguous (does it modify where tannins are synthesized, or where the enzymes are?) and is not the intended message. Revision, deleting it so the stress falls on binding to bacterial enzymes:

> "Plants can increase their resistance to bacterial pathogens by increasing leaf alkaloid concentrations and by synthesizing tannins to bind to bacterial enzymes."

Example 12.9 (p. 118), a buried-stress case that cannot be fixed simply by deletion:

> "The data did not support our initial hypothesis, as no clear trend in reaction rate with pH was observed."

The important information — the absence of a trend in reaction rate — is buried mid-sentence, while the stress falls on "was observed." Revision stressing the reaction-rate finding and improving subject–verb connection:

> "The data did not support our initial hypothesis, as there was no clear trend in reaction rate with pH."

An alternate revision, offered in case the authors intended to emphasize uncertainty about observation rather than assert flatly that no trend existed, which places the observation clause in the sentence's 3-position (middle) under the 2–3–1 rule:

> "The data did not support our initial hypothesis, as we did not observe a clear trend in reaction rate with pH."

Example 12.10 (p. 119), a sentence with both topic and stress buried, worked through in three explicit steps:

> "The qualitative agreement between caribou's preference for feeding on young leaves and the trend for protein to decline with leaf age supports the hypothesis that caribou migration is driven by the patterns of leaf-out and maturation spatially and temporally through their home range, rather than by weather."

Schimel identifies the underlying idea as simple: caribou migrate to follow high-quality, nutritious food.

- Step 1, fix the topic: the original grammatical subject runs 21 words. The real topic — caribou's preference for young, protein-rich leaves — should open the sentence in fewer than 20 words: "Caribou prefer to feed on young, protein-rich leaves . . ." This nine-word opening has a one-word topic immediately followed by a verb, and leaves causality open (it states the leaves are protein-rich without asserting that is why caribou prefer them).
- Step 2, fix the stress: in the original, the stress falls on "weather." It should instead fall on the actual argument — that caribou follow food — via ". . . the spatial and temporal patterns of leaf-out and maturation."
- Step 3, finish: package remaining information into the middle. Complete revision:

> "Caribou prefer to feed on young, protein-rich leaves, supporting the hypothesis that migration is not driven by weather but by the spatial and temporal patterns of leaf-out and maturation."

Section 12.6 gives two long-sentence examples using right-opening (LD) structure.

Example 12.11 (p. 121), from a paleoclimate paper on Lake Tanganyika:

> "At the beginning of the second millennium AD, lake level at Lake Tanganyika fell and remained relatively low during the period from ~1050–1250 AD, which corresponds to the timing of the MWP [Medieval Warm Period] in many locales, albeit with a later onset than in some areas."

Schimel notes this sentence exceeds 40 words with a complex multiclause structure but reads simply because "Lake Tanganyika fell" opens the sentence with subject immediately followed by a powerful verb, and the following clauses add dimension in an LD (right-opening) design. He then constructs a counter-version that instead delays the key action to the sentence's stress, to show the cost of doing so:

> "During the early part of the second millennium AD, from ~1050–1250 AD, a period corresponding to the timing of the MWP in many locales, albeit with a later onset than in some, lake level at Lake Tanganyika fell and remained relatively low."

He argues this version is much harder to follow because readers have no framework to hang the opening timing information on and lose the thread while waiting for topic and action.

Example 12.12 (p. 121), on two GTPase proteins:

> "We focused on two members of this family: Rab5, which controls transport from the plasma membrane to the early endosome and regulates the dynamics of early endosome fusion, and Rab7, which governs membrane flux into and out of late endosomes."

Schimel highlights the short subject-plus-verb opening ("We focused"), the stress of the opening clause ("two members of this family"), and the colon signaling a doubly branched structure that then describes Rab5 and Rab7 in turn — a right-opening sentence held together despite its complexity.

**Terms introduced.**

- **Topic** — the position at the beginning of a sentence; whatever occupies it, readers interpret as what the sentence is about. Term from Joseph Williams, *Style: Toward Clarity and Grace*.
- **Stress** — the ending of a sentence (or, in a multiclause sentence, the end of each clause); the position of greatest emphasis, where key words, the main message, and new terms should go. Term from Joseph Williams.
- **2–3–1 rule of emphasis** — the stress carries the greatest emphasis (1), the topic is next (2), and the middle of the sentence carries the least (3). Attributed to Roy Peter Clark, *Writing Tools*.
- **Subject–verb connection** — the principle that the verb should immediately follow the sentence's subject; the longer the gap, the duller and more confusing the sentence.
- **Right-opening sentence / LD structure** — for long sentences, stating the key point in a short initial main clause and then adding subordinate clauses of depth and nuance, rather than delaying the point to the end. Attributed to Roy Peter Clark's description of a "right opening sentence."

**Connections.** Builds directly on the OCAR structure introduced earlier in the book (opening–challenge/action–resolution), applying it at the sentence level rather than the paragraph or paper level. Example 12.4's revision (fixing subject–verb connection via an LD structure) is referenced again in section 12.6 as an instance of right-opening design. Example 10.3 (the incoherent California fisheries paragraph) is referenced forward in chapter 13. Chapter 13, "Flow," which follows immediately, extends topic/stress management from single sentences to chains of sentences within paragraphs.

**Exercises.**

- **12.1, "Evaluate published papers."** Pick sentences from several of the paragraphs analyzed for the chapter 11 exercises. Evaluate what the authors chose to put at the topic and stress positions and whether those were good choices. If the authors used more complex sentence structures, assess whether they maintained strong subject–verb connection. For poorly structured sentences, rewrite them to make them clearer.
- **12.2, "Write a short article."** Go through every sentence in your article and examine sentence structure: whether the right topic and stress were chosen, and whether subject–verb connection is tight. Rewrite sentences as necessary to strengthen them.
- **12.3, "Write new sentences."**
  - A. Taking three elements allows six possible stories (as argued earlier in the chapter). Using the elements from example 12.2 (viruses, 1989, biological entities in the sea), write the three sentences not already given in the chapter.
  - B. Take the following pieces of information and write three different sentences telling three different stories: benzene, cancer, groundwater.
- **12.4, "Revise the following sentences to strengthen them."**
  - A. "Due to uncertainties resulting from interferences in the X-ray microanalysis, it remains unclear what the crystalline nature of kryptonite is."
  - B. "By reducing diffusion and increasing physiological stress, drought reduces soil microbial activity and causes a build-up of biodegradable C that is rapidly respired upon rewetting."


### Chapter 13 — Flow

**Claim.** A paper or proposal reads as a "page turner" when its sentences and paragraphs link to one another, not merely when each is individually well written. Flow has two components: paragraphs must hold together thematically (all sentences on the same team), and sentences must pass a baton at each transition so ideas carry cleanly from one to the next (a relay).

**Mechanism.** Every sentence has a topic (what it is about) and a stress (the new or emphasized information at its end). The topic performs a Janus function (figure 13.1) — simultaneously backward-looking, connecting to what has already been established, and forward-looking, announcing what the sentence will be about. Two patterns follow from how a sentence's topic relates to the previous sentence's stress (figure 13.2):

- **Topic-to-topic linkage** — successive sentences share the same topic — produces a list: a set of facts about one thing, with no inherent order or connective structure.
- **Stress-to-topic linkage** — a sentence's topic picks up the stress of the sentence before it — produces a story: each sentence builds on and advances from the one before.

A short run of sentences on the same topic (a list) is acceptable when they function as a unit — a general statement followed by its elaboration — but when a paragraph or section shifts to a new story arc, the writer must build an explicit bridge across the transition. The technique Schimel calls "reach back and grab": take an idea (not necessarily the exact word) from the arc that is ending and make it the topic of the sentence that opens the new arc. This may mean repeating a single word, transforming an idea into related language, or restructuring an entire sentence or paragraph — even where that means breaking another rule, such as the short-subject rule from chapter 12, in service of the connection.

The same logic scales up to paragraphs (section 13.1): a new paragraph should open by picking up the resolution of the paragraph before it, using the same stress-topic, resolution-opening strategy used between sentences. Two paragraphs linked only through a shared stress rather than a stress-to-topic relationship — stress-stress — do not connect; per Schimel, "what does stress-stress make? Confused readers" (p. 131).

A further diagnostic: when the "reach back and grab" fix produces local flow but pushes the story in what feels like the wrong direction, the real problem is often not the transition sentence but the aim of the earlier passage. In that case the fix belongs upstream — restructuring the earlier sentence or paragraph — rather than in a tactical patch at the transition. Revising for flow should proceed top-down, from overall structure to local wording, without losing sight of the big picture while handling details.

**Evidence and examples.**

To make several points about a single topic, Schimel notes the writer needs "an LD story structure, which gives an overview to define the theme for the list to follow" (p. 126), after which several sentences can link back to that theme.

*Example 13.1* (p. 125), a topic-to-topic list:

> Molecules are comprised of covalently bonded atoms. Molecules' reactions are controlled by the strength of the bonds. Molecules, however, sometimes react slower than bond strength would predict.

*Example 13.2* (p. 126), the same content restructured as stress-to-topic:

> Molecules are comprised of covalently bonded atoms. Bond strength controls a molecule's reactions. Sometimes however, those reactions are slower than bond strength would predict.

Schimel diagrams both versions in figure 13.2, showing 13.1's linkages as topic-to-topic (a list) and 13.2's as stress-to-topic (a story). The link between the second and third sentences of 13.2 repeats a single word ("reactions"); the link between the first and second transforms an idea ("bonded atoms" into "bond strength") rather than repeating it verbatim.

#### Sentence sketches, unnumbered diagram (p. 126)

**Caption.** None. The diagram sits between the paragraph ending "Let me sketch the sentences out and identify their topics and stresses." and the paragraph beginning "In example 13.1, the topic of each sentence is 'molecules.'"

**What it shows.** Two rows of schematic sentences, each row a horizontal series of three abbreviated sentences with their topic and stress positions labeled beneath, and curved square-cornered brackets drawn above to show which positions link to which.

Upper row (the example 13.1 sketch), three sentences left to right:
- "Molecules...  bonded atoms" — beneath it, in italics: "Topic   ...   Stress"
- "Molecules... bonded strength" — beneath it, in italics: "Topic   ...   Stress"
- "Molecules...bonded strength would predict." — beneath it, in italics: "Topic   ...   Stress"

Two brackets arch above this row. The first begins above "Molecules..." of sentence 1 (the topic) and ends above "Molecules..." of sentence 2 (the topic). The second begins above "Molecules..." of sentence 2 (the topic) and ends above "Molecules..." of sentence 3 (the topic). Both links therefore run topic-to-topic. The brackets have no arrowheads and no labels.

Lower row (the example 13.2 sketch), three sentences left to right:
- "Molecules...  bonded atoms" — beneath it, in italics: "Topic   ...   Stress"
- "Bond strength...reactions" — beneath it, in italics: "Topic ... Stress"
- "Reactions...bonded strength would predict." — beneath it, in italics: "Topic   ...   Stress"

Two shorter brackets arch above this row. The first begins above "bonded atoms" in sentence 1 (the stress) and ends above "Bond strength" in sentence 2 (the topic). The second begins above "reactions" in sentence 2 (the stress) and ends above "Reactions..." in sentence 3 (the topic). Both links therefore run stress-to-topic. Again no arrowheads and no labels on the brackets.

**What it demonstrates.** Laid one above the other, the two rows show the structural difference between the two example paragraphs: in the first, every link jumps from topic to topic, so each sentence restarts from the same subject and the sentences stack as parallel facts; in the second, each link carries the stress of one sentence into the topic of the next, so the sentences chain end-to-beginning. The bracket spans in the lower row are shorter and offset, showing the linkage happening across the sentence boundary rather than between the sentence openings.

*(The diagram is unnumbered and uncaptioned in the book. It sits with Examples 13.1 and 13.2, which it illustrates.)*

*Example 13.3* (p. 127), original — sentences individually well written but with a flow break between the second and third:

> Salvage logging is an increasingly common way of harvesting forests that have been attacked by insect pests. In salvage logging, trees that have been attacked are selectively harvested. Cavities in standing dead trees serve as nesting sites for birds. The population biology of cavity-nesting birds is therefore likely affected by salvage logging.

The first sentence's topic is salvage logging; the third abruptly introduces a new arc (dead trees and cavity-nesting birds) with no bridge. The fix reaches back to grab "harvesting" from the first arc and makes it the topic of a new bridging sentence: "The dead trees that are harvested, however, can provide cavities that are nesting sites for birds." Revised passage in full (p. 127):

> Salvage logging is an increasingly common way of harvesting forests that have been attacked by insect pests. In salvage logging, trees that have been attacked are selectively harvested. The dead trees that are harvested, however, can provide cavities that are nesting sites for birds. The population biology of cavity-nesting birds is therefore likely affected by salvage logging.

Schimel notes the fix required lengthening the subject of the bridging sentence, ordinarily a fault under the short-subject principle, but the flow requirement overrode it here. He then reads the whole passage as a clear OCAR paragraph: the opening sentence sets the topic (salvage logging and its effects); the action fills in those effects; the resolution states the paragraph's point and frames the paper's challenge (salvage logging affects bird population biology). The paragraph opens and closes on "salvage logging," forming a tight circle, but because the reader's understanding of the term has changed by the close, Schimel calls it a spiral rather than a closed loop.

He also examines the first two sentences of example 13.3 in isolation:

> Salvage logging is an increasingly common way of harvesting forests.
>
> In salvage logging, trees that have been attacked are selectively harvested.

Both open on "salvage logging" — a topic-to-topic list rather than a story — but this is not a defect: the second sentence elaborates the first as a single thematic unit, and a short list within one arc is fine. The requirement to bridge applies when the paragraph shifts arcs, not to every sentence pair.

*Example 13.4* (p. 128), original, illustrating a different problem — the wrong sentences are being linked:

> Groundwater level is an important control of the fate of contaminants within the groundwater: are they taken up plants and microorganisms in the surface soil? Industrial landscapes are frequently disturbed, and the effects of this disturbance on the system's ability to process contaminants has not often been studied. Low water tables could reduce an ecosystem's ability to process groundwater contaminants by moving these contaminants out of the reach of plant roots and microorganisms in the surface soil.

The first and second sentences do not connect thematically or through stress-topic linkage. But the real linkage in the paragraph is between the first and third sentences — groundwater, surface soil, and low water tables all relate to level in the soil column — so the fix is to delete the second sentence rather than bridge it. Revised (p. 128):

> Groundwater level is an important control of the fate of contaminants within the groundwater: are they taken up plants or microorganisms in the surface soil? Low water tables could reduce an ecosystems ability to process groundwater contaminants by moving these contaminants out of the reach of plant roots and microorganisms in the surface soil.

The displaced material about industrial landscapes may still matter, but Schimel's instruction is to give it its own story arc elsewhere and tie it back in, rather than leave it wedged between sentences it doesn't connect to.

He then shows the narrower, tactical alternative he rejected: forcing a connection by rewriting the second sentence in the passive voice to reach back and grab the first sentence's stress (p. 129):

> Groundwater level is an important control of the fate of contaminants within the groundwater: are they taken up plants or microorganisms in the surface soil? These organisms may be disturbed by industrial development, but such effects on contaminant fate have not often been studied. Low water tables could reduce an ecosystems ability to process groundwater contaminants by moving these contaminants out of the reach of plant roots and microorganisms in the surface soil.

This version connects the first two sentences but leaves the second and third unconnected — it pushes the same unsolved problem further down the paragraph and redirects the story, probably in the wrong direction. Schimel frames this as a tactical fix applied to a problem that needed a strategic one: restructuring which arcs belong where, rather than patching a single transition.

*Section 13.1, Linking Paragraphs within a Section.* The same stress-topic, resolution-opening logic that links sentences also links paragraphs: a paragraph break signals a shift into a new story arc, but the new paragraph must still build off the one before it.

*Example 13.5* (pp. 129–130), from a paper on nanomaterials, original two paragraphs:

> Most previous studies on the aggregation of nanomaterials have focused on the behavior of uncoated particles in dilute aqueous suspension with few contaminants present. However, particles released into natural settings are likely to have organic coatings and will be exposed to humic acids in solution. The interactions of coatings and humics can alter surface redox potentials and particle aggregation.
>
> Nanotitanium oxide particles coated with hydrophobic coatings showed higher aggregation in a medium with high concentrations of humic acids than in control waters. Particles coated with hydrophilic coatings, however, showed reduced aggregation in the presence of humics.

The paragraphs are related — the first lays out general arguments about particle behavior in nature (an OCAR structure resolving on coatings, humics, and aggregation); the second reports this study's results — but the connection is never made overt: the second paragraph opens on the specific topic "nanotitanium oxide particles" rather than picking up the first paragraph's resolution. The fix grabs the theme of "aggregation" from the first paragraph's resolution and restructures the second paragraph's opening to lead with it (p. 130):

> The interactions of coatings and humics can alter surface redox potentials and particle aggregation.
>
> Aggregation of nanotitanium oxide particles coated with hydrophobic coatings was higher in media with a high concentration of humic acids than in control waters. . . .

*Example 13.6* (pp. 130–131), from a paper on bacterial stress adaptation, illustrating that the linking idea is sometimes larger than a single word, original two paragraphs:

> Any trait that increases a bacterium's ability to survive an environmental stress, such as heavy metals or antibiotics, can be considered a stress-adaptation mechanism. Traditionally, however, studies have focused on internal mechanisms of adaptation: either a bacterium's ability to either repair cell damage (e.g., DNA repair) following stress or on mechanisms that make the cell more able to resist the damage in the first place (e.g., producing chaperones and transporters). However bacteria also have mechanisms that work outside the cell to reduce the intensity of stresses in the first place.
>
> E. coli provides an excellent model system for studying how the relative physiological costs of different stress-adaptation mechanisms vary between heavy metal and antibiotic stressors. We understand E. coli's metabolic pathways well enough to assess the full energetic costs of internal adaptation mechanisms such as DNA repair and exporting toxic agents from the cell vs. external mechanisms such as producing chelating agents to bind heavy metals and extra-cellular oxidase enzymes to break down antibiotics before they enter the cell.

The two paragraphs are topically related but not linked: as the reader enters the second, nothing signals the connection to the first. Schimel diagnoses the relationship between the paragraphs as stress-stress — both share the same underlying idea (that bacteria have multiple mechanisms for handling environmental stress) but neither paragraph's opening picks up the other's resolution. The fix repeats the shared stress as the opening of paragraph two (p. 131):

> An excellent model system for studying the relative costs of different stress-adaptation responses to heavy metals and antibiotics is E. coli.

This revised opening runs nineteen words, breaking the short-subject rule from chapter 12, which Schimel accepts as the cost of making the connection explicit.

He then gives a second option — instead of fixing paragraph two's opening, rewrite the end of paragraph one to introduce E. coli there and let paragraph two reach back and grab the whole preceding sentence (p. 131):

> However bacteria, such as E. coli, also have mechanisms that work outside the cell to reduce the intensity of environmental stressor in the first place.
>
> E. coli is an excellent system for studying the relative costs of these external mechanisms relative to internal mechanisms in response to heavy metal and antibiotic stressors. We understand E. coli's metabolic pathways . . .

Schimel states he cannot say which of the two fixes — rewriting the second paragraph's opening or rewriting the end of the first — is better in the abstract: "only you know your story" (p. 131). He also cross-references example 13.4 (groundwater) as a caution here: the "reach back and grab" approach can create local flow while causing global disruption, in which case the fault lies with the direction of the earlier paragraph rather than the opening of the later one, and the first paragraph is what needs fixing.

#### Figure 13.1 (p. 125)

**Caption.** Janus, the god of doors and beginnings.

**What it shows.** A black-and-white line drawing, no text labels anywhere inside the image. A square panel is enclosed by a decorative border made of short black rectangular blocks arranged like a row of teeth along all four sides, with small white gaps between the blocks. Inside the panel is a bust of the Roman god Janus rendered as a two-faced head: a single head with two profiles facing in opposite directions, sharing one mass of hair and beard between them. The left profile faces left and is drawn as a clean-shaven youthful face — outlined brow, eye, nose, lips, and chin, with a bare neck sloping down to the bottom of the panel. The right profile faces right and is drawn as an older bearded face — heavy brow, eye, nose, moustache, and a long curling beard drawn in dense solid-black lobes. The hair on top of the head is drawn as tight curls in heavy black, spilling down between and behind the two faces and merging into the beard on the right side. The image occupies roughly the lower half of the page; there are no arrows, axes, numbers, or callouts.

**What it demonstrates.** It supplies the visual metaphor the surrounding text uses for the topic position of a sentence: like Janus, the topic looks two ways at once — backward to information the reader already has, and forward to what the sentence is about to say — so a single element connects past to future.

#### Figure 13.2 (p. 126)

**Caption.** Creating a list versus a story: the role of topic and stress.

**What it shows.** Two stacked schematic rows, each row consisting of two identical rectangular boxes side by side connected by a single curved double-headed arrow arching above them, with an italic label to the right of the row.

Top row, labeled **List** in bold italic to the right of the second box:
- Left box contains "Topic ... Stress"
- Right box contains "Topic ... Stress"
- A tall curved arc rises from the left box and descends into the right box. It carries a solid arrowhead at each end. The left arrowhead points down-left into the left box at its left end, on the "Topic" side; the right arrowhead points down into the right box at its left end, also on the "Topic" side. The arc is labeled "Linkage" in italics, set above the apex of the curve, toward the left of center.

Bottom row, labeled **Story** in bold italic to the right of the second box:
- Left box contains "Topic ... Stress"
- Right box contains "Topic ... Stress"
- A lower, shorter curved arc connects the two boxes, again with a solid arrowhead at each end. The left arrowhead points down-left into the left box at its right end, on the "Stress" side; the right arrowhead points down into the right box at its left end, on the "Topic" side. The arc is labeled "Linkage" in italics above its apex, toward the left of center.

The two rows are drawn at the same scale so the arcs can be compared directly: the List arc spans from box-left to box-left and rises high above the boxes; the Story arc spans from box-right to box-left and is a short, low hop across the gap between them.

**What it demonstrates.** It contrasts the two ways consecutive sentences can be joined. When the linkage attaches topic to topic, the result is a list: each sentence begins from the same starting place and the connection has to reach back over the whole preceding sentence. When the linkage attaches the stress of one sentence to the topic of the next, the result is a story: the connection is a short handoff at the sentence boundary, carrying the new information just delivered into the starting position of what follows.

EXERCISES (p. 132):

1. **13.1, Evaluate published papers.** Pick two of the papers you've been evaluating: one that reads well and smoothly, one that doesn't. Evaluate how each manages the flow of ideas between sentences and between paragraphs. Determine whether they use the techniques discussed in the chapter to develop flow, and if not, identify places where you could rewrite them to enhance flow and ease of reading.
2. **13.2, Write a short article.** Revisit your short article and look for whether you could enhance the flow of ideas by working more actively on linking your sentences and paragraphs together. If you can, do it.
3. **13.3, Edit.**
   - **A.** Revise these sentences so the ideas flow smoothly: "Studies comparing iron-resistant and sensitive cell lines confirmed that protein X17 is denatured in the presence of Fe. Protein X17, however, reverts to its native form when cellular Fe concentrations decrease."
   - **B.** Revise the following paragraphs so that the ideas link clearly: "The TREE2 promoter is upstream of the tryb gene. However, Silva et al. (2008) showed that the STEM3 promoter is also upstream of this gene and appeared to be involved in tryb regulation. We showed that both are necessary and they interact to further upregulate tryb: knocking out TREE2 reduced transcription by 50%, knocking out STEM3 reduced it by 75%, and knocking out both prevented transcription entirely. When LEA was absent, however, even with both promoters intact, transcription rates were only 50% of control levels. It appears that LEA interacts with TREE2 and STEM3 to induce greater promotion than either can effect on their own."

**Terms introduced.**

- **Flow.** The property that lets a reader move from one sentence and paragraph to the next without derailing; achieved by managing topic and stress so ideas carry across transitions (p. 124–125).
- **Janus function.** The dual role of a sentence's topic — simultaneously backward-looking (linking to established information) and forward-looking (announcing what follows) — named for the Roman god of doorways and beginnings (figure 13.1) (p. 125).
- **Topic-to-topic linkage.** Successive sentences sharing the same topic; produces a list — a set of facts about one thing without inherent connective structure (p. 126).
- **Stress-to-topic linkage.** A sentence's topic picking up the stress (new information) of the previous sentence; produces a story — each sentence advancing from the one before (p. 126).
- **Reach back and grab.** The technique of taking an idea (word or concept) from a closing story arc and making it the topic of the sentence opening the next arc, to bridge a transition (p. 127).
- **Stress-stress.** Two sentences or paragraphs linked only by sharing the same stress rather than a stress-to-topic relationship; per Schimel, this produces "confused readers" rather than a coherent connection (p. 131).

**Connections.** Builds on chapter 10's discussion of paragraph coherence (the stream-of-consciousness California fisheries paragraph, example 10.3, cited here as the negative case for thematic coherence) and on chapter 12's short-subject rule, which this chapter shows being deliberately broken (in examples 13.3 and 13.6) to preserve flow across a transition. The OCAR structure, introduced earlier in the book, is applied here to example 13.3 as a worked case of paragraph-level shape.


### Chapter 14 — Energizing Writing

**Claim.** Sentence-level energy in science writing comes from verbs. Three habits drain that energy — passive voice, fuzzy verbs, and nominalizations — and each has legitimate uses alongside its costs; the chapter's task is teaching when a writer is using the tool deliberately versus defaulting to it out of habit or fear.

The chapter opens with an epigraph from Mark Twain (p. 133):

> Don't say the old lady screamed. Bring her on and let her scream.

Schimel glosses this as the root of "show, don't tell," and ties it to the two C's of SUCCES from earlier chapters — credible and concrete — both of which depend on showing rather than telling. He states (p. 133):

> Within a sentence, showing action is the job of verbs and it's an important job. Good writers use their verbs well, imbuing their papers with life. Bad writers use them poorly, stealing energy from the story, leaving it dull and listless.

He names three notable ways to emasculate verbs (p. 133): (1) passive voice, (2) fuzzy verbs, and (3) nominalizations. The chapter's three main sections take these in turn.

**Mechanism.**

*14.1 Active versus passive voice.* The simplest sentence structure follows OCAR directly: who did it (O), what they did (A), what happened (R), rendered as Actor–Action–Acted-on, which maps onto Subject–Verb–Object. Schimel diagrams this with the sentence "John called Jane" — John/Actor/Subject, called/Action/Verb, Jane/Acted-on/Object (p. 134) — and calls this the active voice: "clear, concise, and direct... also visual and evocative," because the actor is named up front and the action is carried in a verb immediately following the subject. He cites Strunk and White's commandment: "Use the active voice" (p. 134).

The passive voice inverts this. Schimel's example: "Jane was called by John" — Jane/Acted-on/Subject, was called/Action/Verb, John/Actor/Object (p. 134). He defines the construction (p. 134):

> To create it, we make the acted-on the sentence's subject and make the verb by coupling some form of "to be" to the action verb; in this case, "was called."

He states the passive voice's value directly (p. 134): "It allows you to control who or what, in a sentence, the story is about. It allows you to select the grammatical subject and object of the sentence relative to the actor and acted-on of the story. It allows you to control what goes in the topic and what goes in the stress. And it does all that without changing the action." Whether one writes "John called Jane" or "Jane was called by John," John still made the call — only perspective changes.

But the passive carries a price. Schimel quotes Stephen King (p. 134, from *On Writing*): "With an active verb, the subject of the sentence is doing something. With a passive verb, something is being done to the subject of the sentence. The subject is just letting it happen."

*14.1.1 Controlling perspective.* The passive's first legitimate use is shifting a sentence's topic between actor and acted-on, which Schimel ties back to chapter 13's example 13.3, quoted again here (p. 135):

> In salvage logging, trees that have been attacked are selectively harvested. The dead trees that are harvested, however, can provide cavities that are nesting sites for birds.

He gives two active/passive sentence pairs to illustrate when the passive puts the right term in the right place:

> Example 14.1
> Active: A magnetospheric source produces variable electric fields.
> Passive: Variable electric fields are produced by a magnetospheric source.
> (p. 135)

> Example 14.2
> Active: Soil porosity influences water retention.
> Passive: Water retention in soil is influenced by porosity.
> (p. 135)

His analysis: if either sentence stood alone as the whole story, the active version would be stronger — shorter, punchier. But if the surrounding story is about variable electric fields, or about water retention in soil, the passive puts the right term first and is the version to choose (p. 135). He characterizes this use as a tool "that weakens a single sentence, but in a way that can allow it to fit more snugly into a paragraph, strengthening the whole" (p. 135).

*14.1.2 Hiding the actor.* The passive's second use is omitting the actor. Schimel states the tension plainly (p. 135): "The strength of the active voice is that it forces you to make the actor and action clear. The weakness of the active voice is that it forces you to make the actor and action clear." He gives the political cliché "mistakes were made" as the classic case of using the passive to dodge blame, noting that converting it to active voice requires naming who screwed up (p. 135).

He then gives two legitimate scientific uses of actor-hiding. First, Materials and Methods sections, where first-person active ("We collected samples") is now generally acceptable but breaks down when technicians or interns who aren't coauthors did the work; the passive lets an author "cleanly and honestly tell us what we need to know: samples were collected" (p. 136).

Second, citing prior work without over-specifying attribution. Schimel walks through his own example from a paper on soil microbial community composition (p. 136):

> Example 14.3
> It has been argued that such processes should be insensitive to microbial community composition (Schimel 1995).¹

He explains he chose the passive deliberately because he had made that argument himself in an earlier paper but his thinking had evolved; the active alternative — "In a previous paper, I argued... but now I think I was wrong" — struck him as sounding bad and risked trivializing the point into "a private debate with myself," when in fact other papers had made or reinforced similar arguments (p. 136). The passive let him leave open how many people, and whom, had made the argument.

He then turns to a use he does not endorse: the "long-standing tradition that scientists should divorce themselves from the work by describing their actions in the passive voice" (p. 136), illustrated with:

> Example 14.4
> When expression of Chla and Chlb were compared, similar patterns of transcript abundance were observed in plants at different developmental stages.
> (p. 136)

Schimel's comment: "Someone did the comparing and observing, and most likely it was the authors. So why not make this clearer by naming the actor and shortening the subject?" (p. 136). His revision: "When we compared Chla and Chlb expression, we found similar patterns of transcript abundance in plants at different developmental stages" (p. 136).

He traces the tradition's rationale to a claim of scientific objectivity, quoting an excerpt directly (p. 137, quoting S. R. Leather, "The Case for the Passive Voice," *Nature* 381 [1996]: 467):

> Using the passive voice in scientific writing allows the researcher to stand at a distance from his or her work. By standing at a distance, an unbiased viewpoint is much more likely to be reached. An unbiased viewpoint encourages a world view and an open mind, surely prerequisites for good science.

Schimel calls this "an impassioned plea" containing "some important truths — but some equally important fallacies" (p. 137). His counter: objectivity comes from how a scientist treats the data, not from how the writing is voiced; removing oneself visibly from the prose does not remove one's prejudices, because "We did the work, and we wrote the words. They are inextricably ours" (p. 137). He ties true objectivity instead to Anne Lamott's advice from chapter 2 — listen to your characters, be attentive to the data, then tell the story in the clearest language once that listening is done (p. 137).

He notes that even Strunk and White qualify their own rule, quoting them directly (p. 137): "This rule does not, of course, mean that the writer should entirely discard the passive voice, which is frequently convenient and sometimes necessary." Schimel's summary rule for section 14.1: use the passive when you need to make the acted-on the sentence's subject or when you have an honest reason to avoid naming the actor; otherwise, use the active voice (p. 137).

*14.2 Fuzzy verbs.* Fuzzy verbs "mask the action" and are weak and potentially confusing, in contrast to verbs that show action and make writing clear (p. 137). First example:

> Example 14.5
> Controls on the expression of homeobox genes have been evaluated in several model systems.
> (p. 137)

Schimel's diagnosis: the verb "evaluated" is passive, but passivity isn't the problem — the problem is that it tells us something happened without telling us how the controls were evaluated or how they differ between organisms (p. 138). He identifies the paragraph's real story as varying patterns of control, and offers a stronger opening: "Homeobox gene expression is regulated differently among plants, fungi, and animals" (p. 138). He notes this revision keeps the verb passive but makes the model systems concrete and strengthens the message via "is regulated differently," making clear the paragraph will discuss how expression varies across organisms (p. 138). He also shows the fully active alternative — "Plants, fungi, and animals regulate homeobox gene expression differently" — and explains why he rejects it: it shifts the topic to the organisms instead of the genes, and it forces the verb and adverb apart, a separation the passive original avoided (p. 138).

Second example:

> Example 14.6
> Herbivores facilitate the invasion of exotic grasses by mediating competition between exotic and native plants.
> (p. 138)

Schimel's diagnosis: "facilitate" and "mediate" invite the question "what do herbivores do to mediate competition?" (p. 138). Revision: "Herbivores preferentially eat native plants, giving exotic grasses a competitive advantage that allows them to invade" (p. 138). He notes the revision's verbs — "eat," "give," "invade" — show what the animals physically do, that it would effectively open a paragraph by introducing the characters (herbivores, native plants, invading exotic grasses), the actions, and the challenge, and that it places the critical action "invade" in the sentence's stress position for emphasis (p. 138).

#### Table 14.1 (p. 139)

**Caption.** *Table 14.1.* Fuzzy verbs versus action verbs

A four-column word list with no column headings; the two bold labels below are row-spanning category headings printed flush left across the table.

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Fuzzy Verbs (Weak)** | | | |
| Occur | Facilitate | Conduct | Implement |
| Affect | Perform | | |
| **Action Verbs (Strong)** | | | |
| Modify | Increase | React | Accelerate |
| Accomplish | Decrease | Inhibit | Migrate |
| Create | Invade | Disrupt | |

*14.2.1 Fuzzy hypotheses.* Schimel identifies the hypothesis statement as the worst place for a fuzzy verb, because a fuzzy hypothesis becomes unfalsifiable. His example, drawn from proposals he has read (p. 139):

> Example 14.7
> Microbial community composition is controlled by the chemical nature of plant inputs, water availability, and soil chemistry.
> (p. 139)

His diagnosis: the passive, fuzzy "is controlled" makes this "a truism rather than a falsifiable hypothesis" — it is not conceivable that microbial community composition would be uncontrolled by plant inputs, water, and soil chemistry (p. 139). He states flatly: "Fuzzy hypotheses almost guarantee that your proposal will end up on the 'do not fund' list" (p. 139). His revision, which he identifies as the actual hypothesis of a successful proposal: "The chemical nature of plant inputs is the single strongest control on the composition of soil microbial communities and on their distribution across the landscape" (p. 139). He notes this is active voice with the simple verb "is," a declarative and testable statement that does not ignore other factors but puts them in perspective (p. 139).

He attributes the appeal of fuzzy verbs to fear of being challenged or being wrong, and reframes both as acceptable outcomes: "If people feel challenged, you have engaged their interest, and that is good. Challenging proposals sometimes get funded; boring ones never do" (p. 139). He restates the scientist's actual job (p. 139): "it is not your job to be right. It is your job to be thoughtful, careful, and analytical; it is your job to challenge your ideas and to try to falsify your hypotheses; it is your job to be open and honest about the uncertainties in your data and conclusions." He closes the point (p. 140): "As long as you did it with honesty, integrity, and intellect, you did right, even if you weren't right," and "Being interesting is ultimately more important than being right."

*14.3 Nominalizations.* Nominalizations go further than fuzzy verbs by killing the action entirely, converting a verb into a noun. Schimel's contrast pair:

> Example 14.8
> We investigated the effect of elevated CO₂ on plant growth.
> (p. 140)

He contrasts this with how many would instead write it: "We conducted an investigation of the effect of elevated CO₂ on plant growth" (p. 140). His diagnosis: this version has a verb, the fuzzy "conducted," but asks rhetorically "did you conduct an investigation, a train, or an orchestra?" — the true action is contained in the noun "an investigation," so the sentence names the action and introduces a new verb that hides it (p. 140). He names the pattern (p. 140): "This process of turning a verb into a noun is known as creating a nominalization." He states the cost directly: "using a noun rather than a verb to describe action, example 14.8 lost energy and gained length, but contains no more information" (p. 140).

#### Table 14.2 (p. 141)

**Caption.** *Table 14.2.* Verbs and their nominalized equivalents

The line "In some cases, the verb and nominalization almost have the same form" is printed in roman as a full-width row inside the table, between the sixth and seventh entries.

| Verb | Nominalization |
|---|---|
| Move | Movement |
| Differ | Difference |
| Suggest | Suggestion |
| Interact | Interaction |
| Analyze | Analysis |
| Develop | Development |
| *In some cases, the verb and nominalization almost have the same form* | |
| Influence | A influenced B versus A had an influence on B |
| Approach | A approached the problem versus A took an approach to the problem |
| Yield | The reaction yielded a product versus The yield of the reaction was . . . |

To illustrate a sentence that nominalizes every important action:

> Example 14.9
> Systemic infusion of fetal stem cells appears to be the most practical mode of administration; however, limited migration of cells to the target tissue may act as a constraint on its effectiveness.
> (p. 140)

Schimel's diagnosis: "The only verbs are 'appears,' 'to be,' and 'act,' which is sad, as there is no shortage of actions: 'infuse,' 'administer,' 'migrate,' 'constrain,' and even 'target'" (p. 140). His revision, converting the nominalized actions back to verbs: "The most practical way to administer fetal stem cells is to infuse them systemically; however, if cells don't migrate to the target tissue, this will fail" (p. 141).

He then shows a case where nominalizing pushes the action out of a sentence's critical position:

> Example 14.10
> Although models exist to calculate reaction rates as a function of molecular size, a failure to reproduce the experimental data is often observed.
> (p. 141)

His diagnosis: this "combines a nominalization with a passive to create a sentence with the minimum possible punch." The author's real point — that these models often fail — is nominalized to "a failure," which pushes the passive verb phrase "is often observed" into the sentence's stress while burying the critical action "in the bowels of the sentence" (p. 141). His revision: "Although models exist to calculate reaction rates as a function of molecular size, they often fail to reproduce the experimental data" (p. 141). He explains the fix: opening the second clause with the pronoun "they" ties it back to the models, and the sentence then delivers the point directly — "they often fail" — putting the verb in the important place, the start of the main clause (p. 141).

*14.3.1 Adjective nominalizations.* A second nominalization pattern converts adjectives into nouns.

#### Table 14.3 (p. 142)

**Caption.** *Table 14.3.* Adjective nominalizations

| Adjective | Nominalization |
|---|---|
| Different | Difference |
| Difficult | Difficulty |
| Able | Ability |
| Capable | Capability |
| Similar | Similarity |

Comparison pair:

> Example 14.11
> A. The characteristics of this condition are the oxidation of membrane lipids, the denaturation of proteins, and a reduction in growth rates.
> B. This condition is characterized by oxidized membrane lipids, denatured proteins, and reduced growth rates.
> (p. 142)

Schimel's analysis: version A nominalizes every adjective — "characteristics," "oxidation," "denaturation," "reduction" — while version B restores them all as adjectives, making the sentence shorter and sharper (p. 142).

A three-step repair sequence:

> Example 14.12
> A. There was a difference between the reaction rates of treatments X and Y.
> B. Reaction rates were different between treatments X and Y.
> C. Reaction rates differed between treatments X and Y.
> (p. 142)

Schimel walks through all three (p. 143): version A nominalizes the action as "difference," with "was" as the only verb, a weak one; version B turns it into a real adjective, "different," but still relies on the weak "were"; version C moves the action into the verb "differed," making it "both the shortest and most vigorous."

*14.3.2 Why do nominalizations exist?* Schimel poses the question directly and answers that naming makes concepts concrete and reusable — "Names hold magic" (p. 143) — offering examples of nominalizations used to name concepts economically (p. 143):

> Taxation without representation
> Gene expression
> Aromatic molecule
> Ecosystem services
> Epigenetics

He explains the mechanism and its risk: naming a concept "defines a new schema," which is powerful shorthand when the reader shares that schema and a source of miscommunication when they don't — his example being a reader who interprets "aromatic molecule" as "perfume" rather than a ring with conjugated double bonds (p. 143). When a reader lacks the schema, a nominalization becomes jargon, "an unclear term that seems designed to exclude noninitiates from the club" (p. 143). He notes audience determines whether a term needs defining: a public audience needs "aromatic molecule" defined or the writer looks arrogant; a paper in *Organic Chemistry* would look silly defining it (p. 143). He gives one further example of productive nominalization — "The arguments developed above..." — which compresses paragraphs of prior text into a single word (p. 143).

He closes with a reused example, previously discussed as example 12.1, from Winston Churchill (p. 143): "until in God's good time, the New World, with all its power and might, steps forth to the rescue and the liberation of the old." He notes Churchill put the nominalizations "the rescue" and "the liberation" in the sentence's stress, and shows the verb alternative Churchill could have used but did not: "until in God's good time, the New World, with all its power and might, steps forth to rescue and liberate the old" (p. 143). Schimel's judgment: "This is weaker — the verbs don't have the same mass and solemnity, and Churchill deliberately left the action on 'steps forth'" (pp. 143–144), because Churchill was encouraging the United States to step forth so that Britain would not need rescue and liberation. He credits Churchill with also using parallelism and repetition ("the rescue and the liberation") to add weight, driven home by placing it in the sentence's stress (p. 144).

The chapter's closing instruction (p. 144): "Find the action in your sentences, put it in your verbs, and put them early in their sentences. If you do, your writing will be clear and lively. Sometimes a passive or nominalization will strengthen your writing, and sometimes they are essential. Every time you use them unnecessarily, though, you make your writing heavier and more opaque." He closes with an aphorism: "remember it wasn't the last straw that broke the camel's back — it was the accumulation of all the straws. Don't accumulate straws" (p. 144).

**Evidence and examples.** All examples appear inline above under Mechanism, reproduced verbatim with their revisions: the John-called-Jane / Jane-was-called-by-John diagram (p. 134); the salvage-logging passive sentence reused from example 13.3 (p. 135); examples 14.1–14.12 in full with their revised versions (pp. 135–143); the political cliché "mistakes were made" (p. 135); the Materials-and-Methods "samples were collected" case (p. 136); the five named nominalized concepts (p. 143); and the Churchill rescue-and-liberation sentence reused from example 12.1 with its active-voice alternative (p. 143). Quoted authorities: Mark Twain (epigraph, p. 133), Strunk and White (p. 134 and p. 137), Stephen King, *On Writing* (p. 134), and S. R. Leather, "The Case for the Passive Voice," *Nature* 381 (1996): 467 (p. 137).

The chapter carries three tables: Table 14.1, "Fuzzy verbs versus action verbs" (p. 139); Table 14.2, "Verbs and their nominalized equivalents," including the same-form pairs *influence/an influence*, *approach/an approach*, and *yield/the yield* (p. 141); and Table 14.3, "Adjective nominalizations" (p. 142). Each is transcribed above at the point the chapter refers to it.

**Exercises.**

1. **14.1. Analyze published papers.** Look at the papers you have been analyzing and read the critical paragraphs that define the opening, action, and resolution. Evaluate the actions and the verbs. Do the authors put the action in their verbs? Do they use active verbs? If not, try rewriting those paragraphs using stronger verbs. (p. 144).
2. **14.2. Write a short article.** Go back to your short article. Go through it sentence by sentence, noting the actions you describe and the verbs you use. Is every action in an active verb? If not, can you convert them into active verbs? If you choose to leave any action as anything but an active verb, justify your choice. (p. 144).
3. **14.3. Revise.** A. Increased mobility of predatory nematodes in soil would increase opportunities for ecological interactions and so alter bacterial population dynamics. B. Polyaromatic hydrocarbons and polychlorinated biphenyls present enormous challenges in remediation, invoking large financial costs and presenting significant health risks to the workers who face exposure to the compounds. C. It was demonstrated that extraction of soils by NH4Cl caused an enhancement in the recovery of Al relative to an extraction with K2SO4. (p. 144) — answered in Appendix A.

**Terms introduced.**
- **Active voice** — the sentence structure matching OCAR directly: actor as subject, action as verb, acted-on as object, e.g., "John called Jane." Described as clear, concise, direct, visual, and evocative (p. 134).
- **Passive voice** — constructed by making the acted-on the sentence's subject and coupling a form of "to be" to the action verb (e.g., "was called"); does not change the underlying action, only which term occupies the subject/topic position (p. 134).
- **Topic** and **stress** — reused from chapter 13: the passive lets a writer "control what goes in the topic and what goes in the stress" (p. 134).
- **Fuzzy verb** — a verb that tells the reader something happened without showing what happened or how (e.g., "evaluated," "facilitate," "mediate," "is controlled") (pp. 137–139).
- **Nominalization** — turning a verb into a noun to name the action rather than perform it, forcing the sentence to rely on a separate, weaker verb (e.g., "conducted an investigation" instead of "investigated") (p. 140).
- **Adjective nominalization** — the same process applied to adjectives, converting them into nouns (e.g., "difference" for "different," "characteristics" for "characterized") (p. 142).
- **Schema** — the shared conceptual structure a nominalized term invokes; naming a concept "defines a new schema," which functions as efficient shorthand for a reader who holds it and as jargon for a reader who doesn't (p. 143).

**Connections.** Builds on chapter 13's OCAR structure and its example 13.3, reused here for the passive-voice discussion of controlling perspective (p. 135). Reuses the Churchill sentence from example 12.1 (p. 143). Refers back to chapter 2's discussion of Anne Lamott's advice on listening to one's data as the true source of objectivity (p. 137). Refers to examples 5.8 and 7.1 as instances of color in science writing (p. 137). Feeds forward into the chapter's own exercises, which apply the active/passive, fuzzy-verb, and nominalization diagnostics to the reader's own published-paper analysis and short article (exercises 14.1 and 14.2), and to three unrevised sentences the reader is asked to fix directly (exercise 14.3, parts A–C) (p. 144).

**Exercises** (p. 144):

- **14.1 Analyze published papers.** Return to the papers already being analyzed and read the critical paragraphs defining the opening, action, and resolution. Evaluate the actions and the verbs: do the authors put the action in their verbs? Do they use active verbs? If not, try rewriting those paragraphs using stronger verbs.
- **14.2 Write a short article.** Return to the reader's own short article. Go through it sentence by sentence, noting the actions described and the verbs used. Is every action in an active verb? If not, can they be converted to active verbs? If any action is left as anything other than an active verb, justify the choice.
- **14.3 Revise.** Revise each of the following sentences:
  - A. Increased mobility of predatory nematodes in soil would increase opportunities for ecological interactions and so alter bacterial population dynamics.
  - B. Polyaromatic hydrocarbons and polychlorinated biphenyls present enormous challenges in remediation, invoking large financial costs and presenting significant health risks to the workers who face exposure to the compounds.
  - C. It was demonstrated that extraction of soils by NH₄Cl caused an enhancement in the recovery of Al relative to an extraction with K₂SO₄.


### Chapter 15 — Words

**Claim.** Word choice, not just sentence structure, controls the "chemistry and voice" of scientific writing (p. 145). Jargon, unnecessarily technical terms, heavy Latinate vocabulary, and clunky prepositional phrases all impose costs on readers; each has a lighter, clearer, usually shorter alternative, and the chapter gives criteria for choosing between them.

The chapter opens with an epigraph attributed to George Bernard Shaw: "In literature, the ambition of the novice is to acquire the literary language; the struggle of the adept is to get rid of it" (p. 145).

**Mechanism.**

*Why academics write "heavy."* Schimel gives two causes: academics imitate the heavy style they read, which becomes ingrained habit and also signals club membership (p. 146); and written English is inherently more formal than spoken English, pushing writers toward longer, more elaborate words (p. 146). His test for whether a word is too heavy: "would I use it if I were talking to a friend?" (p. 146). He contrasts "the therapy was efficacious" and "students with different learning modalities" with the plainer "the treatment worked" and "students with different learning styles" (p. 146).

*15.1 Jargon — definitions.* Schimel gives explicit two-part definitions distinguishing jargon from technical terms:

> Jargon: (A) A term that refers to a schema the reader does not hold. (B) A term for which there is an adequate plain language equivalent.
>
> Technical term: (A) A term that refers to a schema the reader does hold. (B) A term for which either there is no plain language equivalent or where using it would be confusing. (p. 147)

He notes this distinction is "fuzzy and fluid" and depends on the reader's knowledge — a word can be jargon for one audience and a technical term for another, and defining an already-familiar term (e.g., "mole" for a chemist, "gene" for a molecular biologist) can make a writer appear ignorant (p. 147). Worked illustration: "net primary production" (NPP) would confuse a public audience (so say "plant growth"), but "plant growth" said to ecologists would itself be ambiguous, since it could mean NPP, gross primary production, net ecosystem production, or another measure (p. 147).

*15.1.1 Avoiding jargon — sentence position.* Schimel ties jargon perception to the old/new information (topic/stress) structure covered in chapter 12: introducing a term in the topic position implies readers already know it and risks it reading as jargon; introducing it in the stress "dejargonizes" it, making it feel defined — which may be good or may be overkill (p. 147). He references his earlier illustration of this in example 12.3 (N mineralization).

He presents two examples of authors placing a term in a middle position to remind, rather than fully define, readers:

> Example 15.1
> This idea that excited states relax with rates determined by the solute-solvent system's ordinary energy fluctuations, commonly called linear response theory, is a critical component in the success of transition-state theories of chemical reaction rates in liquids. (p. 148)

Schimel explains that a reader with basic chemistry background can infer the meaning of "linear response theory" from the surrounding topic content, while a physical chemist who already knows the theory experiences the phrase as "a comfortable reminder" rather than a definition. He attributes the effect to Clark's 2-3-1 rule of emphasis (introduced in chapter 12): the term's name sits in the stress of its own clause, set off by commas in the middle of the sentence, giving it local emphasis while limiting overall emphasis (p. 148). He notes the sentence is 37 words — longer than most reading-ease calculators recommend — but that the length made comprehension easier, not harder (p. 148).

> Example 15.2
> Programmed cell death, or apoptosis, is prominent in neural progenitors and appears to play an important role in the development of the cerebral cortex. (p. 148)

Here "apoptosis" sits in a short parenthetical clause (the 3-position of the 2-3-1 rule) that reminds readers of the term without feeling like a full definition (p. 148).

From these examples Schimel generalizes a three-way pattern for where a technical term can be placed in a sentence:

> Beginning of the sentence: You assume that every reader knows and understands the term. You run the risk of it appearing to be jargon if they don't.
>
> End of the sentence: You define a new term for everyone. You run the risk of appearing ignorant if it is already an accepted schema in the field.
>
> Middle of the sentence: You assume that most readers know the term. You are also indicating that the term itself isn't critical to your story. You run the risk of people missing the term. (p. 149)

His guidance: there is no single correct placement — evaluate the audience — and "if you err, err on the side of overdefining," since an expert's irritation at an unnecessary definition is "slight and short-lived" while a novice's confusion from a missing definition "could be large and permanent — they might stop reading your paper" (p. 149).

*15.1.2 Jargon and acronyms.* Schimel calls undefined abbreviations and acronyms "the worst form of jargon," worse than unfamiliar words because at least a word can be looked up (p. 149). His example: a paper opening with "DCs are APCs that initiate immunity." He could infer DC (dendritic cell) from the paper's title, but had to search online to learn APC meant "antigen presenting cell," noting the ambiguity with "armored personnel carrier" (p. 149). He invokes a style rule:

> "terms must be spelled out on their first occurrence." (Chicago Manual of Style, quoted p. 149)
