His preferred fix: "Dendritic cells (DCs) are antigen presenting cells (APCs) that initiate immunity" (p. 149) — costing a few extra words but not offending experts (who don't need the definition) while helping novices. The stated exception to the spell-out-on-first-use rule: abbreviations so common that every reader knows them, such as DNA — "your aunt knows what DNA is but would be baffled by deoxyribonucleic acid" (p. 149–150).

Schimel extends this to homemade shorthand: researchers create acronyms for their own convenience and then wrongly assume they are obvious to readers. He restates principle 1 (from earlier in the book) — make the reader's job easy — and gives an example: labeling two study forests by their type, "DEC" and "CON" (deciduous, coniferous), rather than by sample-site names such as "ASP" and "HBR" (p. 150).

*15.2 Unnecessarily technical.* A "lesser evil" than true jargon: using words readers do know, where a plainer word would communicate more powerfully — often from being overly specific, and as a result undercommunicative (p. 150).

> Example 15.3
> Current models suggest that climate warming could release 200 times more nitrogen from soils than is taken up annually by terrestrial autotrophs. (p. 150)

Schimel's analysis: the sentence argues potential N release is huge, but "terrestrial autotrophs" weakens the message. Plants are only a subset of autotrophs (lichens and algae also take up N), so "200 × terrestrial autotrophs" is actually larger than "200 × plants" — yet "200 times the amount of nitrogen taken up annually by plants" would have been the stronger, more powerful phrasing, because the common word engages a stronger schema (p. 150).

> Example 15.4
> California has a Mediterranean climate regime, in which the heaviest storms occur when moist subtropical air is entrained by major Pacific storms. (p. 150)

Schimel's target word is "regime": a regime is a pattern of conditions, and climate is already a pattern of weather conditions, so "climate regime" adds nothing beyond "climate" — it merely sounds more technical (p. 150). A footnote qualifies this: climatologists do use "regime" as a technical term in phrases like "regime shift" (e.g., a shift in the Pacific decadal oscillation), but that specific, load-bearing sense is not the one at work in this sentence — here it is "putting on a more complex implication to clutter a simple term" (p. 150).

> Example 15.5
> This suggests that SRT may be a causative agent of chronic pain syndrome (CPS). (p. 151)

Schimel's fix: "SRT may cause chronic pain syndrome (CPS)." He attributes the original phrasing to training that makes writers think noun expressions sound more specific or technical than action verbs, when they are not — "SRT is an 'agent,' so identifying it as one adds nothing" (p. 151).

*15.3 Emotional weight.* Word origin carries emotional connotation. Academic English draws on three main sources — Anglo-Saxon Old English (the peasants' language), Norman-French (the nobles', arriving with William the Conqueror), and Latin (the scholars') — and that historical class legacy persists: Anglo-Saxon words feel comfortable and casual, French words feel formal, Latin words "inevitably feel like jargon; they were originally coined to show off the writer's education" (p. 151).

Worked illustration from Schimel's own field: "soil" (French) versus "dirt" (Anglo-Saxon, from Old Norse "drit," meaning excrement). Soil carries positive connotations ("soil grows plants"); dirt is short, clipped, and generally negative — "calling something 'dirty' is always an insult" — and still carries emotional residue from its etymology (p. 151).

#### Table 15.1 (p. 152)

**Caption.** *Table 15.1.* Examples of long French/Latin vs. short Anglo-Saxon words

| Long French or Latin Word | Short, Anglo-Saxon Word (unless otherwise noted) |
| --- | --- |
| Duration (French.) | Length or time |
| Consume (French) | Eat |
| Mortality (French) | Death |
| Permit (French) | Let |
| Necessary (French) | Need |
| Demonstrate (Latin) | Show |
| Donate (Latin) | Give |
| Initiate (Latin) | Start |
| Attempt (French) | Try (from Old French *trier*) |
| Utilize (French) | Use (from Old French *user*) |
| Methodology (Latin combined form) | Method (Latin borrowed into English) |

The caption is printed with "Table 15.1." in italics and the rest in small capitals. The second column head is set on two lines, "Short, Anglo-Saxon Word" over "(unless otherwise noted)". The first data row prints "Duration (French.)" with a period inside the parenthesis, unlike the other rows.

Schimel notes Table 15.1's Anglo-Saxon alternatives are not only emotionally lighter but usually shorter, and that even among French-derived pairs, the word assimilated into English earlier tends to be shorter and feel more common (p. 151).

He gives a spelling heuristic: "words ending in -ate are derived from Latin and sound heavy and full of themselves. Words ending with -ion are French." He recommends consulting the Oxford English Dictionary for word origin when uncertain, and observes that all the "fuzzy verbs" listed in the previous chapter (chapter 14) are French or Latin — because common, concrete language draws on Anglo-Saxon roots, so scholars reaching for vague, abstract verbs naturally reach for Latin (p. 152).

> Example 15.6
> We performed a study of six-months duration on the mortality rate of rats following exposure to elevated levels of lead. [20 words/119 characters] (p. 152)

Revision: "We did a six-month-long study of the death rate of rats exposed to high levels of lead. [18 words/84 characters]" (p. 152). Schimel's point: the two sentences say the same thing, but the revision is easier to read and 20 percent shorter — and proposals have page limits that make that difference matter (p. 152).

Schimel qualifies the Anglo-Saxon-over-Latin/French preference: sometimes the heavier word carries a different, needed connotation. Returning to example 14.6 (herbivores and exotic grasses), he had suggested "Herbivores preferentially eat native plants," while many writers would default to "consume" because "eat" feels too visceral for technical writing — but he argues eat and consume are synonyms in that context, so the shorter word should win (p. 152). He contrasts a context where the words are not interchangeable: a fire "consumed the fuel" (consume having taken the sense "to destroy" from Latin) but a fire cannot have "eaten the fuel," since eating implies mouths and nutrition — "animals eat, fires don't" (p. 152–153). A further alternative, "Herbivores preferentially forage on . . . ," is rejected as not a plain synonym either: "forage" implies "to collect from," carrying a hunting-strategy connotation rather than a simple taste preference; Schimel advises using the longer, more nuanced word only when that nuance is actually wanted, and cautions that some readers may not catch the distinction at all (p. 153).

On synonym selection generally: a thesaurus is useful but must be checked against a dictionary, because listed synonyms can carry different implications — his example is "consistent" and "coherent," which his thesaurus lists as primary synonyms for each other even though "consistent" suggests constancy (sometimes undesirably so), unlike "coherent." He quotes Ralph Waldo Emerson: "A foolish consistency is the hobgoblin of little minds" (p. 153).

*15.4 Prepositional phrases versus compound nouns.* Schimel defines a prepositional phrase as an object plus a modifier joined by a preposition (e.g., "rate of reaction"), contrasted with a compound noun, where one noun directly modifies another (e.g., "reaction rate"). Prepositional phrases are usually "nasty — longer and clunkier than the compound noun," and have "a strange attraction for nominalizations and passive verbs" (p. 153).

#### Table 15.2 (p. 154)

**Caption.** *Table 15.2.* Prepositional phrases vs. compound nouns

| Prepositional Phrase | Compound Noun |
| --- | --- |
| Source of water | Water source |
| Supply of nitrogen | Nitrogen supply |
| Distribution of resources | Resource distribution |
| Kinetics of enzymes | Enzyme kinetics |
| Burning of fossil fuels | Fossil fuel burning |
| Cancer of the lung | Lung cancer |

The caption is printed with "Table 15.2." in italics and the rest in small capitals. The table sits entirely on printed page 154; it does not run across a page break.

Schimel speculates why writers default to the prepositional form anyway: some think it sounds more precise; some were taught to avoid compound nouns categorically (rather than only when they cause actual problems); and some simply write carelessly in first drafts, as he says he himself often does (p. 153). He argues the compound noun is usually better, noting that English already treats many compounds as unbreakable — "stone wall," "science fiction," "Air Force," "benzene ring," "nitrogen fixation" (p. 153).

> Example 15.7
> A. The rate of the reaction increased sixfold when pH was decreased to 4.5.
> B. The reaction rate increased sixfold when pH was decreased to 4.5. (p. 153)

> Example 15.8
> A. This paper presents a new procedure for synthesizing complexes of iron and benzoate.
> B. This paper presents a new procedure for synthesizing iron-benzoate complexes. (p. 154)

> Example 15.9
> A. Assembly is a stepwise process, starting with binding of Red22 to the coding region followed by binding of Red25 and Blu17 to the control region.
> B. Assembly is a stepwise process, starting with Red22 binding to the coding region followed by Red25 and Blu17 binding to the control region. (p. 154)

Schimel's analysis: in each pair the B version is shorter and tighter; in example 15.9 specifically, flipping the prepositional phrase also converts "binding" from a nominalization back into a verb — "a double win" (p. 154).

*15.4.1 When to leave a prepositional phrase.* Schimel qualifies that prepositional phrases have value too, using a case where removing one goes wrong:

> Example 15.10
> These results suggest that modification of resource allocation allowed Vaccinium . . . (p. 154)

Removing the phrase yields "These results suggest that resource allocation modification allowed Vaccinium . . . " — which Schimel calls "a jumbled mouthful of words," worse for being nominalizations stacked on nominalizations (p. 154–155). He terms such overdone strings "noun clusters," and credits his colleague Ruth Yanai with a term he prefers, "noun trains" — calling it "a lovely term" (p. 155). Noun trains, he states, are worse than prepositional phrases, and can be broken up with an occasional preposition.

His decision rule for compound versus broken-up phrasing: with only two nouns, a compound is almost certainly better; with four nouns, break it up; three nouns is "trickier" — he contrasts the awkward "resource allocation modification" with the acceptable "science fiction writer" (p. 155). Two factors determine which three-noun strings work: the complexity of the individual words (big words strung together form "an undigestible mass"), and whether readers intuitively lump two of the words into a single existing unit — "science fiction" is read as one unit, so "science fiction writer" reads as only two units, while "growth allocation modification" reads as three separate, awkward units (p. 155).

Schimel gives an ambiguity example for extended noun trains: "Arctic system science" could mean either the science of studying the Arctic system, or system science conducted in the Arctic — a distinction he states "is not a purely semantic debate" because it "has at times controlled major research directions and funding decisions by the National Science Foundation" (p. 155).

A noun train can also create noun/verb ambiguity:

> Example 15.11
> Current theory suggests that microbial community composition influences are most likely to be observed for physiologically narrow processes. (p. 155)

Schimel explains "influences" is a nominalization here, but could equally be a verb (as in "microbial composition influences soil processes"); a reader has roughly a 50 percent chance of guessing wrong on first pass and having to back up and reinterpret once the next word breaks the expected pattern. His fix reintroduces a prepositional phrase for clarity: "Current theory suggests that the influences of microbial community composition are most likely to be observed for physiologically narrow processes" (p. 155).

A further use of the prepositional phrase: controlling which word lands in the sentence's stress position.

> Example 15.12
> A. Ecosystems can be managed to limit the effects of global warming.
> B. Ecosystems can be managed to limit the global warming effects. (p. 155)

Schimel prefers A, because it puts the strong phrase "global warming" into the stress position (p. 156).

As a final example, Schimel returns to example 7.3 (signaling in visual transduction) and its sentence: "Despite the tantalizing evidence for DAG and/or its downstream products in visual transduction and the synergistic role of calcium, in no instance has application of such chemical stimuli fully reproduced the remarkable size and speed of the photocurrent" (p. 156). He had earlier praised this sentence's topic/stress control, putting emphasis on "remarkable size and speed of the photocurrent." He now flags what the authors paid for that effect: "in no instance has application of such chemical stimuli reproduced" is, in his words, "a passive-feeling, nominalized, prepositional phrase — the verb is 'has,' and the action is the nominalization 'application.' That's a lot of no-no's packed into a mere six words. But it worked" (p. 156). He contrasts the alternative, "Applying such chemical stimuli has never fully reproduced . . . ," which turns "application" into the verb "applying" but pushes the critical word "never" into the middle of the clause; the authors instead put "never" at the front of the clause for emphasis, using the 2–3–1 rule within the clause itself. Schimel's conclusion: "Breaking some of the rules allowed the authors to put the right information in the right place to make the story flow" (p. 156).

Chapter close: "This chapter covers only a selection of issues involved in choosing words to write clearly and engagingly, but it illustrates the principles. You are working to become an adept, so struggle to get rid of the literary language. Use the necessary technical terms, but avoid unnecessary jargon — and be aware of the difference!" (p. 156).

**Evidence and examples.** All twelve numbered examples are reproduced above under Mechanism at the point each is introduced: 15.1 (linear response theory, p. 148), 15.2 (apoptosis, p. 148), 15.3 (terrestrial autotrophs, p. 150), 15.4 (climate regime, p. 150), 15.5 (SRT/causative agent, p. 151), 15.6 (rats/lead study, before and after, p. 152), 15.7 (rate of reaction / reaction rate, A/B, p. 153), 15.8 (complexes of iron and benzoate / iron-benzoate complexes, A/B, p. 154), 15.9 (binding of Red22 / Red22 binding, A/B, p. 154), 15.10 (modification of resource allocation, p. 154), 15.11 (microbial community composition influences, p. 155), and 15.12 (effects of global warming / global warming effects, A/B, p. 155). The chapter also revisits two examples from earlier chapters without renumbering them: example 12.3 (N mineralization, referenced for topic/stress placement, p. 147) and example 14.6 (herbivores and exotic grasses, revisited for eat/consume/forage, p. 152), plus the DAG/visual transduction sentence from example 7.3 (p. 156). Two supporting tables are referenced, both reproduced above under Mechanism at the point each is introduced: Table 15.1 (long French/Latin words vs. short Anglo-Saxon equivalents, p. 151) and Table 15.2 (prepositional phrases vs. compound nouns, p. 153).

**Terms introduced.**

- **Jargon.** "(A) A term that refers to a schema the reader does not hold. (B) A term for which there is an adequate plain language equivalent" (p. 147).
- **Technical term.** "(A) A term that refers to a schema the reader does hold. (B) A term for which either there is no plain language equivalent or where using it would be confusing" (p. 147).
- **Noun cluster / noun train.** An overdone string of nouns stacked as modifiers of one another (e.g., "resource allocation modification"); Schimel uses his colleague Ruth Yanai's term "noun train," calling it "a lovely term" (p. 155). Noun trains are described as worse than prepositional phrases and can be broken up with an occasional preposition.
- **2-3-1 rule of emphasis** (Clark's rule, introduced in chapter 12, reapplied here): placing a term in the stress of a subordinate clause in the middle of a sentence gives it local emphasis while limiting its overall prominence (pp. 148, 156).
- **Compound noun** vs. **prepositional phrase**: a compound noun is one noun directly modifying another (e.g., "reaction rate"); a prepositional phrase joins an object and a modifier with a preposition (e.g., "rate of reaction") (p. 153).

**Connections.** Builds on chapter 11's atoms/molecules analogy for words and sentences (p. 145) and chapter 12's topic/stress sentence structure and Clark's 2-3-1 rule of emphasis, including a direct callback to example 12.3 (p. 147). Revisits example 14.6 from chapter 14 and identifies chapter 14's "fuzzy verbs" as predominantly French or Latin in origin (p. 152). Returns to example 7.3 from chapter 7 to analyze its closing prepositional-phrase construction (p. 156). Feeds directly into exercises 15.1–15.3, which ask readers to apply the chapter's jargon, word-length, and prepositional-phrase tests to their own reading and writing.

**Exercises.**

1. **15.1, Analyze published papers** (p. 156): Take a paragraph or two from papers the reader has been reading and analyze the authors' word choices against each issue raised in the chapter, checking whether the writing can be lightened by avoiding jargon, picking shorter words, and eliminating prepositional phrases.
2. **15.2, Write a short article** (p. 156): Go through the reader's own short article and lighten the word choices wherever possible, checking specifically for jargon, word length, and prepositional phrases.
3. **15.3, Revise** (p. 157): Lighten up the following sentences.

> A. The ability of animals to arrive at solutions to problems has been undervalued because studies have not been done that are considered to have scientific reliability.
>
> B. Rats that had been maintained under varying environmental conditions demonstrated improved cognitive ability relative to the control group, which had been maintained in conditions that were invariant. (p. 157)


### Chapter 16 — Condensing

> Brevity is the soul of wit.
> — Shakespeare, *Hamlet* (p. 158)

> The Project Description may not exceed 15 pages.
> — National Science Foundation Grant Proposal Guide (p. 158)

**Claim.** Compact writing is a matter of professional survival, not style. Papers without formal page limits still lose readers when ideas are buried in cumbersome sentences and extraneous information, risking extra revision rounds or outright rejection; proposals face hard limits — the National Science Foundation will not read a Project Description over 15 pages. Two approaches shrink a manuscript to fit: tightening the ideas and language, a skill that takes time to develop, or resorting to formatting tricks (smaller fonts, hypotheses packed into paragraphs instead of bulleted lists, undersized figures). The book treats the second approach as illegitimate: it can force the words onto the page, but produces text like Figure 16.1, a dense mass a reader must struggle through, and reviewers notice. Schimel states that he has never encountered writing that could not be streamlined or condensed without cutting substantive content — dense writing reflects a lack of skill or effort to condense, not necessity.

#### Figure 16.1 (p. 159)

**Caption.** Figure 16.1. A blurred page from a densely packed proposal.

**What it shows.** A photographic reproduction of a single full page of a proposal, reproduced out of focus so that the text is a gray texture rather than readable words. The blur is uniform across the whole image and is deliberate — no individual word on the page can be read, and none is transcribed here.

Layout that survives the blur: one justified column running nearly the full width of the figure, with no headings, no figures, no tables, no bulleted or numbered lists, and no white space other than the thin gaps between paragraphs. The column is broken into three paragraph blocks:

- Block 1: 11 lines, flush at both margins, first line indented like the other two blocks.
- Block 2: 17 lines. Its first line begins with a short run-in phrase set in italic (roughly two words, the second followed by punctuation) before the roman text resumes on the same line.
- Block 3: 20 lines, running to the foot of the text block. It likewise opens with an italic run-in phrase (roughly two words plus punctuation) before the roman text continues.

The type is noticeably smaller relative to the page than the type of the book page carrying the figure — the lines are tightly leaded and the paragraphs sit hard against each other. Scattered through the blocks are short parenthetical clusters whose shape reads as literature citations (name-and-year form) and occasional runs whose shape reads as numerals with units, but the characters themselves are not resolvable.

At the bottom center, well below the last line of text, is a single blurred numeral — a page number. It is one digit and too blurred to identify with confidence.

**What it demonstrates.** That a proposal squeezed onto its page limit by formatting tricks — smaller font, hypotheses folded into paragraphs instead of bulleted lists, shrunken figures — arrives at the reader as an undifferentiated dense mass. Blurring the page removes the words and leaves only what a reader takes in at first glance, and at that glance there is no structure to enter by: no headings, no lists, no white space, nothing that signals where an idea starts or whether the ideas are worth the struggle.

**Mechanism.** Condensing proceeds in two stages, following Roy Peter Clark's advice in *Writing Tools*:

> "Prune the big limbs, then shake out the dead leaves." (p. 160)

Clark elaborates: "brevity comes from selection, not compression, a lesson that requires lifting blocks from the work." (p. 160)

Pruning the big limbs means deciding what not to say, which grows directly out of SUCCES (chapter 3) and the simple story (chapter 8, e.g., figure 8.1): once the story is clear, what belongs is clear, and the rest goes.

Shaking out the dead leaves means cutting unnecessary words from what remains, in steps. First, build good, complete story arcs (chapter 10): broken arcs force repetition when an idea is discussed in multiple places, and cleaning up the arcs eliminates both the repeats and the transition words needed to reconnect them — figure 10.2 showed that three complete arcs need only 2 internal transitions, while the same material fragmented needed 13; chapter 13 noted that linking ideas and developing flow often requires *adding* words, so unnecessary transitions are pure waste. Second, work with the delete key: every word should add content, clarify meaning, or provide coherence, and most documents carry filler that does none of these. Schimel cites Stephen King's rule, "Formula: second draft = first draft – 10 percent" (p. 161), and reports counting on trimming 20 percent from his own first drafts. His practical trick for tightening proposals to a page limit: scan every paragraph for a word or two hanging alone on the last line, and cut enough elsewhere to pull them back up into the body of the paragraph — killing one word can save a whole line.

The delete-key targets, in the order the chapter treats them (p. 161):

1. Redundancies
2. Obvious
3. Modifiers: adjectives and adverbs
4. Metadiscourse
5. Verbosity

A sixth target, verbs and action — putting actions into verbs rather than nominalizations — is treated in its own section (16.7) as a reminder of chapter 14's argument that active verbs are tight while passives, fuzzies, and nominalizations cost extra words.

Section 16.10 supplies two caveats against cutting every possible word. First, especially when working with coauthors, a first draft can afford to run a little verbose: it is easier to strip an overloaded draft than to fill the gaps in an underloaded one, because doing so requires knowing the data and the story as well as the writer does, and coauthors rarely do — "Don't be afraid to give them enough that their main work will be trimming" (p. 173). Second, words that build flow and coherence are not unnecessary; any word that helps a reader understand the message is doing useful work.

**Evidence and examples.**

*16.2. Redundancies (p. 161).* Sometimes several words duplicate work that one word already does.

Example 16.1 (p. 161):

> "I will develop, test, and apply a new synthetic approach to produce photovoltaic plastics."

Testing is part of developing, and "synthetic" and "produce" both refer to making things, so:

> "I will develop a new approach to produce photovoltaic plastics."

Example 16.2 (p. 161):

> "Most, but not all of the test subjects responded."

"Most" already means "the majority," hence "not all":

> "Most of the test subjects responded."

Example 16.3 (p. 161):

> "The effectiveness of these antibodies in HIV infection provides a proof-of-principle for the feasibility of using engineered antibodies as a novel therapy."

A proof-of-principle implies feasibility, so:

> "The effectiveness of these antibodies in HIV infection suggests the feasibility of using engineered antibodies as a novel therapy."

Example 16.4 (p. 162), where redundancy spans two sentences and collapsing it means collapsing the sentences together:

> "The altered precipitation patterns associated with global warming will change the water regimes of most ecosystems, particularly those with arid, semi-arid, and Mediterranean climates. These dry environments currently comprise one third of the terrestrial land surface."

Collapsed:

> "Climate change will alter the water regimes of most ecosystems, particularly those in arid and semi-arid regions, which comprise roughly one third of the land surface."

Schimel explains: "climate change" collapses the first sentence's complexity into two words, since readers understand the term includes both warming and altered precipitation; simplifying the first sentence made room to integrate the second sentence's point and drop the transition words "These dry environments." "Mediterranean" was cut because it is a type of semi-arid climate and was only fleshing out the list — if it mattered specifically, it would be highlighted.

*16.3. Obvious (p. 162).* Obvious material is well known or implied and so doesn't need to be said, distinct from redundancy, which duplicates information already stated elsewhere in the passage.

Example 16.5 (p. 162):

> "There is evidence that X17-production can be associated with enzyme induction (Chu et al. 2008)."

If there weren't evidence, the author wouldn't have said it, and there wouldn't be a citation, so "There is evidence that" is obvious and can go:

> "X-17 production can be associated with enzyme induction (Chu et al. 2008)."

The author's likely intent — signaling the finding isn't confirmed — survives because "can" already carries that caveat; an unequivocal statement would read "X-17 production *is* associated . . ."

Example 16.6 (p. 163):

> "Snow cover is a characteristic of high alpine ecosystems that is critical in regulating both plant community dynamics and hydrology."

It's obvious that snow cover is a "characteristic," and alpine ecosystems are defined by being "high," so:

> "Snow cover in alpine ecosystems is critical in regulating both plant community dynamics and hydrology."

Example 16.7 (p. 163):

> "The greatest challenge in dealing with the crisis of a pandemic is that it is global in scope and so public health responses must operate across national borders."

A pandemic is by definition highly infectious and widespread — hence a crisis, and almost certainly international — so those ideas can stay implicit:

> "The greatest challenge in dealing with a pandemic is that public health responses must operate across national borders."

*16.4. Modifiers: adverbs and adjectives (p. 163).* Section epigraphs:

> "Write with nouns and verbs. . . . The adjective hasn't been built that can pull a weak or inaccurate noun out of a tight place."
> — Strunk and White, *The Elements of Style* (p. 163)

> "The Adverb is not your friend."
> — Stephen King, "On Writing" (p. 163)

Good words don't need modifying; strong, clear nouns and verbs give writing power that decorating weak words can't match.

Example 16.8 (p. 163):

> "The entire reaction sequence takes less than one hour to complete."

> "The reaction sequence takes less than one hour to complete."

Taken further: "The reaction sequence takes less than one hour." (p. 164)

Example 16.9 (p. 164), illustrating an adverb used unsuccessfully:

> "The treatment dramatically increased X."

"Dramatically" is fuzzy — a factor of 2, 20, or 200? Adding the concrete figure:

> "The treatment dramatically increased X by a factor of 42."

But once the factor of 42 is stated, the drama is obvious, so the adverb goes:

> "The treatment increased X by a factor of 42."

These are what Schimel calls "empty amplifiers" — they intensify their referent without adding meaning; deleting one leaves the important thought intact (Table 16.1).

#### Table 16.1 (p. 164)

**Caption.** Table 16.1. Empty amplifiers : Adjectives and adverbs that try to intensify their referent but add no meaning

With an "ly" these are adverbs; without "ly" they are adjectives.

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Certain(ly) | Quite | Substantial(ly) |
| Dramatic(ally) | Rather | Very |
| Entire(ly) | Real(ly) | |
| High(ly) | Simple(ly) | |

Example 16.10 (p. 164), where modifiers hide an empty thought rather than merely decorate:

> "The immune system uses a highly effective control mechanism that efficiently discriminates between self and nonself."

> "The immune system uses a control mechanism that discriminates between self and nonself."

Without the modifiers the sentence feels incomplete — what is the mechanism, how is it efficient — but decoration can never substitute for that missing content; the fix is to make the sentence more focused and concrete, not to restore the adjectives.

Example 16.11 (p. 165):

> "Thermal stress induces structural and functional changes in GTH-7."

"Structural and functional" encompasses all possible types of change — changes that almost inevitably go together — so it adds no concrete information:

> "Thermal stress induces changes in GTH-7."

That version now begs "what kind of changes?" — which exposes the emptiness of "structural and functional" rather than solving it. The fix integrates the specific nature of the change into one tight sentence:

> "Thermal stress alters the conformation of GTH-7's active site, reducing its affinity for GXP."

*16.4.1. Good modifiers (p. 165).* Some modifiers clarify or define their referent rather than merely reinforcing it, and cannot be deleted.

Example 16.12 (p. 166), a modification of Example 16.8:

> "The first phase of the reaction sequence takes less than one hour to complete."

"First" distinguishes one phase from others and provides essential information — it cannot be cut the way "entire" could.

Roy Peter Clark's illustration of the same distinction: we expect smiles to be happy, so "she smiled happily" is an empty amplifier, while "she smiled sadly" transforms the image entirely because we don't expect smiles to be sad. The scientific parallel: we assume results are final, so "final result" wastes words, but "preliminary result" signals real tentativeness — "final" could be deleted, "preliminary" could not (p. 166).

*16.5. Metadiscourse: talking about what you're doing (p. 166).* Metadiscourse is description of the author's own actions and thoughts rather than the material itself — "discussing the discussion." The chapter lists common examples (p. 166):

> "We found that . . .
> We argue that . . .
> Our initial hypothesis was that . . .
> These data may indicate . . .
> To conclude . . ."

Some metadiscourse is necessary to develop an argument's flow, but it is often obvious or redundant.

Example 16.13 (p. 166):

> "We found that aniline did not react with . . ."

These are new data, first reported here — who else could have found them?

> "Aniline did not react with . . ."

Example 16.14 (p. 167):

> "In this study, we measured Y . . ."

If Y were measured in a prior study, that would need stating ("In a previous study, we measured Y"); absent that, "in this study" is obvious:

> "We measured Y."

Cutting the metadiscourse also sidesteps the active/passive debate over describing one's own actions — it removes the need for the cumbersome passive "aniline was not found to react," producing text that is both shorter and cleaner: "Aniline did not react."

*16.6. Verbosity (p. 167).* Verbosity is the sum of multiple types of filler together, producing sentences that ramble; the book attributes it to authors who are insecure, afraid to make a definitive statement, or unable to separate their own mental process from the story.

Example 16.15 (p. 167), "a particularly egregious case":

> "The data show that some enhancement in the applicability of these measurements can be accomplished with freeze-fracture prior to analysis by laser-ablation mass spectrometry."

The 25-word original condenses to 11:

> "Freeze-fracture pretreatment improved analyses by laser-ablation mass spectrometry."

Example 16.16 (p. 167), verbose but not badly written — an attempt to limit how much is packed into one sentence, structured as an LD story with a general sentence followed by a detailing sentence:

> "Maximizing the yield of X requires both optimizing the pH and selecting an appropriate catalyst. The optimum pH range is narrow, between 4.5 and 5, while appropriate catalysts include Mn and Fe."

The two-sentence structure would be justified if the detail ran a full paragraph; since it doesn't, the sentences collapse in half:

> "Maximizing the yield of X requires a pH in the range of 4.5 to 5 and either a Mn or Fe catalyst."

*16.7. Verbs and action (p. 168).* Chapter 14 established that active verbs are tight while passives, fuzzies, and nominalizations require extra words; putting action into verbs is a tool for condensing.

Example 16.17 (p. 168):

> "Agents that can interfere with the binding of AS2 protein to DNA are capable of delaying the onset of ovarian cancer."

Converting the actions to verbs makes the sentence both shorter and stronger:

> "Agents that interfere with AS2 protein's binding to DNA can delay the onset of ovarian cancer."

*16.8. A full paragraph example (p. 168).* Schimel offers a 260-word paragraph he calls representative of decent first-draft writing, adapted from early drafts of several of his proposals, and works through condensing it sentence by sentence.

Example 16.18, original (p. 168):

> "A central dogma of ecology has long been that soil microorganisms must decompose organic matter, releasing inorganic N, before that N becomes available for plants to take up. In the arctic tundra, however, several lines of evidence have forced us to question the importance of microbial decomposition and inorganic N uptake by plants: 1) In these soils, microbes appear to take up enough inorganic N during the growing season that they leave inadequate supplies of N to support the N uptake needed to sustain measured plant growth. 2) The total annual net release of inorganic N by microbes is often half the value that is required to meet the demands of plant uptake, as estimated from plant harvests over the course of the growing season. 3) Several tundra plant species have been shown in lab studies to be able to take up amino acids from hydroponic solution, and can use the N to support growth. While these studies suggest that plants should take up amino acids and possibly other forms of organic N in the field, they do not provide conclusive evidence of this. Rather, amino acids are an excellent source of both C and N for soil microorganisms, which might be expected to outcompete plants for any free amino acids in natural soils, thus limiting the access of plants to these compounds. If tundra plants take up a significant amount of their N directly as amino acids, we must reevaluate our basic view of the central role of microbial breakdown of organic N to NH4+ in the tundra N-cycle."

Schimel then works through it sentence by sentence, showing his revision marks — struck wording immediately followed by its replacement, reproduced below in the order the text presents them (p. 168–170).

Sentence 1, redline (p. 169):

> "A central dogma of ecology Ecological dogma has long been that soil microorganisms must decompose organic matter, releasing inorganic N, before that N becomes available for plants to take up."

Explanation: "Dogma is always 'central,' and implies long duration, so we can tighten the opening. Plants don't do anything else but take N up, do they? Obvious." (p. 169)

Sentence 2, redline (p. 169):

> "In the arctic tundra, however, several lines of evidence challenge this. have forced us to question the importance of microbial decomposition and inorganic-N uptake by plants."

Explanation: "I deleted the metadiscourse, and instead of reiterating the decomposition/uptake concept, I encapsulated it in 'this.'" (p. 169)

Discussion before the next redline: "The next two sentences relate to microbial processes but would be unclear to a reader who is not a tundra ecologist. In the tundra, microbes take up inorganic N during the summer but release it during the winter. The distinction between 'growing season uptake' and 'total annual release,' however, might be unclear to other readers. It needs to be either clearer or unsaid. The important point is that soil microbes don't release enough inorganic N to support plant growth — that is what challenges the dogma. So capture the best parts of each weak sentence to make one strong one." (p. 169)

Numbered sentences 1) and 2), redline (p. 169):

> "1) In these soils, Microbes appear to take up enough inorganic N during the growing season that they leave inadequate supplies of N to support the N uptake needed to sustain measured plant growth. 2) The total annual net release of inorganic N by microbes is often half the value that is required to meet the demands of plant uptake, as estimated from plant harvests over the course of the growing season."

Collapsed to:

> "1) Microbes release only half the inorganic N required to support measured plant growth."

Explanation: "I took the idea of 'plant growth' from the first sentence, because it is a stronger concept than 'demands of plant uptake,' but I took the core message from the second — it was stronger." (p. 169)

Sentence 3), redline (p. 169), renumbered 2) in the revision:

> "3) 2) Several tundra plant species have been shown in lab studies to be able to can take up and grow on amino acids from hydroponic solution, and can use the N to support growth."

Explanation: "This was unnecessary detail that would be in a reference. I eliminated the metadiscourse 'have been shown' and collapsed the detailed explanation into the simple 'can.'" (p. 169)

Next sentence, redline (p. 169–170):

> "While these studies suggest that plants should take up amino-acids and possibly other forms of use organic N in the field, they do not provide are not conclusive evidence of this."

Explanation: "By condensing 'amino acids and other forms of organic N,' to 'organic N' I kept the important distinction of inorganic versus organic N. Condensing sharpens that message. In the last clause I cut the nominalization 'conclusive evidence' to leave 'conclusive' in the stress position." (p. 170)

Next sentence, redline (p. 170):

> "Rather, amino acids are an excellent source of both C and N for soil microorganisms, which might be expected to should outcompete plants for them. any free amino acids in natural soils, thus limiting the access of plants to these compounds."

Explanation: "The last phrase was implied — if microbes outcompete plants for amino acids, they necessarily limit plant access to them. This phrase may sound like it's adding information, but it isn't." (p. 170)

Final sentence, redline (p. 170):

> "If tundra plants take up a significant amount of their N directly as amino acids, we must reevaluate our basic view of the central role of microbial breakdown of organic N to NH4+ in the tundra N-cycle."

Explanation: "Since we are reevaluating it, it is necessarily 'our basic view.' Nature doesn't change as a result of research — only our perception of it does. This is unnecessary metadiscourse. 'Central' is an empty adjective. Now let's look at this condensed paragraph." (p. 170)

Example 16.18, fully condensed (p. 170):

> "Ecological dogma has been that soil microorganisms must decompose organic matter, releasing inorganic N, before that N becomes available for plants. In the arctic tundra, however, several lines of evidence challenge this: 1) Microbes release only half the inorganic N required to support measured plant growth. 2) Several tundra plant species can take up and grow on amino acids. While these studies suggest that plants use organic N in the field, they are not conclusive. Rather, amino acids are an excellent source of C and N for soil microorganisms, which should outcompete plants for them. If tundra plants take up a significant amount of amino acids, we must reevaluate the role of microbial breakdown of organic N to NH4+ in the tundra."

"This is only 122 words: over 50 percent reduction with no loss of information. Because it carries less baggage, the message shines brighter." (p. 170)

*16.9. Condensing to clarify (p. 170).* Stripping away excess can clarify a message that is confusing as well as long, by bringing into focus what the text does or doesn't say.

Example 16.19 (p. 171):

> "In the modern era of genomics, access to whole-genome sequence data is critical, but inadequate for the purpose of analyzing networks of physiological processes. The challenge is to effectively assimilate whole-genome sequence data based on objectively defined criteria in ways that facilitate interpretations and biological assessments."

Schimel's diagnosis: most readers' reaction is "Huh? What does that mean?" — a problem of language, not complexity, evidenced by a "massive deficiency of verbs (only four)" and a surfeit of nominalizations, violating "almost every rule on word choice." He marks the filler in the passage and discusses each item in a table (p. 171); the same passage, filler underscored, repeats before the collapsed version:

> "In the modern era of genomics, access to whole-genome sequence data is critical, but inadequate for the purpose of analyzing networks of physiological processes. The challenge is to effectively assimilate whole-genome sequence data based on objectively defined criteria in ways that facilitate interpretations and biological assessments."

Clearing the marked filler collapses this to a version roughly 40 percent shorter:

> "Access to whole-genome sequence data is critical, but inadequate for analyzing physiological networks. The challenge is to assimilate the data in ways that facilitate biological interpretation."

Schimel's item-by-item accounting of the filler he marked (p. 171):

- "In the modern era of genomics" — "We know what era we're in — this is cliché and pompous. It's also a prepositional phrase."
- "purpose of analyzing" — "Verbose, nominalized way of saying 'to analyze.'"
- "networks of physiological processes" — "Redundant and a prepositional phrase. Physiology implies process. This can be condensed to 'physiological networks.'"
- "effectively" — "Empty adjective: of course we want to assimilate data effectively."
- "whole-genome sequence data" (second occurrence) — "Repeats this phrase."
- "based on objectively defined criteria" — "Implied — we assume that criteria are objectively defined unless specified otherwise."
- "assessments" — "Since we don't know what kind of assessments, this says nothing."

Cutting this clutter raised the verb-to-word ratio to 4 out of 27 words — "a comfortable number" — and exposed a second problem: most of the remaining actions are nominalized rather than verbs (p. 172).

Schimel walks the shortened passage through two more diagnostic passes before the final rewrite (p. 172):

> "1. What are the actions?
> Access to whole-genome sequence data is critical, but inadequate for analyzing physiological networks. The challenge is to assimilate the data in ways that facilitate biological interpretation.
> 2. Where are the verbs?
> Access to whole-genome sequence data is critical, but inadequate for analyzing physiological networks. The challenge is to assimilate the data in ways that facilitate biological interpretation."

Only "to assimilate" and "facilitate" are verbs, and both are "heavy Latin words that don't show action"; the rest of the actions are nominalized. The passage is also depersonalized — critical to whom, challenging to whom — with, in Schimel's phrase, no "old lady screaming" anywhere in it (referring back to his rule from an earlier chapter that action needs a concrete actor). Putting the action into verbs and active voice yields:

> "To analyze physiological networks, we need whole-genome sequence data, but such data alone are inadequate. The challenge is to assimilate them in ways that allow better biological interpretations."

This version puts the actor, "we," in the topic position immediately followed by the action verb "need." Schimel shortened "facilitate" to the lighter "allow" but kept "assimilate" rather than shortening it to "use," because among modelers "assimilate" implies a suite of specific mathematical tools that "use" does not — even though the result runs two words and three characters longer than the previous version, "it reads more easily" and remains much shorter and more intelligible than the original (p. 172).

*16.10. When not to kill every possible word (p. 172).* To illustrate that words building flow and coherence are not unnecessary, Schimel notes he kept "entirely" in his discussion of empty amplifiers (section 16.5.1) and compares two short passages:

Example 16.20 (p. 173):

> "A. Writing can get stripped down to the point that it becomes barren. Some filler can add flow, so use it, but carefully.
> B. Writing can get too barren. Filler can add flow. Use it carefully."

His judgment: "I like the first. It's longer, but it reads well. The second is painful." (p. 173)

**Exercises (p. 173).**

16.1. Write a short article: Take the short article from the chapter 2 exercise (800–850 words) and condense it by at least 10 percent, to 720–765 words; go shorter if possible. Then edit a partner's article, cutting it by at least 10 percent, and compare whether the same edits were made.

16.2. Edit: condense the following —
- A. "Polyaromatic hydrocarbons and polychlorinated biphenyls are challenging to remediate: it costs a lot of money and it threatens the health of workers who are exposed to the compounds."
- B. "When expression of Chla and Chlb were compared, similar patterns of transcript abundance were observed in plants at different developmental stages."
- C. "Inherent resistance is an evolved response to living in environments that are constantly harsh. Inherent resistance doesn't require a plant to induce any specific physiological mechanism in response to an imposed stress. Rather plants that are inherently resistant are characterized by traits such as high root biomass, extensive chemical defenses, and relatively low maximum potential growth rates."

**Terms introduced.**

- *Prune, then shake* — Roy Peter Clark's two-stage condensing strategy, adopted as the chapter's organizing method: prune the big limbs (decide what not to say) and shake out the dead leaves (cut unnecessary words from what remains) (p. 160).
- *Empty amplifiers* — adjectives and adverbs that "try to intensify the word they are referring to but don't add meaning"; deleting one leaves the underlying thought intact (p. 164), catalogued in Table 16.1.
- *Good modifiers* — the counter-category to empty amplifiers: modifiers, such as "first" or "preliminary," that clarify or define their referent rather than merely reinforcing it, and cannot be deleted without losing information (p. 165–166).
- *Metadiscourse* — "discussing the discussion": description of the author's own actions or thoughts (e.g., "We found that . . .") rather than the material itself; some is necessary for argument flow, but it is often obvious or redundant and can be cut (p. 166).
- *Verbosity* — the sum of multiple types of filler together, producing rambling sentences, attributed to authors who are insecure, afraid to make a definitive statement, or unable to separate their own mental process from the story (p. 167).

**Connections.** Draws on chapter 3's SUCCES and chapter 8's simple story (figure 8.1) for deciding what to prune; on chapter 10's story arcs and figure 10.2 for eliminating repetition and unneeded transitions; on chapter 13's treatment of flow and transitions; and on chapter 14's case for active verbs over passives, fuzzies, and nominalizations, reprised directly in section 16.7 and in the Example 16.19 rewrite. The short-article exercise (16.1) continues the 800–850-word article assigned in chapter 2's exercises.


### Chapter 17 — Putting it All Together: Real Editing

**Claim.** Real revision does not happen in one pass. A working draft carries multiple, tangled problems at once — structural, clarity, flow, and language issues arrive mixed together — and untangling them requires cycling through the same piece repeatedly, each cycle attacking one layer of problem while incidentally exposing the next. The chapter states its epigraph from Joseph Pulitzer (p. 174):

> These ten or twenty lines might readily represent a whole day's hard work in the way of concentrated, intense thinking and revision, polish of style, weighing of words.

**Mechanism.** The chapter opens with a toxicology analogy (p. 174): contaminants rarely arrive one at a time — oily sludge may also carry PCBs, mercury, and lead — so decontamination requires a series of processes, each solving one problem but potentially creating another. Writing problems arrive the same way: "Broken story arcs travel with weak linkages; prepositional phrases with passive verbs; jargon with nominalizations." Disentangling a draft therefore requires bringing every tool from the book into play, across multiple passes, each solving one or two problems and sometimes unmasking others.

There are no simple rules for fixing multiple simultaneous problems, because every piece is unique, but there is a general order of operations (p. 174): start with the big structural issues and work down into finer and finer details of word use and style. Get the story right, make sure the OCAR elements are in place, and then work on the language. This gives the sequence **SCFL** (p. 175):

- **Structure**: get the structure of the story into shape.
- **Clarity**: ensure that your ideas are clear and concrete.
- **Flow**: make the ideas flow, linking one thought to the next.
- **Language**: make it sound good.

The chapter is explicit that these four passes are not clean and separable (p. 175): "You won't, however, be able to revise a piece by doing just four passes, each dealing with one issue. They overlap, and solving one usually requires considering others. Even while focusing on structure, you can't ignore clarity and language. If the topic is unclear, it's hard to define the structure, and a lack of clarity often results from weak language." The SCFL process may need to be repeated several times; clarifying language may force a rewrite of structure.

**Evidence and examples.** The chapter works through a single worked case, Example 17.1, the opening of a paper on how plants respond to varying light and how that affects community composition, taking it from a rough first draft through repeated SCFL passes to a finished sentence.

*Starting draft* (p. 175):

> Plant behavior in response to dynamic resource availability, such as changing light regimes, has been well studied. Adjustments made at the organelle to canopy level to modify light interception exemplify how responsive plants are at a range of scales to their resource environment.

*17.1 Structure (pp. 175–176).* Because this is a short paper opening, the main structural task is making sure it accurately frames the paper's direction; a secondary goal is making the sentences fit together. The first sentence sets up a story arc about plant behavior, its subject — but its verb, "has been studied," is passive, arrives at the end of the sentence, and is badly separated from its subject. Worse, it leaves the sentence's point ambiguous: is this a story about the history of science (that plant behavior has been studied), or a story about biology (what those studies revealed)? The authors needed to pick one direction and rewrite toward it. Two options (p. 175):

> A. History: Many studies have examined plant responses to varying resource availability.

> B. Biology: Plants use a range of approaches to respond to varying resource availability.

Each option uses an active verb directly following its subject, making the possible story direction of each version clear. The authors moved forward with B — the paper is about plant biology. The chapter notes (p. 176) that the authors also dropped the final clause of the original sentence, "such as changing light regimes," to tighten the sentence and leave "varying resource availability" in the stress position. It characterizes this first-sentence move as "a pawn push, positioning move" — a general opening statement that sets up the story rather than delivering its content.

*17.2 Clarity and Flow (pp. 176–177).* The second sentence of the original draft neither meshes with the new first sentence nor carries enough energy or clarity on its own. Clarity is identified as the bigger problem: the actions in the sentence — plants "adjust" and "respond" to varying resource levels — are never expressed as verbs. "Adjustments" is a nominalization; "responsive" is an adjective; the actual verb, "exemplify," misses the real story. Because of this weak language, the sentence isn't concrete: the reader wants to know not just where adjustments happen (organelle to canopy level) but what they are. A lesser clarity issue: the grammatical subject, "Adjustments . . . interception," runs twelve words, so the verb — though it technically follows the subject immediately — feels lost and disconnected.

The chapter then diagrams the topic-stress linkage between the two sentences (p. 176):

> Plant behavior . . . has been studied → Adjustments . . . resource environment
> Topic Stress Topic Stress

There is no connection between the stress of the first sentence and the topic of the second, even though the second is meant to illustrate the first's general point. The chapter states the two issues to fix in the second sentence (p. 176):

> Clarity: Activate the verb and fix the subject–verb connection. Make the action concrete: what are the adjustments?
> Flow: To connect to the opening sentence, make the topic relate to "a range of approaches to varying resource availability."

To do this, the authors moved the ideas of "exemplify" and "varying resource availability" to the front of the sentence and connected the subject (plants) to a clear active verb (modify), producing (p. 177):

> For example, as light varies, plants modify light interception by mechanisms that range from organelle-level physiological shifts to canopy-level changes in architecture.

Starting with "For example" signals that this sentence illustrates the first sentence's general claim, which in turn let the authors drop "such as changing light regimes" from the first sentence. The sentence then moves to "as light varies," picking up the "varying resource availability" theme that was the first sentence's stress — now linking stress to topic. The subject–verb pairing "plants modify," together with the brief specification of modification types at each level (physiology at the organelle, architecture at the canopy), makes the sentence concrete.

*17.3 Language (p. 177).* Structure, clarity, and flow were now fixed, but the language remained clumsy: the sentence repeated expressions, and the prepositional phrase "changes in architecture" felt awkward. Simply cutting the phrase would have produced "canopy-level architecture changes," a noun train, so instead the authors merged the two words expressing change — "shifts" and "changes" — into one, producing:

> For example, as light varies, plants modify light interception by mechanisms that range from shifts in organelle-level physiology to canopy-level architecture.

This still left an awkward "mechanisms that range from," a prepositional "shifts in," and repetition of "light" and "level." One more editing round fixed those:

> For example, as light varies, plants modify its interception by mechanisms working at different scales, from organelle physiology to canopy architecture.

This version eliminates a redundant "light" by substituting the possessive "its," and eliminates both the awkward "mechanisms that range from" and the repeated "level" by rewording to "mechanisms working at different scales." The chapter calls this version concrete — it identifies conceptually what the mechanisms are — and notes it has "some music to it," ending on a parallelism in the stress position. It breaks the sentence into three compact arcs (p. 177):

1. "For example, as light varies,"
2. "plants modify its interception by mechanisms working at different scales"
3. "[those scales range] from organelle physiology to canopy architecture."

The chapter maps these three arcs onto OCAR (p. 178): the first phrase opens, the second describes action, and the third resolves by stating what the mechanisms are. It draws the general point that "even within a sentence, getting structure right solves many problems and makes the rest much easier."

*Return to structure (p. 178).* Through the SCFL and OCAR passes, the sentence gained clarity and grace while losing words — without being dumbed down. At this point the sentences were strong and well connected, so the authors cycled back to the top of the SCFL checklist and revisited structure once more, prompting a fresh question: if the paper is only about responses to varying light, why keep the first sentence at all? The chapter frames dropping it in the same chess metaphor used earlier: "It's just pushing a pawn, so skip it and launch a queen." The finished opening (p. 178):

> As light varies, plants modify its interception by mechanisms working at different scales, from organelle physiology to canopy architecture.

The chapter reports the arc in raw numbers: this piece started at 42 words and finished at 19, "not one of them wasted."

**The rewriting loop and reading aloud (pp. 178–179).** The chapter generalizes from the example: this multistep SCFL process should be the norm. Every draft starts rough; the writer fixes one set of problems and exposes another, so "you fix one set of problems and expose another. So edit it again. And again. Until it reads effectively and gracefully." It offers one further technique, described as "one final secret weapon in revising, but it is best done in privacy" (p. 178): close the office door, print a clean copy, clear your mind, stand up, step away from the desk, and read the piece out loud — "Awkward expressions, breaks in flow, clunky words — your eyes may skip over them, but not your ears."

The chapter also explains a phenomenon familiar from working with an advisor, students, or peers (p. 178): a writer produces a draft, an editor edits it, the writer makes the changes, and then the editor edits their own edits — sometimes reverting text back to how the writer originally had it. The chapter attributes this not to inconsistency but to the same multi-pass necessity described throughout the chapter: "Every time you come back to a piece, you need to look at it afresh." Changes that seemed fine in isolation, scrawled on paper or typed in, may not actually work once seen in the context of the whole revised piece, or when read aloud; and changes elsewhere in a paragraph can require rewriting a specific sentence to fit the new structure. The chapter points back explicitly to "the section on 'Writing versus Rewriting' in chapter 1" (p. 178): writing is a process of experimentation and revision, with no single right answer. It closes on reassurance: the more this process is practiced, the easier it becomes, and it might even become fun.

**Exercises.**

17.1. Write a short article: take a short article of your own (or another project in progress) and apply the SCFL approach to analyzing and revising it; then take one of a peer's short articles and apply the SCFL approach to editing it.

**Terms introduced.**

- **SCFL** — the revision sequence: Structure (get the story's structure into shape), Clarity (ensure ideas are clear and concrete), Flow (link ideas from one to the next), Language (make it sound good) (p. 175). The chapter states the passes overlap and often must be repeated.

**Connections.** This chapter is explicitly a synthesis chapter, applying tools built throughout the book to one running example: OCAR (paper/sentence structure), subject–verb connection and nominalization (clarity), topic-stress linkage and the stress position (flow), and noun trains and word choice (language). It cites chapter 1's "Writing versus Rewriting" section directly (p. 178) as the underlying rationale for why repeated, multi-pass revision — rather than one-shot editing — is necessary.


### Chapter 18 — Dealing with Limitations

**Claim.** No research is perfect — every study has limitations and every data set has blemishes, and a paper or proposal must address these without letting them undermine its meaningful results. Schimel's central recommendation is to invert the common "yes, but" pattern (present the results and conclusions, then raise limitations last, so the limitation becomes the resolution and the paper's most powerful message) into a "but, yes" pattern: address limitations as early as possible, constrain the conclusions to fit within them, and end powerfully on the "yes."

**Mechanism.** The epigraph frames the chapter: "No plan survives contact with the enemy" (Helmut von Moltke the Elder, p. 180). Schimel's diagnosis of the common failure mode:

> Many writers address limitations using what I call a "yes, but" strategy, where they present the results and conclusions and then discuss their limitations. The most classic form of this is the "more research is needed" resolution. The problem with "yes, but" is that it puts the "but" last, making it the resolution and most powerful message. (p. 180)

The alternative:

> So what is the alternative to "yes, but"? Turn it around. Instead of "yes, but," use "but, yes." Deal with the limitations as early as possible, get that discussion out of the way, and then get on with developing a strong story. Constrain your conclusions to fit within the limitations but end with a "yes." (p. 180)

He states the payoff of the flip in three parts: it lets the paper end powerfully (better storytelling); it is easier for readers to follow, because they never have to back up and reprocess information once they hit a limitation buried later in the argument; and he judges it more honest, since it airs the limitations up front rather than burying them "like the fine print in an advertisement" (pp. 180–181). As a general rule, "earlier is better" for placing limitations (p. 181).

The chapter is then organized around where in a paper or proposal a limitation should be addressed, matching the type of limitation to the section:

*18.1 The Introduction — promise the story you will deliver.* Many apparent limitations are not flaws in the methods or data but a mismatch between the question asked and the methods used to answer it. Schimel's fix is to revise the question in the Introduction so the knowledge gap framed there is one the methods can actually fill (p. 181).

*18.2 Materials and Methods.* Limitations tied to experimental or analytical detail should be addressed immediately in the Methods, to prevent readers from either dismissing the work as too flawed to read further or growing distracted by unaddressed concerns (p. 182). In proposals this is even more critical than in papers: an unsympathetic, critical reviewer must not be given "any excuse to 'go negative'" (p. 183). Where a method has a real limitation, the writer should explain how the study gets around it or why it doesn't matter for the specific question; where something wasn't done, explain why (too expensive, unnecessary, etc.) (p. 183). Schimel adds a broader interpretive principle: "there is no such thing as a bad method. As long as it is done well, a method gives solid data and real information. The 'bad' part always relates to interpretation — a method may truly be bad for measuring one thing but good for something slightly different" (p. 184).

*18.3 Discussion.* Methodological detail belongs in the Methods; limitations that affect how the data should be *interpreted* belong in the Discussion. The rule for placement: avoid the "power positions" of the Discussion — its opening and its resolution — since those are reserved for the critical story points, and "if limitations are the critical point, why submit the paper?" Instead, find a convenient place early in the body of the Discussion, address the limitation briefly and honestly, and move on (p. 184).

- *18.3.1 An Initial Methodological Considerations Section.* On rare occasions — when the experimental approach is unusual or novel — it is necessary to break the "avoid power positions" rule and open the Discussion with a methods-considerations section, calibrating readers on the approach's strengths and weaknesses before presenting results, woven into the opening of the story arc (p. 186).
- *18.3.2 Within the Conclusions.* Limitations should never themselves be made the conclusion, but sometimes must be mentioned within it. The technique is a tightly defined "but, yes": frame the limitation as quickly as possible, then push on to what was learned (p. 187).

**Evidence and examples.**

*Example 18.1* (p. 181) — a hypothetical Introduction Schimel says he would reject without review:

> To determine the size of the bacterial population in soil, we plated bacteria on nutrient agar and counted colonies.

His objection: only about 1 percent of soil bacteria grow on agar plates, so plate counts cannot determine total soil bacterial population — microscopy or DNA analysis is required for that question.

*Example 18.2* (p. 181) — the same method, with the question narrowed to fit it:

> To determine the size of the bacterial population in soil that is able to respond to inputs of fresh, high-quality organic materials, we plated bacteria on nutrient agar and counted colonies.

For this narrower question the plating method is defensible — indeed the methods that would answer the first (broader) question, microscopy and DNA, would fail this one, since they can't show which bacteria would grow if fed. Schimel notes this reframing constrains the paper's scope but doesn't necessarily make it less interesting, and reiterates a principle from earlier in the book: "it is better to fill a small knowledge gap than none at all, which is what readers will think if questions and answers don't match" (p. 181).

*Example 18.3* (p. 182), from a paper on overwinter survival of snowshoe hares in northern Canada, describing an unreplicated predator-exclosure treatment:

> Our final two treatments increased hare survival by excluding predators. One 1-km² area was enclosed by chicken-wire and electrical fencing (predator-exclosure) from 1987 to 1996 to exclude terrestrial predators, primarily lynx (*Lynx canadensis* Kerr) and coyotes (*Canis latrans* Say).

The limitation: the design has no replication (one exclosure), which by traditional statistical logic makes it impossible to attribute effects confidently to the exclosure itself.

*Example 18.4* (p. 182), the same paper's response to that limitation:

> The predator-exclosure and predator-exclosure + food sites were not replicated, owing to the high costs of maintaining 8 km of electric fencing on a nearly daily basis for a decade. Ecologists are caught increasingly in the tension between the manipulations necessary to understand large-scale ecological processes and the impossibility of replicating large manipulations. Our control sites sampled a wide range of natural variation within our study area, increasing the likelihood that differences on the treatments were due to our manipulations rather than to underlying natural variation.

Schimel calls this "effective and unapologetic": the authors explain why replication was impossible, situate the problem within the larger practice of ecology (large-scale manipulations often can't be replicated), and explain how their control sites compensate. Having made the case once, they do not raise the issue again and move on to the results (pp. 182–183).

*Example 18.5*, "Potential pitfalls" (pp. 183–184), from Schimel's own resubmitted funding proposal to characterize soil organic matter (SOM) chemistry in arctic tundra (the first submission, which omitted this discussion, was rejected; reviewers hit on the methods' limitations):

> Potential pitfalls
>
> Each of the methods proposed has potential limitations, and the utility of several have been questioned. However, we believe that we have constrained the limitations adequately. We have done this by being careful in interpreting what the data actually tell you (e.g. isotope equilibration as a method for estimating microbial metabolites rather than the total active fraction) and in part by carefully integrating different approaches to complement each other. Thus we believe that this suite of methods will do a good job of characterizing the nature of available C and N across the tundra landscape.
>
> The isotope equilibration technique relies on several assumptions. As originally construed, it required the assumption that it labels the active fraction without labeling the recalcitrant fraction. This is debatable since some microbial products are recalcitrant. However, the assumption that it labels the microbial metabolite pool is substantially more robust. There is rarely extensive abiotic immobilization of either ¹⁵NH₄⁺ or glucose, so the isotopes should be in the metabolite pool.
>
> The utility of chemical fractionation in characterizing soil organic matter has been debated ever since the first fractionations of fulvic and humic acids. Chemical fractionation of plant material, however, has been quite successful in characterizing litter quality. Since in tundra soils much of the organic matter is still plant detritus these techniques should be useful.

The revised proposal was funded; reviewers still raised the methods and challenges, but "enthusiastically," recognizing that the authors had weighed strengths and limitations and thought through interpretation. Schimel notes: "We didn't change the methods, just how we defined their results" (p. 184).

Discussing Example 18.5 further, Schimel connects it to his "no bad methods" principle: the isotope equilibration technique "was bad for estimating the active fraction, but good for estimating microbial metabolites" (p. 184).

On placement of limitations within the Discussion, Schimel references an earlier example rather than reproducing it here: "Example 8.7 used this approach. The authors included two constraints on the conclusions, but they put them in the middle of an LD paragraph — after the lead but before the rest of the discussion" (p. 184).

*Example 18.6* (p. 185), from a survey study of side effects of inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) used to treat asthma:

> Limitations of our research include potential selection bias. Although our response rate (56%) is not atypical for a questionnaire study, we acknowledge that responders may have had a greater propensity for perceiving ICS side effects as compared to non-responders, and it is possible that side effect reporting may have been inflated in all dose groups as a result. Alternatively, ICS users in our study may have experienced relatively mild ICS side effects, since they continue to use ICS regardless of their perception of side effects.

Schimel's reading: this names a quantitative uncertainty inherent to survey research (you can't make people answer, or answer honestly) that can never be fully overcome, while the paper's robust patterns still stand as information physicians should have (p. 185).

*Example 18.7* (p. 185), from a paper on how individual variation affects transcription patterns across tissues:

> Despite the limited number of validated examples, osteoblast-specific transcript isoforms are present and indicates that expression of certain alternatively spliced variants in bone tissue are controlled by different regulatory SNPs and vary between individuals and/or populations, with potentially important biological functions.

Schimel highlights this as "but, yes" compressed into a single sentence: the opening clause ("Despite the limited number of validated examples") is the "but," and the rest delivers the "yes" and its implications, without dwelling (p. 185).

*Example 18.8*, "Experimental Considerations" (pp. 186–187), the opening section of the Discussion in a paper by Schimel on nitrogen movement between decomposing leaves in forest litter, using an artificial "microcosm" of two leaves (one ¹⁵N-labeled, one not) wired together and wrapped in plastic — shown in Figure 18.1:

#### Figure 18.1 (p. 186)

**Caption.** Figure 18.1. The set-up for an experiment evaluating N movement between decomposing leaves.

**What it shows.** A black-and-white photograph of a single experimental unit, shot from directly above and filling the frame. There are no axes, no scale bar, no callouts, and no printed labels on the image.

The unit is a broad, roughly oval leaf — dark, nearly black in the reproduction, with a pointed tip at the right edge and a lobed, irregular outline; its lower right shows a torn or curled section that reads lighter gray. The leaf lies on a pale, faintly creased sheet that fills the background of the frame, consistent with the plastic wrapping described in the text.

Laid diagonally across the leaf, running from lower left to upper right, is a light-colored strip — a second, pale leaf or a paper label — noticeably narrower than the dark leaf and tapering toward its right end. Written on this strip by hand in dark ink is a specimen code: it reads as "P", then a small raised "15", then "C", then "2" — that is, "P 15C 2", the 15 rendered the way one would write the mass number of the 15-N isotope tracer. The handwriting is legible but the code's intended spacing is not certain.

Overlying the whole unit is a square grid of thin straight wires — roughly six or seven verticals and five or six horizontals crossing the frame — which holds the two leaves clamped together in contact. Several cut wire ends stick out past the leaf at the upper left and right. The grid's spacing is even and its squares are appreciably smaller than the leaf, so about a dozen squares cover the leaf's face.

**What it demonstrates.** It shows the physical reality of the "microcosm" design behind the paper's data: two leaves, one 15-N labeled and one not, pressed into contact by wire mesh and sealed in plastic so they do not dry out. Seeing it makes plain how far the set-up is from a forest floor litter layer — an artificial structure whose constraints on interpreting the results the authors had to raise up front, in an opening "Experimental Considerations" section of the Discussion.

> Experimental Considerations
>
> This experiment used a rather artificial structure: pairs of individual leaves held together to ensure contact and minimize diffusion limitations. We labeled [with ¹⁵N] only the microorganisms growing on the litter and so were evaluating the short-term movement of N as regulated by the release and uptake of N by communities growing on different substrates. Although fungi were likely involved in decomposing the leaves, we did not observe fungal mycelia developing between them; such mycelia are thought to be important in moving N through a natural litter layer. Thus, this design provides little information about the amount of N that would actually move within natural litter layers decomposing *in situ*. However, this controlled experimental design allowed us to do a ¹⁵N budget on each individual leaf-pair microcosm and thereby evaluate the bi-directional N movement among leaves of different species and N-levels, and to evaluate the separate role of source and sink N concentrations in regulating N movement within the microcosms.

Schimel calls this honest about what the experiment could not show (field-realistic movement rates) while using the controlled design to argue for what it could show: a full ¹⁵N budget per leaf pair and the separate roles of source and sink concentrations in regulating N movement. He notes the section also let the authors remind readers of the knowledge gap and questions the paper was targeting (p. 187).

*Example 18.9* (pp. 187–188), from a paper evaluating methods for estimating under-five child mortality in nations without reliable birth/death records, where no absolute reference data set existed to validate the estimates. The authors placed the extended limitations discussion (21 lines) in the next-to-last paragraph rather than the final conclusions paragraph (11 lines), to avoid overwhelming the conclusion:

> Despite these limitations, the methods proposed here represent a major advance on current practice and offer the prospect of vastly increasing our knowledge about levels, recent trends, and inequalities in child mortality. If we are to make rapid progress with the unfinished agenda of reducing child deaths, policy and practice must be better informed by more comprehensive, relevant, and timely information. Systematic application of the methods proposed here will establish that evidence base, and thereby increase accountability among countries and the global health community to accelerate efforts to reduce the global toll of child deaths.
