Schimel points to "these limitations" as a nominalization referring back to the prior paragraph's extended discussion — a phrase that "simultaneously highlights and minimizes them," letting the authors keep the negatives and positives in separate story arcs while linking them, addressing the constraints honestly without undermining the power of the conclusion (p. 188).

The chapter closes by restating its opening principle: no research is perfect, there is nothing to be embarrassed about in admitting it, and it is the researcher's responsibility to address a work's limitations. The "but, yes" approach does this while keeping the presentation "clear, strong, and credible," and by being clear about both the limits and the power of the work, motivates others to build on it and cite it (p. 188).

**Terms introduced.**

- **"Yes, but" strategy** — presenting results and conclusions first and limitations last, which makes the limitation the paper's resolution and most powerful message (p. 180).
- **"But, yes" strategy** — addressing limitations as early as possible, constraining the conclusions to fit within them, and ending on the positive "yes"; Schimel's recommended alternative to "yes, but" (p. 180).
- **"No such thing as a bad method"** — Schimel's interpretive principle that a properly executed method always yields solid data; a method's "badness" is a mismatch between what it measures and what it is being used to answer, not a flaw in the method itself (p. 184).
- **Experimental/Methodological Considerations section** — an opening section of the Discussion, used when methods are unusual or novel, that calibrates readers on a method's strengths and weaknesses before the results are presented, woven into the opening of the Discussion's story arc (p. 186).

**Connections.** References Example 8.7 and the LD (lead-development) paragraph structure from chapter 8 as a model for placing a limitation mid-paragraph, after the lead but before the rest of the discussion (p. 184). Builds on the book's recurring principle, restated here from earlier chapters, that filling a small knowledge gap is preferable to a mismatch between question and answer (p. 181). The chapter's treatment of "power positions" (a Discussion's opening and resolution reserved for critical story points) presupposes the story-structure concepts governing the Discussion section developed elsewhere in the book.


### Chapter 19 — Writing Global Science

**Claim.** Writing science in English as a second language is often assumed to be primarily a language problem. Schimel argues it is primarily a structure problem and, before that, a science problem: the hardest part of writing science is developing the story and laying it out cleanly, and that challenge is the same for native and non-native English speakers alike. Correct English is a real and non-negotiable requirement for publication, but it ranks behind story in what determines whether a paper is accepted, and responsibility for producing correct English rests on the author (and, where applicable, on native-English-speaking coauthors), not on editors or reviewers.

**Mechanism.** Schimel lays out the chapter's argument as a sequence of priorities rather than a single rule:

- *Structure before language.* "Structure comes before language in the SCFL formula I discussed in chapter 17, and most of this book is about structure. Story structure transcends language; OCAR isn't about English" (p. 189).
- *Doing good science comes before writing it.* The first and most important step toward publication in an international journal is doing science that leading journals want — which requires developing "intellectual courage" and the confidence/humility balance to take risks, challenge established ideas, and question authority (p. 190).
- *Information versus knowledge as the novelty test.* A paper that repeats a known pattern in a new system, with new technology, or both, is not thereby made novel — it "merely fills in the information base." Leading journals look for papers that produce new knowledge and understanding, not new information (p. 191).
- *Audience and journal fit before submission.* Identify the intended audience and match the journal to the paper's scope rather than its prestige. "Don't focus on the journal's status, but on its scope" (p. 192). If unsure of a journal's focus, read its published papers and, failing that, email the editor for advice (p. 192).
- *Language as the author's responsibility, applied last.* "It's only after this that specific language skills matter" (p. 192). At minimum, a submitted manuscript must use the right words, spell them correctly, and follow the rules of grammar and usage, and ensuring this is the author's job, not the reviewers', editors', or publisher's (pp. 192–193).
- *Tools and help, ranked by reliability.* Spell checkers catch some errors but miss real-word substitutions ("their"/"there," "from"/"form"); grammar checkers catch some real errors but also generate false flags; a short, practical reference book (Schimel names Strunk and White's *The Elements of Style* as the standard) beats an exhaustive grammar text; a native-English-speaking friend is not a substitute for a skilled editor unless that friend is also a good editor; professional editing services are a worthwhile expense relative to the cost of the research itself (pp. 193–194).
- *Coauthor responsibility is different from friend responsibility.* Where a native-English-speaking coauthor is involved, that person is responsible for ensuring the paper's language is correct, because all authors are responsible for the entire content of a paper, language included. Poor language in a paper with English-speaking coauthors reflects on those coauthors' diligence, not only on the primary author's English (p. 194).
- *When story and language conflict, story wins.* As an editor, Schimel sends back papers with a strong story and weak language to have the language fixed; a paper with a weak story he simply rejects. "So which is more important — getting the grammar or the story down? I'll vote for story every time. You can hire an editor to help with the language. But you can't hire a scientist to help with the science" (p. 194).

**Evidence and examples.** The chapter opens without a numbered exercise or formal epigraph attributed to another writer; its first line is Schimel's own: "Life isn't fair" (p. 189), introducing the observation that science is increasingly dominated by scholars for whom English is a second language and by nations with developing scientific cultures, and that language and scientific-culture challenges do not necessarily track each other — Schimel gives Germany (a founder of the modern scientific tradition, non-English-speaking) and India (English well established, developing science program) as a contrast pair (p. 189).

*The confidence/humility framing.* Schimel describes doing science as "inherently an act of both confidence and humility":

> Confidence in developing your own ideas and data, doing the work knowing it may fail, and then putting it out in public where people can criticize it (and you). Humility in that you know that those data and ideas are imperfect and incomplete, and you have to admit openly to the limitations. Too much confidence can blind you to the limitations; too much humility can blind you to the accomplishments. (p. 190)

He connects this to his own experience of "academic adolescence" halfway through his doctoral program — asking "can I do this?" — and credits his advisors with pushing him to develop and present his own ideas rather than only report results, tying this back to figure 2.2 from earlier in the book (p. 190).

*The Professor Genelle example.* Schimel works through a full hypothetical case to demonstrate why repeating a known result in a new setting fails the novelty test:

> Many, however, are learning not directly from a world-leading scientist but from reading the work of world-leading scientists. That distinction has led to too many papers that basically say "Well-known Professor Genelle found X, and I want to see whether X occurs in my system." I suspect this grows from a sense that "if Prof. Genelle did it, it must be good science, so if I repeat it in a new system, I'll be doing good science, too." When Prof. Genelle did it, it was good science because it was novel. But because she did it, it isn't novel anymore — now it's an old story. (pp. 190–191)

He specifies the hypothetical: if Prof. Genelle's original paper were "Fungi are more drought tolerant than bacteria in a French grassland," what made it novel was the fungi/bacteria drought-tolerance finding itself, not that it occurred in a French grassland — the location is "just incidental qualifying information" (pp. 190–191). Showing the same pattern in a different system only reinforces her original conclusion; it does not generate a new story. Schimel then lists what would generate a new story from that starting point: showing that Genelle's pattern does *not* hold in another system (raising the question of why they differ), analyzing the mechanism behind fungi's enhanced drought tolerance, or evaluating how drought tolerance interacts with other stresses (p. 191). He generalizes the point: "Answering an old question in a new system won't make the science novel. Answering an old question using new technology also won't make the science novel. Even answering an old question in a new system with new technology won't make the science novel" (p. 191).

*Rejection pattern at a leading journal.* Writing from his position as an editor, Schimel describes seeing many submissions that are "rigorously done but only offer information," where authors highlight novelty in the form of "the first data on a process in a new region — trace gas emissions, nitrification, and so on." He states the authors are usually correct about the fact, "but that very argument is why the paper was rejected" (p. 191). He extends the same point with further examples of information-only submissions: a first data set for a new region, a reaction shown to work similarly under a slightly different molecular substitution, or a new bacterium's gene sequence found only modestly different from known bacteria (pp. 191–192).

*The plowing/nitrate example, distinguishing applied research from information-only research.* Schimel uses a hypothetical applied study to show that "information versus knowledge" is not the same distinction as "applied versus basic":

> For example, a paper on how plowing a soil alters nitrate leaching and nitrous oxide emissions might be valuable for local managers who are trying to maximize crop yield while minimizing groundwater pollution and greenhouse gas emissions. They need the information, and it should be published in an appropriate venue. But unless the paper also offers new insights into the fundamentals of N-cycling or develops a new, transferable management regime, that venue is not likely to be a high-impact basic-science journal — and that will be true regardless of whether the work was done in India or Indiana. (p. 192)

*Footnote on editorial help (p. 193, note 1).* Schimel qualifies his statement that reviewers and editors won't fix a paper's English:

> Actually, many of us do help with language and writing. We know that beginning writers struggle, and most of us want to help. But we usually only do so when it means tidying up and fixing quirks of English, rather than doing a full copy edit. It is also an act of generosity you should not count on. Editors help those who help themselves. (p. 193, note 1)

*Footnote on Strunk and White (p. 193, note 2).* "If you don't have access to Strunk and White, the original 1918 version by Strunk is available online for free, http://www.bartleby.com/141/" (p. 193, note 2). In the body text he explains the recommendation: "For guides to grammar and usage, shorter is better. You don't need to understand the deepest arcana of English grammar — you need practical, everyday advice. It's no accident that the most battered and coffee-stained book on every writer's shelf is the shortest: Strunk and White, *The Elements of Style*" (p. 193). He also directs readers to appendix B for his list of favorite grammar and language references (p. 193).

*Caution against relying on a friend for language editing.* "I've sent back too many papers that were edited by friends who hadn't done an adequate job, and I've had some 'polite disagreements' with authors who were sure that because their American friend looked over the paper it must be okay" (p. 193). He recommends professional editing services as an alternative, noting they are not very expensive relative to the cost of the underlying research (p. 193).

*Coauthor responsibility.* "My suggestion to not rely on an English-speaking colleague changes completely, however, when that colleague is a coauthor. All authors are responsible for a paper's entire content, and that includes the language. Your English-speaking coauthor is responsible for ensuring the language is correct" (p. 194). Schimel describes how reviewers react to poorly written papers carrying coauthors from English-speaking countries: they "can be appropriately brutal," questioning whether those coauthors were actually involved or simply failed their responsibility to make the paper submission-ready — with reputational fallout for both the primary author and the coauthor (p. 194).

*Closing story.* Schimel recounts a colleague who questioned whether the book would be useful for scholars writing in a second language, worrying that the book's focus on storytelling might be "overkill" for people who struggle with grammatical sentences. His answer, drawn from his own editorial practice: a paper with a strong story but weak language gets sent back for language fixes; a paper with a weak story gets rejected outright (p. 194). He closes on the vote for story over grammar quoted above, adding: "Remember, always, that science is not about information; it is about knowledge and understanding. If you can offer understanding, you are most of the way to writing a paper that will be publishable in the world's best journals" (p. 194).

**Terms introduced.** No new terms are coined in this chapter. It applies two terms established earlier in the book — SCFL (structure before language, ch. 17) and OCAR — to the specific case of non-native-English authorship, and it restates the information-versus-knowledge distinction that runs through the book without renaming it.

**Connections.** Depends on chapter 17 for the SCFL formula and on the OCAR structure established earlier in the book; references figure 2.2 from chapter 2 regarding reaching for new understanding rather than only reporting results. Points readers to appendix B for Schimel's list of recommended grammar and language references. No numbered exercises appear in the assigned page range for this chapter.


### Chapter 20 — Writing for the Public

> Put it before them briefly so they will read it, clearly so they will appreciate it, picturesquely so they will remember it, and above all, accurately so they will be guided by its light.
> — Joseph Pulitzer (epigraph, p. 195)

**Claim.** Writing for the public is not a different skill from writing science for peers — it is the same skill pushed further. The tools the book has already taught (sharpening the story, hitting the SUCCES elements, cutting jargon) apply unchanged to lay audiences; the only difference is that a public-facing piece needs more of them, and less complexity and jargon. The chapter also argues that scientists must communicate with the public and with policy makers because those groups act on their own (sometimes mistaken) perceptions of the science, and journalists cannot be relied on to bridge that gap alone.

**Mechanism.**

Section 20.1, "Writing Stories for the Public," gives five rules for shaping a story for a lay audience:

1. Story: a simple story is critical.
2. Focus: it must target either the "who knew?" elements or the application — what does this mean for them or for society? It helps to talk about the people doing the work. Scientists are trained to downplay the human dimensions, but for nonscientists, people are more engaging characters than chemicals or concepts.
3. Story structure: the audience is impatient and itching to know why this story will be interesting. That calls for a front-loaded structure, most likely LDR, to quickly engage the SUCCES elements.
4. Language: keep it simple. Assume that you need to define every technical term, or better yet, find a way to avoid them. In writing for the public, a technical term will likely feel like jargon. Importantly, never introduce a technical term in the topic position of a sentence. Finally, don't be patronizing; assume your audience is intelligent but ignorant. You are not "dumbing down" the story, you are highlighting its simple core and telling it in their language. Your job is to interest and educate your audience.
5. Scientific method: the public thinks that science proceeds through "Eureka!" breakthroughs made by brilliant men and women and expects stories to present those breakthroughs. But that isn't how science works. It advances through incremental advances by many people struggling to collect difficult data and make sense out of the puzzles that emerge. We are about process, not product — we don't expect to achieve the ultimate breakthrough, though we are driven by the search. The best stories for the public integrate our joy in puzzling out nature with their focus on results and application. (p. 197–198)

Section 20.2 introduces the message box, a story-framing tool the book credits to Nancy Baron. It has two overarching elements and four ordered questions, worked through as a physical box divided into quadrants around a center label (Figure 20.2):

> A and B. Issue and Audience. Start by identifying your overall issue and audience. Keep your eye on them as you develop the story. These aren't specific elements but overarching themes to weave through the story.
>
> 1. Problem: what is your specific problem? This defines your opening and your challenge. In a story for the public, these should be conflated into a clear lead.
> 2. So what? Moving clockwise around the box, we come to "So what?" Why should readers care about your problem? Your "so what?" must be appropriate for your audience; it will be different for your colleagues and relatives. If you are writing an LDR-structured story for the public, your "so what?" should be integrated into the lead.
> 3. Solution: having defined the problem, offer your solution. This will comprise the body of the story: the A in OCAR, the D in ABDCE or LDR.
> 4. Benefits: finally, what are the benefits of your solution? What would your audience get from it? This should be your resolution. (p. 198)

This A/B/1–4 breakdown is set off from the surrounding prose in the book, mirroring the four-quadrant box of Figure 20.2. It is reproduced above as a block.

Section 20.3 walks through applying the message box to convert a research topic into a public story, using a worked scenario. Section 20.4 extends the same tool to writing for scientific peers, on the grounds that peer readers ask the same four questions (problem, solution, why care) even though their concerns differ from the public's. Section 20.5 addresses policy makers specifically: the chapter states that clear communication is necessary but not sufficient for influencing policy, because decision makers' choices are shaped as much by political as by natural reality, and because the audience — e.g., a 22-year-old congressional staffer with a B.A. in political science — has neither the technical background nor the time (often "five minutes in a hallway") that other audiences get. The chapter's advice for this audience is to front-load the wrap-up message — the specific ask — since that is the part staffers are described as actually listening for.

**Evidence and examples.**

*NYT frog headlines (p. 196).* To illustrate that obscure topics get picked up by the press, the chapter reports scanning the New York Times for 2010 for stories on frogs:

- Endangered frogs
- How do frogs handle the shock of landing?
- Frogs use foams to make nests for their eggs
- Tree frogs shake branches to communicate with each other
- And of course: A giant frog that "hopped with dinosaurs"

The chapter notes some of these stories concern environmental threats but most are "who knew?" curiosity pieces — including the detail that toads fire the muscles used to absorb a landing shock 90 milliseconds *before* they leave the ground on the jump.

Figure 20.1 (p. 197) diagrams the public's flow of concern from data through to application, used to explain why people react differently to medical stories (application seen as making life better) versus global-warming stories (application feared as making life worse), with the pushback said to cascade back to the underlying data.

#### Figure 20.1 (p. 197)

**Caption.** Figure 20.1.  The public's flow of concern from data to application.

**What it shows.** A single left-to-right horizontal chain of five rectangular boxes joined by four short right-pointing arrows, running the full width of the text block at the top of the page. Reading left to right the box labels are: **Data** → **Information** → **Knowledge** → **Understanding** → **Application**. Each box is a plain thin-ruled rectangle with the word centered inside; there are no other labels, no axes, and no annotations. The type size grows at every step: "Data" is set in the smallest type in the smallest box, "Information" larger, "Knowledge" larger still, "Understanding" larger again, and "Application" is set in the largest type in the largest box, roughly three to four times the height of "Data." The boxes grow in width and height in proportion to their type. The arrows between boxes are all the same short, plain, single-headed style; the flow is one-directional, with no feedback arrows.

**What it demonstrates.** That the public's interest in science increases along the chain from raw data to eventual application — what matters least to them is data and what matters most is what can be done with the knowledge — with the escalating type size carrying the weight of concern at each stage.

*Worked example: zebrafish gene cascades and cancer (p. 198–201).* The chapter poses a scenario: a researcher studies gene networks that regulate cell growth in zebrafish, where cascade failure causes cells to become cancerous. Asked by a reporter or an aunt whether the work will cure cancer, the researcher must answer in terms that connect to the questioner's own schema. The chapter fills out a message box for this scenario:

> A. Issue: curing cancer.
>
> B. Audience: your aunt specifically, and the public more generally.
>
> 1. Problem: we don't understand the complex cascade of genetic interactions that cause normal cells to "go bad" and start growing out of control.
> 2. So what? The switch from normal to out-of-control growth is the critical step in developing cancer.
> 3. Solutions: we need to understand the genetic interactions that cause this switch so we can prevent or reverse it. To study these interactions, we need a model system. Zebrafish turn out to be a surprisingly good system because they are easy to grow, their genetics are easy to work on, and they function similarly to humans.
> 4. Benefits: figuring out the genetic interactions that cause normal cells to become cancer cells may allow us to develop treatments that would prevent or treat cancer. (p. 199–200)

Figure 20.2 (p. 199) shows the blank message box template (quadrants labeled Issue, Audience, Problem?, So What?, Solutions?, Benefits?).

#### Figure 20.2 (p. 199)

**Caption.** Figure 20.2.  The message box.

**What it shows.** A "message box" diagram: a central rectangle with four straight lines radiating outward from its four corners toward the corners of the figure area, so the figure reads as a rectangle with a flared trapezoidal zone above, below, left, and right of it. The four radiating lines are plain, unarrowed, and open-ended — they do not close into outer boxes; the wedge-shaped regions they define hold text.

Inside the central rectangle, stacked vertically and centered:

- the letter **A**, with a short horizontal rule beneath it, and under the rule the italic label *Issue*
- the letter **B**, with a short horizontal rule beneath it, and under the rule the italic label *Audience*

The rules read as blank fill-in lines for the writer to complete.

In the four outer wedge regions, each labelled with a numeral above an italic prompt:

- Above the central box (top wedge): **1** / *Problem?*
- Right of the box (right wedge): **2** / *So What?*
- Below the box (bottom wedge): **3** / *Solutions?*
- Left of the box (left wedge): **4** / *Benefits?*

The numbering therefore runs clockwise from the top: Problem (1) → So What (2) → Solutions (3) → Benefits (4). There are no axes, values, units, or arrows in the figure.

**What it demonstrates.** The template form of the message box: issue and audience are fixed at the center and held in view throughout, while the four story elements are worked through clockwise around them, from problem to so-what to solution to benefits.

Figure 20.3 (p. 200) shows the filled-in message box for the zebrafish/cancer scenario corresponding to the A/B/1–4 text above.

#### Figure 20.3 (p. 200)

**Caption.** Figure 20.3.  A message box for studying gene cascades in zebrafish.

**What it shows.** The same message-box form as figure 20.2 — a central rectangle with four unarrowed lines radiating outward from its corners, defining top, right, bottom, and left wedges — but with every slot filled in with the zebrafish gene-cascade example. The numerals 1–4 are dropped; only the italic prompts remain as headings.

Central rectangle, stacked vertically and centered:

- **Curing cancer**, with a horizontal rule beneath it, and under the rule the italic label *Issue*
- **Public**, with a horizontal rule beneath it, and under the rule the italic label *Audience*

Top wedge, italic heading *Problem?* over the text:

> We don't understand the genetic interactions that cause normal cells to "go bad" and start growing out of control

Right wedge, italic heading *So What?* over the text:

> Switch from normal to out of control growth is the critical step in developing cancer

Bottom wedge, italic heading *Solution?* over the text:

> We need to understand the switch. That requires a good model system: the zebrafish

Left wedge, italic heading *Benefits?* over the text:

> Figuring out these interactions may allow us to develop treatments for cancer

All four wedge texts are centered under their headings. No axes, units, values, or arrows.

**What it demonstrates.** A worked example of the completed template: the same research is framed for a public audience by fixing the issue as "curing cancer" and the audience as "Public," then filling problem, so-what, solution, and benefits in that audience's terms rather than the researcher's.

From that message box the chapter writes out the resulting story, given in full:

> Example 20.1
> Gene Cascades in Zebrafish
>
> Cancer is what happens when normal cells start growing and dividing out of control. If we want to prevent cancer, we need to know what causes that switch — why do "good cells go bad?" My research targets that question — I study how genes interact with each other to keep cells working and growing at the "right rate," and how those interactions break down, turning normal cells into cancer cells. I work on zebrafish because their genes behave similarly to those of people — and you can't grow people in an aquarium. If we learn what causes growth regulation to break down, we may be able to prevent or reverse it. So, yes, I hope that my work will ultimately contribute to curing cancer. (p. 200)

The chapter states this passage is accurate, honest, and free of grandiose claims, and that it frames the research so the aunt can understand both why it is intellectually interesting and how it might contribute to curing cancer, and could stand alone or serve as the opening of a longer story. It attributes the example's effectiveness to a clean OCAR structure and to hitting the SUCCES elements: it is simple (focused on the breakdown in growth regulation), emotional (addresses a question the audience cares about), concrete and credible (describes the work and its place in the search for a cancer cure), and written in simple language.

*Worked example: winter tundra microbial respiration (p. 201–202).* The chapter presents a second message box, this one for a research-proposal opening on microbial activity in arctic tundra soils during winter:

Figure 20.4 (p. 201) shows the filled message box for this project: Issue — microbial activity in tundra soils during the winter; Audience — scientists; Problem — "we've ignored winter CO2 fluxes and so got the arctic C cycle wrong"; So what? — "tundra soils hold a lot of C that can be released and accelerate global warming"; Solution — "evaluate the magnitude and controls on wintertime tundra CO2 fluxes"; Benefits — "enhance our understanding of the global C cycle: develop better climate models."

#### Figure 20.4 (p. 201)

**Caption.** Figure 20.4.  A message box for studying microbial activity in arctic tundra soils during the winter.

**What it shows.** Again the message-box form — central rectangle with four unarrowed lines radiating from its corners to define top, right, bottom, and left wedges — filled in for the author's own arctic tundra project. No numerals; italic prompts as headings.

Central rectangle, stacked vertically and centered:

- **Microbial activity in tundra soils during the winter**, set on two lines, with a horizontal rule beneath, and under the rule the italic label *Issue*
- **Scientists**, with a horizontal rule beneath, and under the rule the italic label *Audience*

Top wedge, italic heading *Problem?* over the text:

> We've ignored winter CO₂ fluxes and so got the arctic C cycle wrong

(CO₂ is printed with a subscript 2; "C cycle" uses the elemental symbol for carbon.)

Right wedge, italic heading *So What?* over the text:

> Tundra soils hold a lot of C that can be released and accelerate global warming

Bottom wedge, italic heading *Solution?* over the text:

> Evaluate the magnitude and controls on wintertime tundra CO₂ fluxes

Left wedge, italic heading *Benefits?* over the text:

> Enhance our understanding of the global C cycle: develop better climate models

All four wedge texts are centered under their headings. No axes, units, tick values, or arrows. The figure sits in the lower half of the page, below the text of section 20.4, with the caption running two lines directly beneath it.

**What it demonstrates.** That the message box works for a scientific audience as well as a lay one: with "Scientists" written into the audience slot, the same four questions — problem, so what, solution, benefits — frame an apparently abstruse soil-microbiology project in terms a peer reader cares about.

From that box, the chapter gives the condensed proposal opening in full:

> Example 20.2
> Microbes in frozen soil?
>
> Arctic ecologists have traditionally focused on the growing season, the short period between snowmelt and snowfall. As researchers packed up in August to head home for the academic year, there was a sense of "last one out, turn out the lights." Yet, the Arctic is dominated by the 9-month-long winter. For many years, it was assumed that plants and microbes were frozen into dormancy for the winter. Soil microbes, however, continue to respire through the winter. While the rate may be slow, Arctic winters are so long that this respiration constitutes a large fraction of annual carbon budgets, a fraction that could increase with climate warming, which is greatest during winter.
>
> Because Arctic tundra contains half of the global soil organic C — more than twice the C that is in the atmosphere as CO2 — an increase in the rate of wintertime microbial respiration could increase atmospheric CO2 and intensify global warming. Developing a better understanding of the magnitude and controls of winter microbial respiration is therefore critical to building models that can project the future climate. (p. 202)

The chapter identifies this as an autobiographical example — a condensed version of the author's own proposal opening, with technical material and citations deleted but the argument's flow and the "last one out, turn out the lights" phrasing preserved from the original. It states the message box both structured the proposal's story and, separately, prepared the author for a reporter's call about the same work for a New York Times piece (cited in a footnote: Paul Voosen, "Even When Frozen, Soils Get Busy Emitting CO2," New York Times, November 29, 2010).

*Policy makers (p. 202–203).* The chapter illustrates the gap between scientific and political communication with a composite of what a congressional staffer hears from a scientist: "Blah, blah, carbon, blah, blah, respiration, blah, blah. If we wait until the permafrost melts to do something about global warming, that will be bad." It states the closing words — the request — are the ones staffers actually attend to, even though scientists tend to think about them least.

The chapter also gives an autobiographical example from the author's time as an assistant professor at the University of Alaska, during the response to the Exxon Valdez oil spill at Bligh Reef. A manager on the damage-assessment effort told the scientists: "this isn't about science, it's about damage assessment, but you have to use the best available scientific methods." The chapter reads this as meaning sophisticated analytic tools, missing that good science — the author's definition of the essential scientific method — was not guaranteed by sophistication alone; it states sophisticated but misapplied measurements have less value than simple, smart ones. It also quotes the project's lawyers describing the work as "you're not collecting data, you're collecting evidence." The chapter's point is that managers and politicians operate under pressures beyond the science and do not see science in the same terms scientists do, so effecting change means working in their world rather than expecting them to work in the scientist's.

**Terms introduced.**

- **Message box** — a graphical story-framing tool, credited to Nancy Baron, *Escaping the Ivory Tower: A Guide to Making Your Science Matter* (Island Press, 2010), cited in a footnote on p. 198. It lays out a story around two overarching themes (Issue, Audience) and four ordered questions read clockwise: (1) Problem — the specific problem, which defines the opening/challenge; (2) So what? — why the audience should care, tailored to that audience; (3) Solution(s) — the offered solution, forming the story's body; (4) Benefits — what the solution gets the audience, forming the resolution.

**Connections.** Applies, without modification, the story-structuring vocabulary the book built in earlier chapters — OCAR, LDR, ABDCE, the SUCCES elements, and schema — to nonexpert and policy audiences; the chapter is explicit that none of these tools change for a lay audience, only their degree of use. Section 20.4 extends the message box from public writing back to writing for scientific peers, tying this chapter to the book's earlier treatment of structuring a paper's opening. The closing EXERCISES block (20.1–20.3: fill out a message box for the reader's own short article for a scientific audience; fill out a message box for the same article for the public; rewrite the short article for the public) carries forward the "short article" exercise thread used in earlier chapters' exercises, for a later agent to link against Appendix A.


### Chapter 21 — Resolution

**Claim.** The book closes by enacting the resolution structure it has been teaching: it reopens on the same sentence chapter 1 opened on, reframes the reader's position (from student of the material to practitioner of it), and restates the book's central thesis — that a scientist is a professional writer — as the one message everything else grew from. Learning to write is a road without end, and success in a scientific career is a function of writing well, not of writing often.

**Mechanism.** The chapter has no rule or test of its own; it is structured as a graduation address, built from the "commencement" framing in its opening anecdote — an ending that is simultaneously a beginning. It runs in three named movements:

- **21.1 Looking Back: The Lessons** — restates the book's throughline: everything in every chapter grows from two principles, stickiness and story structure (named as SUCCES and OCAR), applied through targeting the audience and focusing on critical story elements.
- **21.2 Looking Forward: Becoming a Successful Writer** — reframes finishing the book as the start of an open-ended skill, not a completed credential.
- **21.2.1 Looking Forward: Becoming a Successful Scientist** — draws the career-level conclusion from the book's opening claim: contrasts "survival strategies" (publish or perish, judged by quantity) against "success strategies" (judged by citation, influence, and peer regard), and argues the second subsumes the first.

**Evidence and examples.**

The chapter opens with an anecdote about the word "commencement," used to set up the chapter's own dual structure as both ending and beginning (p. 204):

> As a student, it always bugged us that they called graduation ceremonies "commencement." We knew that we were celebrating finishing, earning our degrees after years of hard work, and that we were also mourning finishing, ending years of comradeship. The idea that the ceremony was a beginning was absurd, and to give it the pompous name of "commencement" only made it worse. We were wrong, as I realized the day I finished my Ph.D. ... So who was I? No longer at the pinnacle of the student world, I was now at the bottom of the professional. The most junior, inexperienced, and unproven Ph.D. in a world of Ph.D.s. The degree I'd struggled for meant nothing; it was just an entry ticket to this new arena, where I had to start proving myself all over again. Indeed, all graduations are commencements.

This is followed directly by the resolution's own framing of the book's ending, on p. 205:

> Now we come to the resolution of this book, and it is both graduation and commencement. Here I tell you that you have learned new tools and skills and congratulate you on your accomplishments. But this, too, is a new beginning.

Schimel offers Homer as an example that the book's principles predate writing itself, and restates the book's opening line as its central message (p. 205):

> After all, Homer didn't write the Odyssey — he recited it. Only centuries later did someone capture it in the written word. But Homer understood OCAR and SUCCES.
>
> The most important message I have tried to emphasize is the one I started with: as a scientist, you are a professional writer.

He uses a coal-versus-gemstone metaphor to state the limits of what writing craft can do for weak research, and what it can do for flawed-but-real data (p. 205):

> All the tools I offer will not enable you to turn a lump of coal into a diamond. Writing skills cannot replace other scientific skills — if the research is fundamentally weak, you can't use clever writing to strengthen it. But writing skills will help you take a rough and flawed gem of a data set, identify its valuable core, facet it, and polish it to play up its assets.

On finishing the book as the start rather than the end of the skill (p. 205):

> Working through the chapters and doing the exercises should have expanded your tool box, but that won't make you a writer. Now you have to take those tools and work with them to develop expertise, deepening your insights and abilities.

Schimel illustrates that his own competence kept revising upward after publication, using his own early funded proposal as the example (pp. 205–206):

> When you opened this book, you were probably a scientist who writes, a scientist who wants to write, or perhaps a scientist who has to write. I hope this book has set you on the road to becoming a scientist-writer. Learning to write, however, is a road without end. I was proud of the first proposal I got funded, until I read it later and wondered what drugs the review committee had been taking. I thought I knew enough about writing two years ago to write a book on writing.
>
> I can't tell you how much I have learned in the last few years, and how much better my writing has become. The tools I offer are just a starting point for becoming a successful writer and developing a successful career in science.

The survival-versus-success argument is built across four passages on p. 206. First, the general claim:

> As you start your career, some will give you advice on survival strategies. I think that is a mistake — I prefer to focus on success strategies. If you clear the bar of success, you will clear the bar of survival without noticing it was there.

Then the critique of "publish or perish" as a quantity metric:

> Survival strategies usually distill down to "publish or perish." Unfortunately, some colleagues interpret this as arguing that quantity is more important than quality. They argue that you survive by publishing and succeed by publishing a lot. They recognize that administrators rarely work in our immediate field and may not know whether our work is important, but they know that those administrators can look at our CVs and count our publications.

Then the explicit callback to the book's opening chapter, plus his brother's advice as a worked example of identifying the audience that matters:

> "Publish or perish" may be the basis for survival, but it is not the basis for success. As I argued in chapter 1, you don't succeed by getting papers published but by getting them cited. Ultimately academic success is grounded in a piece of advice my brother gave me once when I was frustrated with university politics. He said simply "remember who your real peers are." That meant soil and ecosystem scientists around the world, the people who read my papers. Your real peers will see through flash, splash, and numbers to identify papers that contribute. They'll cite those.

Then the mechanism by which peer regard converts into institutional reward, including his own experience writing promotion letters:

> Impress your real peers, and you will impress your tenure committee and your dean. Your dean can see your citation history, and can read your record of invitations to present at conferences and serve on committees and editorial boards. Those speak loudly about your standing in the field. We also rely on peers to send us students and postdocs — the people who do the work and write the papers — so quality begets quantity.
>
> Most important, in the U.S. system the decisive part of a promotion package is letters from your peers. When I write letters assessing people for promotion, I usually don't know exactly how many papers they've published, nor do I particularly care. I do know whether they've been creative. I do know whether their papers have influenced my thinking. I do know how those papers have moved the field. I sometimes even know whether they've done work so clever that its value has been missed by the crowd. I respect colleagues whose papers are thoughtful and insightful, and I've gone to bat for them. I've fought for colleagues who don't write lots of papers, but who write ones worth reading and citing.

The chapter's closing argument uses Einstein as an example that public and scientific stature come from the quality and reach of work, not its volume (p. 206):

> You may survive by publishing a lot of papers, but you will only succeed by writing good ones — papers that are clearly structured and tell a compelling story. Quality ultimately trumps quantity, and it will stand out in a crowded scientific universe. After all, Einstein's face isn't on T-shirts and advertisements around the world because he published a lot.

The book's final line, closing the chapter and the volume, is (p. 206):

> Good luck and good writing.

No numbered lists, figures, tables, or exercises appear in this chapter.

**Terms introduced.** No new terms. The chapter recapitulates, without redefining, the book's core vocabulary as the throughline the whole book grew from: stickiness, story structure, SUCCES, and OCAR (p. 205).

**Connections.** Reopens on the same sentence chapter 1 opened on — "As a scientist, you are a professional writer" (p. 204; compare p. 3) — and restates it as the book's single most important message (p. 205). Explicitly cites chapter 1's argument that success is measured by citation rather than publication ("As I argued in chapter 1," p. 206). Names SUCCES and OCAR as the two principles every other chapter's guidelines grew from, without reintroducing their definitions, which belong to the chapters that first developed them.


### Appendix A — My Answers to Revision Exercises

Schimel's own worked answers to the revision exercises set inside the chapters. Exercise numbers carry the chapter number they belong to (e.g., 5.3A belongs to chapter 5).

**Exercise 5.3A** (chapter 5), p. 207:

> The problem with this example is that the opening sentence doesn't give direction. This is trying to be a pawn push, introducing the idea that all chemical reactions are temperature-sensitive and preparing for the argument that this should be true in soil. However, an effective opening must introduce the story's central character and major issue; that is not "all chemical reactions" but soil respiration. So soil respiration should appear in the opening sentence.
>
> Respiration in soils, surprisingly, doesn't always increase with temperature as predicted by transition state theory and the Arrhenius equation. Some studies have shown no respiration response to increasing temperature, while a few have even reported a negative response.

**Exercise 5.3B** (chapter 5), p. 207:

> This is trying to capture a very wide audience with a broad statement about the importance of chemotherapy, but it is an example of bad misdirection. Everyone knows that chemotherapy is a common treatment for cancer, so a reader would just skip over that and get caught by "development of new targeted-delivery systems." That is an exciting and novel idea that would lead readers to assume the paper is going to tell us something about such systems. In fact, that was just an add-on to argue that chemotherapy is going to become even more important than it already is.
>
> The real story, however, is not about drug delivery systems but overcoming resistance to treatment. This opening needs to get straight to that idea and cut out distracting bells and whistles. Delete the first sentence and then adapt the second as the opening.
>
> A common constraint to effective cancer chemotherapy is that patients may be resistant to the treatments. Such resistance is often closely associated with the activity of the enzyme γ-glutamyl transpeptidase (GGT), which acts to increase intracellular concentrations of glutathione and thereby block the apoptotic cascade in tumor cells. Inhibiting GGT before chemotherapy would therefore reduce tumor cell resistance and increase treatment effectiveness.

**Exercise 11.3A** (chapter 11), p. 208:

> It had been thought that the lack of jet contrails over the United States caused the increase in the diurnal temperature range (DTR) during the three-day grounding of aircraft over the United States during the period of 11–14 September 2001. Variations in high cloud cover, including contrails and contrail-induced cirrus clouds, contribute weakly to the changes in the diurnal temperature range, which is governed primarily by lower altitude clouds, winds, and humidity. While missing contrails may have affected the DTR, their impact is probably too small to detect with statistical significance.
>
> This poses the question in the first sentence, rather than stating the conclusion. For that to work, the remainder of the paragraph would need to present some of the results and build the argument to support the conclusion. This worked as a TS-D paragraph, because it is apparent that the core results are presented elsewhere and this just presents their essence. I think the author's point-first structure worked better than a point-last.

**Exercise 11.3B** (chapter 11), p. 208:

> Great Plains mammoths apparently did not routinely migrate long distances, such as between northern Colorado and southern High Plains sites that are separated by about 600 km. Mammoth samples from Clovis sites in the Dent site had different ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr ratios than those at Blackwater Draw and Miami, indicating they come from distinct populations.
>
> To me, this paragraph feels weaker. The original poses a question and proposes a case study approach to answering it. To readers, the constraints on the approach are clear. The TS-D version starts with an argument that makes the constraints less apparent. TS-D is a weak structure for developing a complex argument — it needs a simple topic sentence. The OCAR structure allowed the author to develop both the simple core story — mammoths did not migrate long distances — and its limitations. The TS-D version is shorter, however, and if space were at a premium that might be more important than the nuance allowed by OCAR.

**Exercise 12.3A** (chapter 12), p. 208:

> 1. Viruses are the most abundant biological entities in the sea, yet were not studied until 1989.
> 2. It wasn't until 1989 that it was discovered that the most abundant biological entities in the sea are viruses.
> 3. It wasn't until 1989 that it was discovered that viruses are the most abundant biological entities in the sea.

**Exercise 12.3B** (chapter 12), p. 209:

> 1. Benzene contamination of groundwater is linked to elevated cancer levels.
> 2. Groundwater contaminated with benzene can cause cancer.
> 3. Cancer levels are higher in areas where groundwater is contaminated with benzene.

**Exercise 12.4A** (chapter 12), p. 209:

> The story in this sentence is that we don't know the crystalline structure of kryptonite, so the critical word is "unclear." That word should therefore be the stress, instead of being buried in the middle of the sentence. So move it to the end:
>
> Due to uncertainties resulting from interferences in the X-ray microanalysis, the crystalline nature of kryptonite remains unclear.

**Exercise 12.4B** (chapter 12), p. 209:

> The actor in this story is "drought," so it should be the topic. If we can't delete the initial clause, we can move it into the middle of the sentence.
>
> Drought reduces soil microbial activity by reducing diffusion and increasing physiological stress, causing a build-up of biodegradable C that is rapidly respired upon rewetting.

**Exercise 13.3A** (chapter 13), p. 209:

> There is no stress–topic linkage between these sentences. So make Fe the topic of the second sentence.
>
> Studies comparing iron-resistant and sensitive cell lines confirmed that protein X17 is denatured in the presence of Fe. When cellular Fe concentrations decrease, however, protein X17 reverts to its native form.

**Exercise 13.3B** (chapter 13), p. 209:

> The critical argument in the first paragraph is that the two promoters are synergistic: together they are more effective than either alone. The second paragraph adds a new twist to explaining the synergy, but it starts by introducing a new character. The connection to the previous paragraph is unclear. Reach back and grab the idea of synergy.
>
> The synergy between TREE2 and STEM3 that allows full transcription of *tryb* appears dependent on the presence of LEA. When LEA was absent, even with both promoters intact, transcription rates were only 50% of control levels . . .

**Exercise 14.3A** (chapter 14), p. 209–210 — Schimel's answer runs across the page break; corrected to the printed order (see note below):

> This sentence has only two verbs in it: increase (opportunities) and alter (population dynamics). But what is the critical action? It is "ecological interactions." What does that mean? It's a euphemism for a nematode eating a bacterium. That is dramatic, so make that action the verb; if eat seems too intense, you could use consume.
>
> Increased mobility of predatory nematodes in soil would allow them to consume more bacteria and so alter bacterial population dynamics.

**Exercise 14.3B** (chapter 14), p. 210:

> Two things to note here: all the actions are expressed in nominalizations, whereas the verbs are fuzzy and carry no sense of the action.

| Action | Expressed as | Verb associated with the nominalization |
|---|---|---|
| Challenge | Challenges | Present |
| Remediate | remediation | Present |
| costs money | financial costs | Invoke |
| threatens health | health risks | Present |
| Expose | exposure | Face |

> The following version uses the same core words to express the ideas of costs and threats but expresses them in verbs.
>
> Polyaromatic hydrocarbons and polychlorinated biphenyls are challenging to remediate: it costs a lot of money and it threatens the health of workers who are exposed to the compounds.

**Exercise 14.3C** (chapter 14), p. 210–211:

> This includes most of the problems I've discussed.

| | |
|---|---|
| was demonstrated | Fuzzy and passive |
| extraction | Nominalization |
| an enhancement | Fuzzy and nominalized |
| an extraction | Nominalization |

> Extracting soils with NH₄Cl instead of K₂SO₄ enhanced Al recovery.
>
> Here, I applied some of the rules from chapter 12 and restructured the sentence to unbury the stress as well as to energize the verbs! And no, that isn't cheating. You are always going for the best possible sentence. That wouldn't be: "Extracting soils with NH₄Cl enhanced Al recovery relative to extracting with K₂SO₄."

(This exercise's final sentence runs onto p. 211, where Exercise 15.3A begins immediately below it. The book sets 14.3A, 14.3B and 14.3C top to bottom in a single column, with separate tables under 14.3B and 14.3C.)

**Exercise 15.3A** (chapter 15), p. 211:

> This sentence suffers badly from prepositional phrases and nominalizations. It also suffers from a buried stress: the important message is that studies haven't been done.
>
> Animals' abilities to solve problems have been undervalued because scientifically reliable studies have not been done.

**Exercise 15.3B** (chapter 15), p. 211:

> A lot of heavy words.
>
> Rats kept under varying environmental conditions had better cognitive ability that the control group, which had been kept under constant conditions.

**Exercise 16.2A** (chapter 16), p. 211:

> Polyaromatic hydrocarbons and polychlorinated biphenyls are challenging to remediate: it is expensive and dangerous for clean-up workers.
>
> This is shorter, and I think it is stronger. I shortened it by condensing "costs a lot of money" and "threatens the health" into nominalizations — "expensive" and "dangerous" — and by switching an Anglo-Saxon word (threaten) for a French one. I broke the rules, but I collapsed short phrases into well-understood words. I think the trade-off worked. I also replaced "workers who are exposed to the compounds" with "clean-up workers." These chemicals are toxic to everyone, but since we're talking about remediating, the workers of particular concern are those doing the clean-up.

**Exercise 16.2B** (chapter 16), p. 211:

> Chla and Chlb transcript abundance showed similar patterns in plants at different developmental stages.
>
> I condensed this by deleting metadiscourse. We don't need to know about the comparison or the observation, just about the transcripts.

**Exercise 16.2C** (chapter 16), p. 211:

> Inherent resistance is an evolved response to living in constantly harsh environments. Resistant plants don't induce a physiological response to stress; rather, they have traits such as high root biomass, extensive chemical defenses, and low growth rates.
>
> The biggest problem with the paragraph is that "inherent resistance" is repeated three times. There are several other expressions that are cumbersome — prepositional phrases such as "environments that are constantly harsh" and "characterized by traits." By cutting these out, I was able to collapse two sentences into one.

### Appendix B — Writing Resources

Schimel's annotated list of books and websites for further reading, grouped as they appear in the source under "BOOKS" and "WEBSITES," with the subheadings he uses within BOOKS.

**BOOKS**

*General Writing*

- *Style: Toward Clarity and Grace*, Joseph Williams (University of Chicago Press); also published as *Style: Ten Lessons in Clarity and Grace* or *Style: The Basics of Clarity and Grace* (both from Pearson Longman). P. 212: "The different versions of *Style* are, in my opinion, the best books on writing English that exist. I prefer the original *Style: Toward Clarity and Grace* because it's more analytical and less of a pure textbook."
- *The Elements of Style*, William Strunk Jr. and E. B. White (Longman). P. 212: "The essential reference."
- *Writing Tools*, Roy Peter Clark (Little, Brown) and *The Glamour of Grammar*, Roy Peter Clark (Little, Brown). P. 212: "These are both insightful, useful, and entertaining. They range from basic to very advanced insights into writing. Clark is a leading teacher of journalism and it shows."

*Approaches to Writing*

- *Bird by Bird*, Anne Lamott (Anchor Books). P. 212: "A wonderful book by a talented fiction writer. Many of the insights transfer to science writing."
- *On Writing Well*, William Zinsser (Collins). P. 212: "The classic guide for journalism and nonfiction. Science is supposed to be nonfiction!"
- *Made to Stick*, Chip and Dan Heath (Random House). P. 212: "A brilliant guide to communication strategy. So much so that I spent all of chapter 3 to reprise it."

*Communicating Science*

- *Eloquent Science*, David M. Schultz (American Meteorological Society).
- *Essentials of Writing Biomedical Research Papers*, Mimi Zeiger (McGraw-Hill).

P. 213, on this pair together: "These are both extensive and technical guides to writing science, each targeted at a specific disciplinary area. The information in them is detailed and excellent."

**WEBSITES**

- Oxford English Dictionary: http://www.oed.com/. P. 213: "This is the essential language resource in English."
- Merriam-Webster: http://www.merriam-webster.com/. P. 213: "It includes both a good dictionary and a thesaurus."


## Cross-cutting synthesis

### One shape, repeated at every scale

The book's structural claim is recursive, and it is the through-line a linear read leaves scattered. Chapter 4 names four functional elements — Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution — as the shape of a paper. Chapter 10 then asserts that the same shape governs every level below the paper: sections, subsections, paragraphs, and sentences each carry a complete arc of their own. Chapter 11 applies it to paragraphs, which can be built as TS-D, LD, LDR or full OCAR. Chapter 12 applies it to the sentence, mapping grammatical subject, verb and object onto opening, action and resolution, so that a sentence becomes the shortest story possible.

OCAR appears in sixteen of the twenty-one chapters. What changes between them is only the scale it is being applied at. A reader who takes the vocabulary from chapter 4 and nothing else can follow the argument of chapters 10 through 13, because those chapters are the same claim restated three sizes smaller.

The consequence Schimel draws in chapter 10 is the reason the recursion matters: writing that lacks these nested arcs reads as an incoherent mass *even when every individual sentence is clear*, because coherence lives in the structure connecting sentences rather than in the sentences themselves. Fixing sentences one at a time cannot produce a coherent paper.

### Power positions

Readers do not distribute attention evenly, and the book returns to this repeatedly under the name **power position**: the places in a piece of writing where attention actually lands, which are its beginning and its end.

The idea operates at four scales at once, and the chapters that use it are scattered across the book:

- **The paper.** Chapter 5 calls the opening a power position that must identify the driving problem, introduce the characters, and target an audience. Chapter 9 makes the harder version of the claim about the ending: whatever occupies the last position is accepted as the take-home message *whether or not the author intended it that way*, which is why a resolution spent on limitations or on future work quietly reassigns the paper's message.
- **The section and paragraph.** Chapter 10 identifies the same two positions inside every nested arc.
- **The sentence.** Chapter 12's topic and stress are the sentence-scale version: the topic is the opening position, the stress is the closing one, and information placed in either is read as important regardless of what the writer intended.
- **The revision strategy.** Chapter 18's "but, yes" inversion applies the rule to a whole manuscript. The common "yes, but" habit — results first, limitations last — places the limitation in the resolution, where readers treat it as the paper's most important point. Moving limitations early and ending on the result is chapter 9's rule about resolutions, applied to a different problem.

### Topic and stress, the mechanical core

Chapter 12 introduces topic (what a sentence is about, at its start) and stress (the new information, at its end) with the **2-3-1 rule of emphasis**: of three items, the most important goes last, the second-most first, the least important in the middle. Chapters 13 through 17 all run on this machinery.

Chapter 13 turns it into the mechanism of flow. Every topic performs a **Janus function**, looking backward to what has been established and forward to what the sentence will say. Two linkage patterns follow, and they are not interchangeable:

- **Topic-to-topic** — successive sentences share a topic — produces a list: facts about one thing, with no inherent order.
- **Stress-to-topic** — one sentence's stress becomes the next sentence's topic — produces a chain, which is what carries a reader forward.

Two named repair moves come out of the same analysis. **Reach back and grab** takes an idea from a closing arc and makes it the topic of the sentence opening the next, bridging a transition. **Stress-stress** linkage, where two sentences share a stress rather than passing one to the other, is named as a failure mode rather than a technique.

Chapter 15 reuses the 2-3-1 rule for word order inside a sentence, and chapter 16's condensing work depends on knowing which positions can be emptied without losing emphasis.

### The structure vocabulary as a menu, not a prescription

Chapter 4 supplies a set of named structures rather than one recommended form, and the book keeps returning to the choice between them:

| Structure | Shape | Where the book applies it |
|---|---|---|
| **OCAR** | Opening, Challenge, Action, Resolution | Full papers; patient audiences |
| **ABDCE** | Action, Background, Development, Climax, Ending | Openings that must hook first |
| **LD** | Lead, Development | Journalism; Results sections; point-first paragraphs |
| **LDR** | Lead, Development, Resolution | Proposals; Discussion sections; paragraphs |
| **IMRaD** | Introduction, Methods, Results, and Discussion | The journal-imposed format OCAR must be fitted into |
| **Hourglass** | Wide, narrow, wide | The opening-to-resolution width match |
| **Spiral** | Returns to its starting point, displaced | The relation of resolution to opening |

The recurring instruction is to match structure to the audience's patience. OCAR asks a reader to wait; LD gives the point immediately and is what a reader who may stop reading requires. Chapter 8 pairs them explicitly — LD for Results, LDR for Discussion — and chapter 11 offers the same menu at paragraph scale. The hourglass and the spiral are not alternative structures but constraints on OCAR: the hourglass requires the resolution's breadth to match the opening's promise, and the spiral requires the resolution to return to the opening's problem having moved it.

### SUCCES as a standing checklist

The Heath brothers' six factors — Simple, Unexpected, Concrete, Credible, Emotional, Stories — arrive in chapter 3 as borrowed material and then persist through seventeen of the twenty-one chapters as the book's diagnostic vocabulary. Chapter 8's governing instruction is to remember the final S. Chapter 9 diagnoses the standard weak resolution as a missing C, concrete. Chapter 20 argues public writing needs more of the same six factors rather than different ones.

The book is explicit that this framework is imported, and the borrowing extends further: the Ladder of Abstraction from Roy Peter Clark, the knowledge gap theory of curiosity, the curse of knowledge, "murder your darlings," and epigraphs from Zinsser, Williams, Lamott, Twain, Chandler, Strunk and White, Shakespeare and von Moltke. The chess analogy of the pawn push versus the queen launch in chapter 5 is the same move: a structure borrowed from outside science to name something scientists already do.

### The reader's cognitive economy

Chapter 1 states the principle the rest of the book operates under — it is the author's job to make the reader's job easy — and chapters 7, 8 and 15 restate it at their own scales.

**Schema** is the mechanism, and it recurs in seven chapters. A schema is an established mental structure a reader already holds; writing that builds on one is absorbed cheaply, and writing that requires a reader to construct a new one is expensive. Chapter 3 introduces it for simple language, chapter 5 for openings, chapter 8 for the Methods section, chapters 14 and 15 for verbs and words, chapter 20 for lay audiences.

Chapter 11's **curse of knowledge** is the failure mode: an author cannot un-know the material, and so cannot directly perceive what a reader will find hard. The book's answer is not introspection but structural tests, which is why so many chapters end in checklists of diagnostic questions rather than in advice.

### Order of operations

The book treats revision as sequenced rather than iterative-in-general, and states the sequence twice. Chapter 1 establishes that the book is about rewriting rather than writing, and that first drafts do not matter because no one else sees them — attending to the book's principles while drafting risks never drafting at all.

Chapter 17 gives the order as **SCFL**: Structure, Clarity, Flow, Language. Get the story's structure right, make the ideas clear and concrete, fix the flow, then work the language. Chapter 19 reuses SCFL as its central argument for second-language writers — structure precedes language, so the hardest part of the work is the same regardless of the author's first language.

The claim underneath both is that fixing language first is wasted effort, because structural revision deletes the sentences that were polished.

### Every device has a legitimate use

The book declines to ban anything, and instead gives the cost and the case for each:

- **Passive voice** (ch. 14) drains energy, and is correct when the receiver of the action is the sentence's proper topic.
- **Nominalizations** (ch. 14) bury the action in a noun, and are correct when the process itself is the thing under discussion.
- **Jargon versus technical terms** (ch. 15) — the distinction belongs to the reader, not the word. A technical term carries meaning the audience holds; the same word is jargon before an audience that does not.
- **Formatting tricks** (ch. 16) are the one exception. Shrinking fonts and packing hypotheses into paragraphs is named as illegitimate rather than merely costly, on the grounds that reviewers notice.

Chapter 14's stated task is teaching a writer to notice whether a tool is being used deliberately or reached for out of habit, which is the form most of the book's rules take.

### The book enacts its own method

Chapter 21 reopens on the sentence chapter 1 opened on — "As a scientist, you are a professional writer" — which is chapter 4's spiral structure and chapter 9's resolution rule performed rather than described. Chapter 16 demonstrates the cost of formatting tricks with a deliberately blurred figure rather than by asserting it. Chapter 17 exists so that the method is shown operating on one manuscript rather than only stated across sixteen chapters.

The exercises and Appendix A extend this. Most chapters set revision exercises; Appendix A gives Schimel's own answers with reasoning, which makes the disagreement between a reader's revision and the author's into the instructional event.

## Scope and limitations

**What the book covers.** Craft, at every scale from the shape of a whole manuscript down to the choice of a verb, for two output types: the journal paper and the grant proposal. Chapters 19 and 20 extend the same method to writing in English as a second language and to public and policy audiences.

**What it explicitly declines to cover.** Chapter 1 draws the boundary itself. Other science-writing books, in Schimel's account, treat writing as something a scientist *does* — how to build figures and tables, how to handle bibliographies, how to navigate submission and peer review — and this book treats being a writer as something a scientist *is*. Manuscript mechanics are therefore out of scope by design, not by omission. The book also does not teach grammar or usage; chapter 19 states that correct English is a non-negotiable requirement and that responsibility for it rests on the author and coauthors, then declines to teach it.

**What the advice assumes about its reader.** An academic scientist writing for peer-reviewed journals and competing for grants, working within IMRaD, and publishing in English. The proposal chapters assume US funding norms: the hard case throughout is the National Science Foundation's fifteen-page Project Description limit, and the review dynamics described are panel review. Readers in other funding systems get the principles but not the specific constraints.

**What it assumes about its discipline.** The worked examples run heavily to ecology, soil and environmental science, and biology, which is Schimel's own field. Chapter 8's structural claims about Methods and Results assume experimental or observational work with data. The book states that the method generalizes; a reader in a proof-based or purely theoretical field is extrapolating.

**Its era.** Published 2012. Preprints, open peer review, data-availability statements, registered reports and the metrics debates that followed are absent. The H-factor is presented in chapter 1 as the measure of success without the critique that has since accumulated around it.

**The nature of its evidence.** The book's authority is craft experience rather than empirical study of reader behaviour. Its claims about what readers notice, remember, and skip are drawn from the author's editing practice and from other writers on writing — Zinsser, Williams, Lamott, the Heath brothers — rather than from reading research. The examples are real published passages chosen to illustrate the book's positions rather than drawn as a representative sample.

**Where the book overlaps itself.** Chapters 14 and 15 both treat verb choice and word origin; chapters 12, 13 and 15 all use the 2-3-1 rule; chapters 10 and 11 both apply OCAR below the section level. These are deliberate re-approaches from different angles rather than redundancy, and the chapter treatments above cross-reference them.

## Topic mapping

Sections touched, stated neutrally:

- Scientific writing craft — papers and grant proposals
- Narrative structure applied to technical writing (OCAR, LD, LDR, ABDCE, IMRaD, hourglass, spiral)
- Story selection and memorability (SUCCES, schema, abstraction)
- Document architecture and nested structure
- Paragraph construction
- Sentence construction, topic/stress placement, emphasis
- Cohesion and flow between sentences and paragraphs
- Verb choice, voice, nominalization
- Word choice, jargon, technical vocabulary, abbreviation
- Condensing to page limits
- Revision method and order of operations
- Handling study limitations and imperfect data
- Writing science in English as a second language
- Science communication to public and policy audiences
- Citation, impact, and research-career metrics
